# Study guide anecdote ledger

This ledger records every sentence changed in the 62 study guides (`client/public/studyguides/*.json`) to remove events, scenes, conversations, feelings and details from James Bell's life or ministry that go beyond the verified facts: he came to faith from atheism, he was raised without a father, he has five sons, he is lead pastor of First Baptist Church of Fenton, Michigan, he founded the Pastors Connection Network, and he is an author.

How to read it. Each row gives the guide, the JSON field path, the original sentence or sentences verbatim, what was done, and the new text. "Generalized" means the teaching point was kept and the story or first-person claim was turned into a common pattern, a type figure ("Start with a familiar figure"), or a collective "we". "Cut" means the added biography was removed and the sentence repaired, or the sentence was removed outright. To restore a story that is true, replace the new text with James's own wording in the same field. Where a field's summary changed, the card blurb in `client/public/studyguides/index.json` was regenerated from it with `scripts/build-studyguides-index.mjs`.

Two appendices follow the change tables: lines deliberately kept, with the reason, and changed sentences that also appear word for word in other files in the repo.

Totals: 1163 changes across 62 guides (829 generalized, 334 cut).

### after-christendom (18)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| after-christendom | `summary` | He pastored through the years when the pews thinned, watched his own assumptions about Christian America fall apart, and found that he had built more of his ministry on cultural Christianity than on Christ than he wanted to admit. | generalized | The church, its pastors included, has built more on cultural Christianity than on Christ than it wants to admit, and the writer does not exempt himself. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after a service that he wanted his country back, the one where stores closed on Sunday and prayer opened the school day and being a decent person and being a Christian were assumed to be the same thing. Bell understood the grief. He also had to tell him a hard thing, that the country the man was mourning had taught a great many people they were Christians who had never once met Christ. | generalized | Many Christians say they want their country back, the one where stores closed on Sunday and prayer opened the school day and being a decent person and being a Christian were assumed to be the same thing. The grief is understandable. But a hard thing has to be said to it, that the country being mourned had taught a great many people they were Christians who had never once met Christ. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].discussion[2].q` | The man wanted his country back, and Bell told him that country had convinced many people they were Christians who had never met Christ. | generalized | The country so many want back had convinced a great many people they were Christians who had never met Christ. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he built more of his ministry on cultural Christianity than on Christ than he wanted to admit. | generalized | Many pastors and churches have built more on cultural Christianity than on Christ than they would like to admit. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a service who wanted his country back. He meant the country where the stores closed on Sunday, where the school day opened with a prayer, where being a decent person and being a Christian were assumed to be roughly the same thing, where you did not have to explain yourself for believing. | generalized | Start with a longing many Christians voice, the wish to have their country back. They mean the country where the stores closed on Sunday, where the school day opened with a prayer, where being a decent person and being a Christian were assumed to be roughly the same thing, where you did not have to explain yourself for believing. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell understood the grief completely, and he felt it himself. He also had to tell the man something hard. The country he was mourning had taught a great many people they were Christians who had never once met Christ. | generalized | The grief is understandable, and most of us have felt some of it. But something hard has to be said to it. The country being mourned had taught a great many people they were Christians who had never once met Christ. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].teaching` | Christendom was not the gospel, which destroys the first answer, because the thing the man wanted back was never the kingdom of God. | generalized | Christendom was not the gospel, which destroys the first answer, because the thing so many want back was never the kingdom of God. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because I did not see clearly for a long time. I built more of my ministry on cultural Christianity than on Christ than I want to admit. I counted the crowds that came because it was respectable to come, and called that fruit. I assumed an America that would always be vaguely on our side, and built as if that assumption were the gospel. The end of Christendom found me out, and it is finding the whole church out, which is exactly the mercy in it. | generalized | And here we have to stand inside it, because many of us did not see clearly for a long time. We built more of our churches on cultural Christianity than on Christ than we want to admit. We counted the crowds that came because it was respectable to come, and called that fruit. We assumed an America that would always be vaguely on our side, and built as if that assumption were the gospel. The end of Christendom is finding the whole church out, which is exactly the mercy in it. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he spent years preaching as if the exile were temporary, waiting for the culture to come back rather than teaching his people to live well where God had actually placed them. | generalized | Many churches have spent years preaching as if the exile were temporary, waiting for the culture to come back rather than teaching their people to live well where God had actually placed them. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess that I missed this for years. I preached as if the exile were a brief inconvenience, as if the right strategy or the right election or the right cultural push would bring the old world back, and so I never taught my people to actually live where God had put them. I had them waiting at the window for a Jerusalem that was not coming, when God was saying, plant a garden, here, in Babylon, because I am here too. | generalized | And many of us have missed this for years. We preached and prayed as if the exile were a brief inconvenience, as if the right strategy or the right election or the right cultural push would bring the old world back, and so we never learned to actually live where God had put us. We kept our churches waiting at the window for a Jerusalem that was not coming, when God was saying, plant a garden, here, in Babylon, because I am here too. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he reached for cultural and political power because the slow work of being a community that simply lives the gospel felt too weak and too slow to matter. | generalized | It is tempting to reach for cultural and political power because the slow work of being a community that simply lives the gospel feels too weak and too slow to matter. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to confess that I did not believe this for a long time, not really. The slow work of being a community that simply lives the gospel felt too weak and too slow to matter, so I reached for the faster lever. I put my hope in the vote, in the cultural fight, in the influence I could grab, because being the church felt like bringing a candle to a war. | generalized | And many of us have not believed this, not really. The slow work of being a community that simply lives the gospel feels too weak and too slow to matter, so we reach for the faster lever. We put our hope in the vote, in the cultural fight, in the influence we can grab, because being the church feels like bringing a candle to a war. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he spent years measuring his ministry by size and feeling that a smaller, more distinct church was a defeat. | generalized | Many pastors and congregations measure ministry by size and feel that a smaller, more distinct church is a defeat. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here I have to confess my own captivity, because I measured everything by size. For years a smaller, more distinct, more costly church felt to me like a defeat, like something had gone wrong, like faithfulness should fill the building. I had absorbed, without noticing, the conviction that bigger was the same as better and that a shrinking church was a failing one, and so I poured my energy into growth and called the hunger for size a hunger for impact. The end of Christendom is curing me of that, slowly and against my will. | generalized | And here we have to confess our own captivity, because the church has measured everything by size. A smaller, more distinct, more costly church can feel like a defeat, like something has gone wrong, like faithfulness should fill the building. Many of us absorbed, without noticing, the conviction that bigger was the same as better and that a shrinking church was a failing one, and so we poured our energy into growth and called the hunger for size a hunger for impact. The end of Christendom is curing us of that, slowly and against our will. |
| after-christendom | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to end where the honest preacher always has to end, inside the indictment. I have grieved this loss facing the wrong way. I have spent more prayer wanting the old world back than wanting the kingdom to come, and called the nostalgia faithfulness. I have stood at the window like the exiles waiting for a Jerusalem that was not coming, when the word the whole time was, turn around, I am ahead of you, build here, I am bringing something you cannot yet see. | generalized | And we have to end where the honest preacher always has to end, inside the indictment. Many of us have grieved this loss facing the wrong way. We have spent more prayer wanting the old world back than wanting the kingdom to come, and called the nostalgia faithfulness. We have stood at the window like the exiles waiting for a Jerusalem that was not coming, when the word the whole time was, turn around, I am ahead of you, build here, I am bringing something you cannot yet see. |
| after-christendom | `leaderPrimer` | He may say it in those words, as a man once said to the writer of this study after a service, or he may simply carry it, the ache for stores closed on Sunday, prayer before kickoff, a world where the culture did half the church's work for it. | cut | He may say it in those words, or he may simply carry it, the ache for stores closed on Sunday, prayer before kickoff, a world where the culture did half the church's work for it. |
| after-christendom | `leaderPrimer` | The writer holds them from inside a small Michigan pastorate, where the end of the arrangement is not a thesis but the view from the pulpit, and he still argues that the church has been here before and done some of its best work here. | cut | The writer holds them as a working pastor, and he argues that the church has been here before and done some of its best work here. |
| after-christendom | `faq[10].a` | He pastored through the years the pews thinned. He watched his assumptions about a Christian America fall apart. He admits he built more of his ministry on cultural Christianity than on Christ, counted respectable crowds as fruit, reached for power because being a small distinct church felt like defeat, and prayed more for the old world to return than for the kingdom to come. He writes from inside the loss and the nostalgia, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot lead a room through a grief you have not felt or out of a nostalgia you are secretly nursing. | generalized | The study names how the church, its pastors included, built more on cultural Christianity than on Christ, counted respectable crowds as fruit, reached for power because being a small distinct church felt like defeat, and prayed more for the old world to return than for the kingdom to come. Bell writes from inside that indictment, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot lead a room through a grief you have not felt or out of a nostalgia you are secretly nursing. |

| aging-and-legacy | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, where death was simply the end and the honesty of that was its own kind of clean. He was raised without a father, which taught him to count his own days early. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, where death was simply the end, and he was raised without a father. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell keeps a photograph of a father he never really knew, and for most of his life he could not have told you the man's exact age at his death, because a boy raised without a father does not count that way. He counted his own days instead, from the beginning, quietly certain his time ran shorter than other men assumed theirs to be. | generalized | Most of us do not count our days until something forces the arithmetic on us. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has lived stretches of his life as if the calendar were endless, postponing the things that matter most. | generalized | Most of us live stretches of our lives as if the calendar were endless, postponing the things that matter most. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell keeps a photograph of a father he never really knew, and for most of his life he could not have told you the man's exact age when he died, because a boy raised without a father does not count time that way. | aging-and-legacy | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I have lived long stretches of my own life on the fiction of unlimited time, postponing the very things a numbered life would never postpone, the presence with my sons, the reconciliation I kept meaning to get to, the confession I kept deferring, the God I kept planning to seek more seriously once things settled down. The calendar felt infinite, so nothing was urgent, and a life in which nothing is urgent is a life quietly wasted. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us live long stretches of our lives on the fiction of unlimited time, postponing the very things a numbered life would never postpone, the presence with our children, the reconciliation we keep meaning to get to, the confession we keep deferring, the God we keep planning to seek more seriously once things settle down. The calendar feels infinite, so nothing is urgent, and a life in which nothing is urgent is a life quietly wasted. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I feel the fear too, the fear of becoming a burden, of outliving my usefulness, of being the one talked over and managed and hidden. The gospel does not tell me that fear is silly. It tells me it is answered, by a God who carries what the world discards, and who calls the gray hair I am afraid of a crown. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us feel the fear too, the fear of becoming a burden, of outliving our usefulness, of being the one talked over and managed and hidden. The gospel does not tell us that fear is silly. It tells us it is answered, by a God who carries what the world discards, and who calls the gray hair we are afraid of a crown. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[2].teaching` | Now the real question is whether that enemy has the last word, and here I have to take the strong case of the honest unbeliever, because it deserves better than the dismissal it usually gets, and because I once made it myself. | cut | Now the real question is whether that enemy has the last word, and here I have to take the strong case of the honest unbeliever, because it deserves better than the dismissal it usually gets, and because I came to faith from atheism. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have served it too, more than I knew, my own grasping and avoiding traceable, when I finally traced them, back to a fear of the end I had never admitted I carried. | generalized | And most of us serve it more than we know, our grasping and avoiding traceable, when we finally trace them, back to a fear of the end we have never admitted we carry. |
| aging-and-legacy | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here is the hard part, the part that separates raising a legacy from building one, and I feel the difficulty in myself as a father of five sons. | generalized | And here is the hard part, the part that separates raising a legacy from building one, and every parent feels the difficulty of it. |
| aging-and-legacy | `leaderPrimer` | Bell leads this from inside it, a man who came to faith from atheism where death was simply the end, who was raised without a father and learned to count his days young, who has five sons who will one day bury him. | cut | Bell leads this from inside it, a man who came to faith from atheism where death was simply the end, who was raised without a father, who has five sons who will one day bury him. |

### alone-in-a-crowded-church (16)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |

| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `summary` | He has pastored a church of hundreds and felt unknown inside his own congregation. He has asked how are you and not waited for the answer. He has let a member drift out the back door and told himself they were busy. He writes from inside the loneliness, as one of the people who helped build the room where it grows. | generalized | Pastors can lead hundreds and still feel unknown inside their own congregations, ask how are you without waiting for the answer, and let a member drift out the back door while telling themselves the person was busy. He writes from inside that indictment, as one of the church's leaders, not above them. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after twelve years of faithful attendance that if he stopped coming, it might be three weeks before anyone noticed, and he was not bitter when he said it, only tired. He was not doing anything wrong. He had done everything the church asked, joined and served and kept coming, and he was still a stranger in a building full of familiar faces. | generalized | Someone can attend faithfully for a decade and suspect that if he stopped coming, it might be weeks before anyone noticed, and say so without bitterness, only tired. He is not doing anything wrong. He has done everything the church asked, joined and served and kept coming, and he is still a stranger in a building full of familiar faces. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has pastored hundreds and still felt unknown, and has asked how are you without waiting for the answer. | generalized | Most of us, pastors included, have asked how are you without waiting for the answer. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[0].objections[0].response` | The man who said it would be three weeks before anyone noticed had joined and served and kept coming. He used the means, and was still a stranger. | generalized | The faithful attender who suspects it would be weeks before anyone noticed has joined and served and kept coming. He used the means, and is still a stranger. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a service. Twelve years he had come, faithfully, every week, and he told Bell, without bitterness, that if he simply stopped coming it would probably be three weeks before anyone noticed he was gone. He was not angry. He was tired. And he was not doing anything wrong. He had done everything the church ever asked of him. He had joined. He had served. He had come in the rain. And he was still, after twelve years, a stranger among familiar faces. | generalized | Start with a person many churches have without knowing it. He has come faithfully, every week, for a decade or more, and he suspects, without bitterness, that if he simply stopped coming it would be weeks before anyone noticed he was gone. He is not angry. He is tired. And he is not doing anything wrong. He has done everything the church ever asked of him. He has joined. He has served. He has come in the rain. And he is still, after all those years, a stranger among familiar faces. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, in both directions, because I have been both the unknown and the one who kept others unknown. I have pastored a church of hundreds and driven home from my own building feeling like a stranger in it. And I have stood at the door and asked a hundred people how they were and not once stopped long enough to want the real answer. The how are you that does not wait is its own small cruelty, and I have practiced it for years. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, in both directions, because most of us have been both the unknown and the one who kept others unknown. Pastors can lead a church of hundreds and drive home from their own buildings feeling like strangers in them. And most of us have stood at a door and asked a hundred people how they were and not once stopped long enough to want the real answer. The how are you that does not wait is its own small cruelty, and we practice it for years. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits his own church has a fellowship hall and almost no fellowship, a building for the thing it does not actually do. | generalized | Many churches have a fellowship hall and almost no fellowship, a building for the thing they do not actually do. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | And here I have to confess the gap in my own house, because my church has a fellowship hall and almost no fellowship. We built a room and named it for a thing we do not actually do in it. We have the gathering. We built a room and named it for a thing we do not actually do in it. We have the gathering. We have lost the shared life, and we have let ourselves lose it, because the gathering is cheap and the life is expensive. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits that as a pastor he has been surrounded by people and starved of friends, and has let friendships lapse because they could not be scheduled. | generalized | It is possible to be surrounded by people and starved of friends, letting friendships lapse because they could not be scheduled. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this one with particular shame, because I am a pastor, which means I am surrounded by people every single day and have been starved for actual friends for years. I know hundreds of people. I am known by almost none of them, and a good deal of that is my own doing, because I have let friendship after friendship lapse, not by any decision, never by a decision, but by the slow neglect of the thing that was never urgent enough to make the calendar. | generalized | And pastors have to stand inside this one with particular shame, because a pastor is surrounded by people every single day and can be starved for actual friends for years. It is possible to know hundreds of people and be known by almost none of them, and a good deal of that is our own doing, because we let friendship after friendship lapse, not by any decision, never by a decision, but by the slow neglect of the thing that was never urgent enough to make the calendar. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has let his congregation believe that care is the staff's department, building a machine that let everyone else off the hook. | generalized | Many pastors have let their congregations believe that care is the staff's department, building a machine that lets everyone else off the hook. |
| alone-in-a-crowded-church | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here I have to confess my own part as a pastor, because I have helped build exactly this machine. ### anger (20)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| anger | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, which means he has been angry in every direction a man can be, at a God he did not believe in, at a father who was not there, at boys who tested him on nights he had nothing left. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the man who called his temper honesty, who nursed a grievance until it hardened into a wall, who let a slow contempt stand in for the grief he would not feel. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he does not point from a safe distance. Most of us have been the one who called a temper honesty, who nursed a grievance until it hardened into a wall, who let a slow contempt stand in for a grief we would not feel. |
| anger | `sessions[0].summary` | A woman told Bell, with real shame, that she had prayed for years to stop being an angry person and God had never answered, as though her anger were a disease he kept declining to cure. He asked her a question she did not expect. He asked her what she was angry about. And the story came out slowly, a marriage in which she had been talked over and made small and quietly diminished for twenty years, and by the end of it he told her the truth, which was not the truth she had come expecting. He told her that her anger was not the sin she had been repenting of all this time. It was the last part of her that still insisted she was worth more than she had been treated, and God had not answered her prayer to remove it because he had no intention of removing it, only of teaching her what it was for. | generalized | Many people carry a shame they have carried for years, and they say it almost as a confession, that they have prayed for a long time to stop being angry and God has simply never answered, as though their anger were a disease he kept declining to cure. | anger | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell says he chose anger over grief because anger feels like power and grief feels like weakness, and called it strength. | generalized | Bell says we choose anger over grief because anger feels like power and grief feels like weakness, and call it strength. |
| anger | `sessions[2].teaching` | Grant it, because it is largely true, and I have watched the excavation of a wound become a permanent excuse for the harm the anger keeps doing. | generalized | Grant it, because it is largely true, and the excavation of a wound can become a permanent excuse for the harm the anger keeps doing. |
| anger | `sessions[2].teaching` | And now I have to go where I would rather not, because I cannot teach this from the outside. Under my own worst anger, when I have finally had the courage to dig, I do not find a principled man defending a righteous cause. I find a boy without a father. I find the fear of being left again, the certainty that if I am not in control I will be abandoned, and I find a grief I never once let myself feel, for the man who was not there and the childhood that shaped itself around his absence. And I chose the anger over the grief every time, for years, and I will tell you exactly why. Because anger feels like power and grief feels like weakness. Anger stands up and grief lies down. Anger makes me feel strong and grief makes me feel exposed, like the abandoned boy again, and I would do almost anything not to feel that. So I picked up the fire instead of letting the tears come, over and over, and I called it strength. I called my temper honesty and my hardness resolve and my rage conviction. It was none of those things. It was a bandage slapped over a wound I was far too proud to let anyone see, least of all myself, and the bandage kept slipping, and the wound kept bleeding, and the anger kept coming back because I never once let anyone near the thing underneath it. | generalized | And now we have to go where we would rather not, because none of us can learn this from the outside. Under our worst anger, when we finally have the courage to dig, most of us do not find a principled person defending a righteous cause. We find an old wound. We find the fear of being left again, the certainty that if we are not in control we will be abandoned, and we find a grief we never once let ourselves feel, for someone who was not there or something that was taken. And we choose the anger over the grief every time, for years, and it is not hard to say why. Because anger feels like power and grief feels like weakness. Anger stands up and grief lies down. Anger makes us feel strong and grief makes us feel exposed, like a child again, and we would do almost anything not to feel that. So we pick up the fire instead of letting the tears come, over and over, and we call it strength. We call our temper honesty and our hardness resolve and our rage conviction. It is none of those things. It is a bandage slapped over a wound we are far too proud to let anyone see, least of all ourselves, and the bandage keeps slipping, and the wound keeps bleeding, and the anger keeps coming back because we never once let anyone near the thing underneath it. |
| anger | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, angry at a God he did not believe in, was raised without a father and carries the grief of it still, and is raising five sons who have seen him get it wrong. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he writes as one still learning, not as a master of the subject. |
| anger | `faq[9].a` | Your own struggle, honestly named, is the very thing that lets you stand inside the study alongside everyone else rather than above them, because the writer himself leads it from inside, a man who came to faith angry and is still learning to read his own alarm. | cut | Your own struggle, honestly named, is the very thing that lets you stand inside the study alongside everyone else rather than above them, because the writer himself leads it from inside, a man who came to faith from atheism and is still learning to read his own alarm. |

| attention | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has looked up from his phone to find one of his sons waiting, and felt the small betrayal of it. | generalized | Many parents have looked up from a phone to find a child waiting, and felt the small betrayal of it. |
| attention | `sessions[0].discussion[4].behind` | The writer goes first with his own children, and so should the leader. | cut | The leader should go first. |
| attention | `sessions[0].teaching` | So let me stop pointing and step inside the thing I am describing, because I will not preach this from a height I do not occupy. I write this with my own phone face-down on the desk, having reached for it twice since I began this paragraph, having lost my own hours to it that I will not get back. I have read to a child with one eye on a screen. Worse than that, I have looked up from the phone to find one of my sons standing there, waiting, who had been waiting, and I do not know for how long, because the device had taken the part of me that would have noticed. That is the small betrayal, and I have committed it, and calling it small is part of how I have let myself keep committing it. | generalized | So let me stop pointing and step inside the thing I am describing, because none of us can preach this from a height we do not occupy. Most of us have lost hours to the phone that we will not get back. Many of us have read to a child with one eye on a screen. Worse than that, many parents have looked up from the phone to find a child standing there, waiting, who had been waiting, and they do not know for how long, because the device had taken the part of them that would have noticed. That is the small betrayal, and most of us have committed it, and calling it small is part of how we let ourselves keep committing it. |
| attention | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits the research convicts him as much as it diagnoses his children, that he handed his own sons devices and modeled the very distraction he worries about in them. | generalized | The research convicts parents as much as it diagnoses their children, because many of us handed our kids devices and modeled the very distraction we worry about in them. |
| attention | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to say plainly that I am not standing over this research as a diagnostician of other people's children. It convicts me. I handed my own sons devices, some of them too early, with a vague unease I overrode because everyone else was doing it and because the device was convenient and because saying no was hard. And worse than what I handed them is what I modeled, because a child learns presence or its absence from watching the adult, and my sons have watched their father reach for his phone at the dinner table, in the car, in the middle of their sentences, and whatever I have told them about attention, that is the lesson they actually received. So the generation I am worried about is, in part, a generation I helped form, by the device I gave and the distraction I demonstrated, and I cannot preach this session from outside it. | generalized | And we have to say plainly that we are not standing over this research as diagnosticians of other people's children. So the generation we are worried about is, in part, a generation we helped form, by the device we gave and the distraction we demonstrated, and none of us can preach this session from outside it. |

I can write a session on silence and still be unable to sit in it for ten minutes without my hand drifting toward input. I value presence and have built a life arranged, in a hundred small ways, to avoid ever having to be present, to keep the silence filled and the stillness postponed and the attention always partly elsewhere. | generalized | And none of us can pretend we have recovered what this session describes. | attention | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he can read theology for hours but cannot sit with five verses of Scripture without his mind reaching for his phone, the slow word the hardest of all to receive. | generalized | Many of us can read theology for hours but cannot sit with five verses of Scripture without the mind reaching for a phone, the slow word the hardest of all to receive. |
| attention | `sessions[3].discussion[4].behind` | The writer names that even a trained reader finds the slow word hardest. | generalized | Even a practiced reader can find the slow word hardest. |
| attention | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to step inside this before I say the verdict, because the irony of my own life convicts me. I can read theology for hours; I can stay with a dense argument, follow a hard book, attend to ideas with real endurance. And yet I can sit down with five verses of Scripture, the slow word, the thing I claim to believe is more worth my attention than anything else I will read all day, and within a minute my mind is reaching for my phone, restless, impatient, unable to stay with the very word I say I treasure most. The slow word is the hardest of all for me to receive, and the difficulty is not in the word. It is in me, in an attention so thoroughly trained by the fast world that the deepest things have become the hardest to attend to, which is a sentence I should sit with longer than I want to. | generalized | And we have to step inside this before the verdict, because the irony convicts many of us. We can read for hours, even theology; we can stay with a dense argument, follow a hard book, attend to ideas with real endurance. And yet we sit down with five verses of Scripture, the slow word, the thing we claim to believe is more worth our attention than anything else we will read all day, and within a minute the mind is reaching for the phone, restless, impatient, unable to stay with the very word we say we treasure most. | attention | `sessions[4].teaching` | I am writing a session about reordering a life around presence with my own phone within arm's reach of this page, picked up more times than I will count while writing it. I have not solved this. I am not the man reporting back from the far side of the problem with the map for everyone still lost in it. I am in it, and I will be in it for the rest of my life, taking my own counsel slowly and imperfectly, building banks and breaching them and rebuilding them, putting the device beneath the people I love and finding it climbing back above them again, a thousand times, for years. So I cannot offer you a finish line, because I have not crossed one. | generalized | I have not solved this. | baptism-and-communion | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he spent years more comfortable with a well-made argument than with a wet head or a shared cup, because the argument he controlled and the sign he could only receive. | generalized | Many of us are more comfortable with a well-made argument than with a wet head or a shared cup, because the argument we control and the sign we can only receive. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[0].teaching` | A man sat in Bell's study who had followed Jesus for a decade and had never once been baptized, and when Bell asked him why, the man said, with no defensiveness at all, that he did not really see the point of getting wet. His faith, he explained, was inward. It was a matter of the heart and the mind, of what he believed and how he lived, and the water and the bread struck him as leftover religion, ritual for people who needed props to feel something. Bell says he did not argue, because he understood the instinct completely. He came to faith from atheism, and he arrived carrying the exact conviction the whole modern world carries in its bones, that the real world is the world you can weigh and measure, and that if there is a spiritual world at all it is invisible and private, happening somewhere behind the eyes where no one else can check. | generalized | Plenty of people follow Jesus for years and never once get baptized, and when asked why, many say, with no defensiveness at all, that they do not really see the point of getting wet. Their faith, they explain, is inward. It is a matter of the heart and the mind, of what they believe and how they live, and the water and the bread strike them as leftover religion, ritual for people who need props to feel something. The instinct deserves understanding before argument. It is the exact conviction the whole modern world carries in its bones, that the real world is the world you can weigh and measure, and that if there is a spiritual world at all it is invisible and private, happening somewhere behind the eyes where no one else can check. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[0].teaching` | I am a Baptist pastor, and I find myself cautious in my words, closer to ordinance, and large in my wonder, closer to sacrament, unwilling to call the sign an empty picture and equally unwilling to make it magic. And I confess I spent years treating these signs as the boring part of the service, more comfortable in a pulpit with a well-made argument than with a wet head or a shared cup, because the argument I controlled and the sign I could only receive. | generalized | I find myself cautious in my words, closer to ordinance, and large in my wonder, closer to sacrament, unwilling to call the sign an empty picture and equally unwilling to make it magic. And many of us have treated these signs as the boring part of the service, more comfortable with a well-made argument than with a wet head or a shared cup, because the argument we control and the sign we can only receive. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell once baptized a grandmother by her own long-delayed profession and, on the same Sunday, watched an infant baptized in the arms of a visiting father from another tradition, and someone asked him afterward, with real confusion, which one was the real baptism. | generalized | Christians from different traditions often ask, with real confusion, which baptism is the real one, the believer's own profession or the infant carried to the font. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].summary` | He names where he stands, as a Baptist, and names the strongest objection he has not fully answered, the covenant continuity the paedobaptist presses. | generalized | He names where he stands, with believer's baptism, and names the strongest objection he has not fully answered, the covenant continuity the paedobaptist presses. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].teaching` | Bell once baptized a grandmother on a Sunday morning by her own long-delayed profession of faith, water running off a face that had waited seventy years for it, and on that same Sunday a visiting father from another tradition brought his infant forward and that child was baptized too, in his arms, before the baby could believe or even understand. Afterward someone came to Bell, genuinely confused, and asked which one had been the real baptism. | generalized | Christians from different traditions often ask, with real confusion, which baptism is the real one, the grandmother who finally professes faith after a lifetime of waiting or the infant carried to the font in a parent's arms before the child can believe or even understand. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].teaching` | Now I will do what this study asks and name where I stand. I am a Baptist. I hold believer's baptism, persuaded by the sheer weight of the New Testament pattern and by baptism as the answer of a professing conscience. | cut | Now I will do what this study asks and name where I stand. I hold believer's baptism, persuaded by the sheer weight of the New Testament pattern and by baptism as the answer of a professing conscience. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have stood at the Lord's table beside paedobaptists I fully expect to spend eternity with, and I will not let a disagreement about the timing of the water become a wall Christ never built. | generalized | Believers on both sides of this question will spend eternity together, and I will not let a disagreement about the timing of the water become a wall Christ never built. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess I have not always held it that way. I have been sharper about this than love allowed, have enjoyed winning the argument more than I enjoyed the brother across from me, have treated a matter Scripture leaves genuinely contested as though it were the gospel itself. | generalized | And many of us have not always held it that way. We have been sharper about this than love allowed, have enjoyed winning the argument more than we enjoyed the brother across from us, have treated a matter Scripture leaves genuinely contested as though it were the gospel itself. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[2].teaching` | I know the pull of it. There is an old version of me, defined by the fatherless boy I was, by the anger I inherited and the fears I have never fully outrun, and on my worst days that old self feels more real than the new one, more solid, more like the truth about me than anything said over water decades ago. That is the drift, and it is relentless, back toward the label, back toward the sin, back toward the failure I keep wanting to call the final word. | generalized | Most of us know the pull of it. There is an old version of each of us, defined by old wounds and old fears we have never fully outrun, and on our worst days that old self feels more real than the new one, more solid, more like the truth about us than anything said over water. That is the drift, and it is relentless, back toward the label, back toward the sin, back toward the failure we keep wanting to call the final word. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[3].summary` | Bell grants the pastoral and biblical instinct guarding each, then names where he lands, as a Baptist who cannot rest in bare memory, closer to Calvin, confessing a real spiritual presence at a table where Christ actually meets and feeds his people, and he names it a second-order matter. | cut | Bell grants the pastoral and biblical instinct guarding each, then names where he lands, unable to rest in bare memory, closer to Calvin, confessing a real spiritual presence at a table where Christ actually meets and feeds his people, and he names it a second-order matter. |

| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[3].teaching` | I am a Baptist, and I might be expected to land on bare memory, and I cannot. | generalized | I hold believer's baptism, and a pastor who does might be expected to land on bare memory, and I cannot. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[3].teaching` | The tragedy is that we took a meal of grace and turned it into a border checkpoint, a place to decide who is in and who is out, and I have done my own smaller version of this, guarding the table more fiercely than I have loved the ones on the other side of it. | generalized | The tragedy is that we took a meal of grace and turned it into a border checkpoint, a place to decide who is in and who is out, and most of us have done our own smaller version of this, guarding the table more fiercely than we have loved the ones on the other side of it. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[4].teaching` | For most of my life I heard discern the body as a command to reverence the bread, to recognize that the elements are holy and to take them with due gravity. | generalized | Many of us have heard discern the body as a command to reverence the bread, to recognize that the elements are holy and to take them with due gravity. |
| baptism-and-communion | `sessions[4].teaching` | I have passed the bread to a person I was cold toward and called it communion. | baptism-and-communion | `faq[0].a` | The writer is a Baptist and says so plainly, and he still works to make the infant-baptism case and the range of communion views as strong as their own advocates would want them made. | cut | The writer says plainly where he lands, and he still works to make the infant-baptism case and the range of communion views as strong as their own advocates would want them made. |
| baptism-and-communion | `faq[3].a` | Then the writer names where he lands, as a Baptist who cannot rest in bare memory, closer to Calvin, confessing a real spiritual presence at a table where the living Christ genuinely meets and feeds his people by his Spirit. | cut | Then the writer names where he lands, unable to rest in bare memory, closer to Calvin, confessing a real spiritual presence at a table where the living Christ genuinely meets and feeds his people by his Spirit. |
| baptism-and-communion | `faq[4].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism carrying exactly that instinct, and he does not pretend it away. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he does not pretend that instinct away. |

| baptism-and-communion | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer is a Baptist and says so plainly, and he still works hard to state every other side fairly, and so will we, whatever tradition each of us brings. | cut | Third, the writer says plainly where he lands, and he still works hard to state every other side fairly, and so will we, whatever tradition each of us brings. |

| baptism-and-communion | `promoKit.bulletinBlurb` | It states every contested position at its strongest before any verdict, holds the difference between what the faith stands or falls on and what faithful churches disagree on, and is written by a Baptist pastor who names where he lands and still honors the brother who lands elsewhere. | cut | It states every contested position at its strongest before any verdict, holds the difference between what the faith stands or falls on and what faithful churches disagree on, and is written by a pastor who names where he lands and still honors the brother who lands elsewhere. |

### believe (16)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| believe | `summary` | He was raised without a father, which shapes how he hears the word Father applied to God. He still has mornings the doubt comes back. He writes from inside the questions, not from a safe distance above them. | cut | He was raised without a father, and the study does not pretend the word Father, applied to God, is easy for everyone. He writes from inside the questions, not from a safe distance above them. |
| believe | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after a talk that he wanted to believe but could not make himself, that the whole thing felt like wishing on a star, and he wanted to know how an honest person starts. He starts where everyone actually starts, with the question itself. | generalized | Many people want to believe but cannot make themselves, because the whole thing feels like wishing on a star, and they want to know how an honest person starts. They start where everyone actually starts, with the question itself. |
| believe | `sessions[0].objections[1].response` | Bell did exactly that, from the other side, and changed his mind. | cut | Bell began on the other side, in atheism, and changed his mind. |
| believe | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a talk. He said he wanted to believe but could not make himself, that the whole enterprise felt like wishing on a star, and he wanted to know how an honest person even begins. | generalized | Start with a question many seekers ask. They want to believe but cannot make themselves, the whole enterprise feels like wishing on a star, and they want to know how an honest person even begins. |
| believe | `sessions[0].teaching` | I have sat on the other side of this table and meant my objections, not as poses but as the honest convictions of a man who thought belief was for people who could not face the dark. I was also raised without a father, which means the word Father, when the Bible applies it to God, has never been an easy or sentimental word for me, and I will not pretend it is for everyone in this room. | cut | I have sat on the other side of this table and meant my objections. I was also raised without a father, and I will not pretend the word Father, when the Bible applies it to God, is an easy or sentimental word for everyone in this room. |
| believe | `sessions[1].teaching` | And here I have to implicate myself, because I spent years preferring the admired Jesus to the real one. | generalized | And here we have to implicate ourselves, because most of us prefer the admired Jesus to the real one. |
| believe | `sessions[1].teaching` | What I did not want, what I think most of us do not want, is the thing he actually asks, which is the one thing no mere teacher has the right to ask. | generalized | What most of us do not want is the thing he actually asks, which is the one thing no mere teacher has the right to ask. |
| believe | `sessions[1].teaching` | The question is the one his own words force, the one I avoided for years. | generalized | The question is the one his own words force, the one so many of us avoid for years. |
| believe | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits the doubt still visits him on some mornings, that he has stood at the edge of the same question and felt the floor move. | generalized | Doubt still visits long-time believers on some mornings, and many have stood at the edge of the same question and felt the floor move. |
| believe | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I will not stand over this as a man for whom it is all settled and easy, because it is not, even now. The doubt still visits me on certain gray mornings, when the floor seems to move and the whole thing feels like a story we told ourselves against the dark. I came to this from unbelief, and unbelief knows the way back to my door. So I am not asking you to feel a certainty I do not always feel. I am asking you to do what I had to do, to stop treating the resurrection as a mood to be summoned or dismissed and start treating it as a claim to be weighed, the most consequential claim ever made, offered not as a leap into the dark but as the best account of why that tomb was empty and those frightened men became people who would rather die than say it never happened. | generalized | And no one should stand over this as if it were all settled and easy. Doubt visits believers on certain gray mornings, when the floor seems to move and the whole thing feels like a story we told ourselves against the dark. I came to this from unbelief, and I am not asking you to feel a certainty no honest person feels every day. I am asking you to stop treating the resurrection as a mood to be summoned or dismissed and start treating it as a claim to be weighed, the most consequential claim ever made, offered not as a leap into the dark but as the best account of why that tomb was empty and those frightened men became people who would rather die than say it never happened. |
| believe | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to tell you that I know this from the inside, not as a man who has always believed easily, but as one who came to belief from the far side of unbelief and still feels the pull of the old doubts on certain mornings. I used to think the strongest faith was the one that had never been questioned, and I had it exactly backwards. | generalized | And I have to tell you that I know this from the inside, not as a man who has always believed easily, but as one who came to belief from the far side of unbelief. Many of us think the strongest faith is the one that has never been questioned, and we have it exactly backwards. |
| believe | `sessions[4].teaching` | I came to him myself from the far side of unbelief, and I will not pretend the following has been easy or that the doubts never returned, because both would be lies, and this study has tried not to lie to you. But I will tell you that the trade was the best one I ever made, that I lost a life I was clutching and found one I could not have built, and that the One who asked for everything turned out to be the only master who first gave everything for me. | generalized | I came to him myself from the far side of unbelief, and no honest follower will pretend the following is always easy or that the doubts never return, because both would be lies, and this study has tried not to lie to you. But the witness of the church across two thousand years is that the trade is the best one a person can make, that those who lose the life they were clutching find one they could not have built, and that the One who asks for everything is the only master who first gave everything for us. |
| believe | `leaderPrimer` | Someone else thinks faith means making yourself believe a thing you suspect is false, and has quietly concluded they cannot do it, like the man who told the writer of this study that the whole business felt like wishing on a star. | cut | Someone else thinks faith means making yourself believe a thing you suspect is false, and has quietly concluded they cannot do it. |
| believe | `leaderPrimer` | The writer came to all of this as an outsider, an atheist who had to be persuaded the subject was even discussable, and the study is built the way he needed it built, evidence before invitation, ground cleared before anyone is asked to build on it. | cut | The writer came to all of this from atheism, and the study is built for the outsider, evidence before invitation, ground cleared before anyone is asked to build on it. |
| believe | `faq[9].a` | He was raised without a father, which shapes how he hears the word Father applied to God and which he will not pretend is easy for everyone. He still feels the pull of the old doubts on certain mornings. | cut | He was raised without a father, and he will not pretend the word Father applied to God is easy for everyone. |
| believe | `facilitatorScript.open` | He came to faith out of atheism, and he still has mornings the doubt comes back, and so do I. | cut | He came to faith out of atheism, and he does not pretend doubt is foreign to faith, and neither will I. |

| character-before-competence | `sessions[0].teaching` | This is a study about the foundation, written by a man who has built on sand and watched it shift, and if it makes you uneasy about how you last evaluated a leader, or how you would survive being evaluated yourself, that unease is not condemnation. | generalized | This is a study about the foundation, written from inside the same danger, and if it makes you uneasy about how you last evaluated a leader, or how you would survive being evaluated yourself, that unease is not condemnation. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he told himself the meeting was ministry, the email was care, the next fire was the Lord's work, and looked up at the end of a week having served every table and devoted himself to nothing, and called it faithfulness. | generalized | Leaders tell themselves the meeting was ministry, the email was care, the next fire was the Lord's work, and look up at the end of a week having served every table and devoted themselves to nothing, and call it faithfulness. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know the difference now because I lived a long time on the wrong side of it. I prepared sermons I did not let preach to me. I read Scripture professionally, hunting for what I could use on Sunday, and I lost the older instinct to read it as a hungry man reads, for myself, for my own soul, with no congregation in the room. I was handling the bread. I had stopped eating it, and I did not notice, because my hands were full and the work looked exactly like devotion from outside. | generalized | Many pastors learn the difference only after living a long time on the wrong side of it. They prepare sermons they do not let preach to them. They read Scripture professionally, hunting for what they can use on Sunday, and lose the older instinct to read it as a hungry man reads, for their own souls, with no congregation in the room. They are handling the bread. They have stopped eating it, and they do not notice, because their hands are full and the work looks exactly like devotion from outside. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have lost this fight more than I have won it. I have told myself the meeting was ministry, the email was care, the next fire was the Lord's work, and looked up at the end of a week to find I had served every table in the building and devoted myself to nothing. I called it faithfulness. It was avoidance in faithfulness costume, because tables are visible and prayer is not. | generalized | And most of us lose this fight more than we win it. We tell ourselves the meeting was ministry, the email was care, the next fire was the Lord's work, and look up at the end of a week to find we have served every table in the building and devoted ourselves to nothing. We call it faithfulness. It is avoidance in faithfulness costume, because tables are visible and prayer is not. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he built a room that ran because he was in it and mistook the dependence for fruit, that he prayed daily to an idol with his own face on it without ever naming it. | generalized | Leaders build rooms that run because they are in them and mistake the dependence for fruit, praying daily to an idol with their own face on it without ever naming it. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[2].teaching` | I built one. I stood in a room that ran because I was in it and mistook the dependence for fruit. That is not humility. It is an idol with my own face on it, and I prayed to it daily without ever calling it by name. | generalized | Many leaders build one. That is not humility. It is an idol with the leader's own face on it, prayed to daily without ever being called by name. |
| character-before-competence | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has wanted the clean line of command, the decision he does not have to defend, the meeting where no one tells him he is wrong, and called it leadership when it was impatience and pride in leadership's clothes. | generalized | Many leaders want the clean line of command, the decision they do not have to defend, the meeting where no one tells them they are wrong, and call it leadership when it is impatience and pride in leadership's clothes. |

They notice. |

### christian-nationalism (33)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| christian-nationalism | `summary` | He came up out of atheism into a faith already fused with the flag, he still has flags on his own platform he has not moved, and he writes from inside the indictment. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he writes from inside the indictment. |
| christian-nationalism | `sessions[0].summary` | A man left Bell's office certain his pastor had insulted his grandfather, who landed at Normandy. He had not. The phrase Christian nationalism has been ruined in both directions, used as a slur against patriotic faith by some and as a catchall for people I disagree with who pray by others. | generalized | Plenty of believers hear the phrase Christian nationalism as an insult to a grandfather who landed at Normandy. That is rarely what is meant. The phrase has been ruined in both directions, used as a slur against patriotic faith by some and as a catchall for people I disagree with who pray by others. |
| christian-nationalism | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where the man in the office started. He left certain his pastor had insulted his grandfather, the one who landed at Normandy, because he heard the phrase Christian nationalism as an accusation against everyone who ever stood for a flag with a hand over the heart. He was wrong about what was said. He was right to be careful. | generalized | Start where many believers start. | contentment | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons in a house that always seems to need one more thing, and he writes from inside the wanting, not from a shelf above it. He has refreshed the same screen looking for a number to make him feel like enough. He has been happy for a friend with his mouth and sick over the same news in his chest. He has told God he trusted him for tomorrow while quietly hoarding against it. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he writes from inside the wanting, not from a shelf above it. Most of us have refreshed the same screen looking for a number to make us feel like enough, been happy for a friend with our mouths and sick over the same news in our chests, and told God we trusted him for tomorrow while quietly hoarding against it. |
| contentment | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell describes standing in a garage he had wanted for years, a good house paid for by honest work, and feeling, in the middle of the thing he had wanted, the old low hum start up again, already reaching past it toward the next thing. Bell calls it the hum, the engine that never idles, and he says most of us have mistaken it for ambition when it is closer to a wound. |
| contentment | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell describes the hum, the low engine of wanting that starts up again inside the very thing he had wanted for years. | generalized | Bell describes the hum, the low engine of wanting that starts up again inside the very thing we had wanted for years. |
| contentment | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has refreshed a screen looking for a number that would make him feel like enough. | generalized | Many of us refresh a screen looking for a number that would make us feel like enough. |
| contentment | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell tells it plainly. He stood one afternoon in a garage he had wanted for years, attached to a good house paid for by honest work, a thing he had genuinely earned and genuinely wanted, and there, in the middle of having it, he felt the old low hum start up again. Already reaching. Already past the thing, scanning for the next one. He calls it the hum, the engine in the chest that never quite idles, and he has come to believe that most of us have misnamed it. | generalized | Most of us know the moment. You stand in something you wanted for years, a house or a garage paid for by honest work, a thing genuinely earned and genuinely wanted, and there, in the middle of having it, you feel the old low hum start up again. Already reaching. Already past the thing, scanning for the next one. Call it the hum, the engine in the chest that never quite idles, and most of us have misnamed it. |
| contentment | `sessions[0].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because the writer is named before the reader. I have refreshed a screen looking for a number that would tell me I was finally enough. I have stood in the thing I wanted and felt the hum reach past it. I have five sons watching their father chase, learning from him what a man believes will make him whole. The reaching is not the obvious villain's sin. It hides inside diligence, inside providing, inside the perfectly defensible wish to give my family a little more. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, writer and reader alike. Most of us have refreshed a screen looking for a number that would tell us we were finally enough. We have stood in the thing we wanted and felt the hum reach past it. Our children watch us chase, learning from us what we believe will make us whole. The reaching is not the obvious villain's sin. It hides inside diligence, inside providing, inside the perfectly defensible wish to give our families a little more. |
| contentment | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell admits that the news arrived and he was happy for his friend with his mouth and sick over it in his chest at the same time, and that the two reactions did not feel like a contradiction, they felt like weather. | contentment | `sessions[1].teaching` | Bell tells it against himself, the way he tells most things. The news arrived, a friend's good news, the kind you are supposed to celebrate without reservation, and he found that he was happy for the man with his mouth and sick over it in his chest, both at once, and the strangest part was that the two did not feel like a contradiction. They felt like weather, like the natural climate of his own heart. He calls envy the quietest of all the sins, and he means it precisely. | generalized | Most of us know the moment. | contentment | `sessions[1].teaching` | And now I have to stand where the teaching points, because I am the man happy with his mouth and sick in his chest. | contentment | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here I have to stand where the verse actually points, because I have wanted the poster version. | contentment | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because this is the trap that catches the respectable, and I am the respectable. | generalized | And here I have to stand inside it, because this is the trap that catches the respectable, and most of us are the respectable. |
| contentment | `sessions[3].teaching` | I have called my anxiety prudence. I have called my hoarding wisdom. I have moved my security, quietly, out of the hands of God and into an account, and told myself I was just being careful, and I have five sons watching to learn from their father whether a man's peace lives in God or in a number. | generalized | We call our anxiety prudence. We call our hoarding wisdom. We move our security, quietly, out of the hands of God and into an account, and tell ourselves we are just being careful, and our children are watching to learn from us whether a person's peace lives in God or in a number. |
| contentment | `sessions[4].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside it, because I am the man who cannot stop. I have called my inability to rest devotion. We call our inability to rest devotion. | contentment | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons in a house that always seems to need one more thing, and he writes from his own refreshed screens and his own hoarded tomorrows and his own inability to stop. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he writes as one of the restless, not as one who has arrived. |
