| deep-roots | `summary` | He has preached the ladder and called it grace. He has prayed to a lonely idol while confessing the Trinity. He has used the Bible as a weapon and kept the ledger he claimed Christ had torn up. | generalized | Preachers preach the ladder and call it grace, pray to a lonely idol while confessing the Trinity, use the Bible as a weapon and keep the ledger they claim Christ has torn up. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he had the doctrine right on paper and a lonely idol in his chest, praying to a distant figure he was forever trying to please. | generalized | It is possible to have the doctrine right on paper and a lonely idol in the chest, praying to a distant figure we are forever trying to please. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to confess where I have failed this, because it is easy to teach the Trinity as a museum piece and never let it touch you. There were long seasons I prayed to a single distant figure I half feared, a God I was forever trying to satisfy, and I called that reverence. It was not reverence. It was unbelief in the very God I claimed to confess. I knew the word homoousios. I did not believe, in my body, that the love came first. | generalized | And we have to confess where we fail this, because it is easy to teach the Trinity as a museum piece and never let it touch you. Many believers go through long seasons praying to a single distant figure they half fear, a God they are forever trying to satisfy, and they call that reverence. It is not reverence. It is unbelief in the very God they claim to confess. You can know the word homoousios and not believe, in your body, that the love came first. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].summary` | Every argument about the Bible that Bell has watched turn bitter was not, underneath, an argument about the Bible. It was an argument about fear. | generalized | Most bitter arguments about the Bible are not, underneath, arguments about the Bible. They are arguments about fear. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].summary` | Then the harder fear, the manuscripts and the canon, the game of telephone and the back-room vote, and Bell answers both, and finally implicates himself, because he has used this book as a weapon. | generalized | Then the harder fear, the manuscripts and the canon, the game of telephone and the back-room vote, and Bell answers both, and finally implicates the reader and himself, because this book is so easily used as a weapon. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has used this book as a weapon, ending conversations by quoting a verse and hiding his own will behind the will of God. | generalized | Many of us have used this book as a weapon, ending conversations by quoting a verse and hiding our own will behind the will of God. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].discussion[4].behind` | A high view of the Bible did not protect Bell from using it as a sword, it armed him for it. | generalized | A high view of the Bible does not protect anyone from using it as a sword. It can arm them for it. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].teaching` | Every argument about the Bible that Bell has ever watched turn bitter was not, underneath, an argument about the Bible. It was an argument about fear. | generalized | Most bitter arguments about the Bible are not, underneath, arguments about the Bible. They are arguments about fear. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have used this book as a weapon. I confess it because the fear of the second person at the table is not paranoia, it is memory. I have ended conversations by quoting a verse, used the authority of Scripture to defend a position that was really just mine, and hidden my own will behind the will of God. A high view of the Bible did not protect me from that. It armed me for it. The remedy was never a lower view. It was the discovery that this word was given to make me complete, which means it was given first to correct me, and a man holding a sword for his own defense has not let it do its actual work. | generalized | Many of us have used this book as a weapon. Say it plainly, because the fear of the second person at the table is not paranoia, it is memory. We have ended conversations by quoting a verse, used the authority of Scripture to defend a position that was really just ours, and hidden our own will behind the will of God. A high view of the Bible does not protect anyone from that. It can arm them for it. The remedy was never a lower view. It is the discovery that this word was given to make us complete, which means it was given first to correct us, and a man holding a sword for his own defense has not let it do its actual work. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell says the heresies the councils fought are not museum pieces but the defaults of the lazy heart, that some days he wants Jesus only human and admirable from a distance, and some days only divine and too exalted to feel what he feels. | generalized | Bell says the heresies the councils fought are not museum pieces but the defaults of the lazy heart, that some days we want Jesus only human and admirable from a distance, and some days only divine and too exalted to feel what we feel. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[2].teaching` | I have to admit how easily I make Jesus convenient, because the heresies are the defaults of the lazy heart. Some days I want him only human, a wise teacher I can admire from a comfortable distance. Some days I want him only divine, too exalted to have felt what I feel. Both are easier to live with than the real one, and both leave me alone. The whole and difficult Christ, true God and true man, is the only one who can both understand me at the bottom and rescue me from above. The judge climbed down off the bench, stood in the place of the accused, and was condemned in my stead. | generalized | We have to admit how easily we make Jesus convenient, because the heresies are the defaults of the lazy heart. Some days we want him only human, a wise teacher we can admire from a comfortable distance. Some days we want him only divine, too exalted to have felt what we feel. Both are easier to live with than the real one, and both leave us alone. The whole and difficult Christ, true God and true man, is the only one who can both understand us at the bottom and rescue us from above. The judge climbed down off the bench, stood in the place of the accused, and was condemned in our stead. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he preaches a free righteousness from the pulpit and walks straight back onto the holy stairs, scoring his acceptance by how the sermon landed. | generalized | Preachers can proclaim a free righteousness from the pulpit and walk straight back onto the holy stairs, scoring their acceptance by how the sermon landed. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[3].teaching` | I keep the ledger too, and writing about Luther does not exempt me. I have stood in a pulpit preaching a free righteousness from Christ and walked off the platform straight back onto the holy stairs, climbing again on my own knees, scoring my acceptance by how the sermon landed and how I measured against the men I quietly compete with. I preach sola fide and live, much of the week, by sola me. The gate to paradise Luther found is a gate I keep walking back out of, into the courtyard of my own performance, because the courtyard is where I am in control and the gift is unbearable to a man who would rather earn. | generalized | We keep the ledger too, and writing or reading about Luther does not exempt us. A preacher can stand in a pulpit preaching a free righteousness from Christ and walk off the platform straight back onto the holy stairs, climbing again on his own knees, scoring his acceptance by how the sermon landed and how he measured against the men he quietly competes with. We confess sola fide and live, much of the week, by sola me. The gate to paradise Luther found is a gate we keep walking back out of, into the courtyard of our own performance, because the courtyard is where we are in control and the gift is unbearable to people who would rather earn. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[4].discussion[4].behind` | Bell confesses he performed the already while privately living in the not yet, and the performance sent struggling people home to fight alone. | generalized | Leaders perform the already while privately living in the not yet, and the performance sends struggling people home to fight alone. |
| deep-roots | `sessions[4].teaching` | I have read this book the wrong way for long stretches, hunting for sermon material and the verse that would win the argument, treating the living story as a quarry and myself as the man with the pickaxe, making the Bible about me and missing the figure the whole thing was bending toward. And I have hidden my own war, standing in front of people performing the already, the victorious settled pastor, while privately living deep in the not yet, fighting the same fears I fought a decade ago, ashamed that a man who preaches freedom is still so unfree. The performance was a lie about the overlap. It told struggling people the war should already be over, and sent them home to fight alone and silent because I would not admit I was fighting too. | generalized | Many of us have read this book the wrong way for long stretches, hunting for sermon material and the verse that would win the argument, treating the living story as a quarry and ourselves as the one with the pickaxe, making the Bible about us and missing the figure the whole thing was bending toward. And pastors hide their own war, standing in front of people performing the already, the victorious settled pastor, while privately living deep in the not yet, fighting the same fears they fought a decade ago, ashamed that one who preaches freedom is still so unfree. The performance is a lie about the overlap. It tells struggling people the war should already be over, and sends them home to fight alone and silent because the one in front will not admit he is fighting too. |
| deep-roots | `leaderPrimer` | The writer of these essays came to Christian doctrine from atheism, an adult outsider who had to learn the grammar from scratch, which is why the study keeps translating instead of assuming, and why you can promise your intimidated members that no prior vocabulary is required. | cut | The writer of these essays came to Christian doctrine from atheism, which is one reason the study keeps translating instead of assuming, and why you can promise your intimidated members that no prior vocabulary is required. |
| deep-roots | `faq[9].a` | Bell implicates himself in every one of these, and the study asks the same honesty of you. | generalized | Bell does not exempt himself from any of these, and the study asks the same honesty of you. |
| deep-roots | `faq[10].a` | He confesses, in every session, that he is inside the failure. He has preached the ladder and called it grace, prayed to a lonely god while confessing the Trinity, used the Bible as a weapon, kept the ledger he told everyone Christ had torn up, performed the victory of the already while hiding his own war in the not yet. | generalized | He does not exempt himself, in any session, from the failures he names, the ladder preached and called grace, the lonely god prayed to while confessing the Trinity, the Bible used as a weapon, the ledger kept after it was declared torn up, the victory of the already performed while the war of the not yet stays hidden. |

### end-times (15)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| end-times | `summary` | And the writer stands inside all of it, because Bell came to faith from atheism arguing that the whole thing was a fairy tale for people afraid to die, and he has done his own date-math in the dark, and he has used the end times as an escape from a world he was supposed to love. | cut | And the writer stands inside all of it. Bell came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the date-math in the dark or the use of the end times as an escape from a world we are supposed to love. |
| end-times | `sessions[0].summary` | A man once told Bell, with total certainty, that the European Union was the revived Roman Empire of Daniel and that the end would arrive within five years. That was a long time ago, and the man is still waiting. | generalized | Plenty of Christians have been told, with total certainty, that the European Union was the revived Roman Empire of Daniel and that the end would arrive within five years. The predictions have come and gone, and the predictors are still waiting. |
| end-times | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he once used end-times speculation to feel like an insider, one of the few who really understood. | generalized | End-times speculation can make us feel like insiders, one of the few who really understand. |
| end-times | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man who knew. He told Bell, with total and untroubled certainty, that the European Union was the revived Roman Empire predicted in Daniel, that the pieces were all in place, and that the end would arrive inside of five years. That conversation happened a long time ago. The man is, as far as anyone knows, still here, still waiting, and probably still certain, having simply moved the date. He is not a fool. He is part of a very long line, and the line is the first thing a study of the last things has to reckon with honestly. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, the believer who knows. He will tell you, with total and untroubled certainty, that the European Union is the revived Roman Empire predicted in Daniel, that the pieces are all in place, and that the end will arrive inside of five years. Such predictions have been made for decades. The people who made them are still here, still waiting, and often still certain, having simply moved the date. They are not fools. They are part of a very long line, and the line is the first thing a study of the last things has to reckon with honestly. |
| end-times | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I have felt the pull. As a younger believer I loved the feeling of being an insider, one of the few who had cracked the code, who could read the newspaper with the Bible open and see what the ordinary Christians missed. That was not faith. That was pride wearing the costume of prophecy, and Jesus said plainly that not even the Son knew the day or the hour, which should have ended my certainty and did not, for far too long. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us have felt the pull. Many believers love the feeling of being an insider, one of the few who has cracked the code, who can read the newspaper with the Bible open and see what the ordinary Christians miss. That is not faith. That is pride wearing the costume of prophecy, and Jesus said plainly that not even the Son knew the day or the hour, which should end our certainty and too often does not. |
| end-times | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits the rapture once appealed to him as an escape hatch, a way of not having to face the world's trouble. | generalized | The rapture can appeal as an escape hatch, a way of not having to face the world's trouble. |
| end-times | `sessions[2].teaching` | What is at stake is more pastoral than people realize, and here I have to climb inside it. When I first encountered the rapture as a young believer, freshly out of atheism and still half-braced for the world to be as bad as I had always believed, the appeal was not mainly exegetical. The appeal was escape. The promise that I would be lifted out before the worst of it, that the trouble was for the people left behind, not for me. And I have come to think that a hope built mainly on getting out can quietly excuse us from loving a world we expect to abandon. That is not a charge against everyone who holds the view, many of whom love the world fiercely. It is a confession of what the view did in my own heart, and a question I have to keep asking. | generalized | What is at stake is more pastoral than people realize, and here we have to climb inside it. For many believers the appeal of the rapture is not mainly exegetical. The appeal is escape. The promise that we will be lifted out before the worst of it, that the trouble is for the people left behind, not for us. And a hope built mainly on getting out can quietly excuse us from loving a world we expect to abandon. That is not a charge against everyone who holds the view, many of whom love the world fiercely. It is a question every one of us has to keep asking. |
| end-times | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he swings between the two errors, some weeks fleeing the world and some weeks frantic to fix it all himself. | generalized | Most of us swing between the two errors, some weeks fleeing the world and some weeks frantic to fix it all ourselves. |
| end-times | `sessions[3].teaching` | Now watch how two distortions pull at this tension from opposite sides, because almost everyone leans one way or the other, and I lean both ways depending on the week. | generalized | Now watch how two distortions pull at this tension from opposite sides, because almost everyone leans one way or the other, and many of us lean both ways depending on the week. |
| end-times | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to confess I swing badly between the two errors. Some weeks I am the escapist, weary of the world, half-wishing to be done with it, treating my actual life as a waiting room. Other weeks I am the frantic fixer, gripping the wheel, trying to force outcomes that were never mine to force, crushed when the kingdom does not arrive on my timetable. The gospel steadies me in neither extreme but in the center, in the already and the not yet held together, where I can do real good without the burden of finishing it, and wait with real hope without despising the present. | generalized | And most of us swing badly between the two errors. Some weeks we are the escapist, weary of the world, half-wishing to be done with it, treating our actual lives as a waiting room. Other weeks we are the frantic fixer, gripping the wheel, trying to force outcomes that were never ours to force, crushed when the kingdom does not arrive on our timetable. The gospel steadies us in neither extreme but in the center, in the already and the not yet held together, where we can do real good without the burden of finishing it, and wait with real hope without despising the present. |
| end-times | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Take this seriously and without defensiveness, because Bell himself once made this exact argument as an atheist and means to answer it honestly rather than dismiss it. | cut | Take this seriously and without defensiveness, because Bell came to faith from atheism and means to answer it honestly rather than dismiss it. |
| end-times | `sessions[4].teaching` | I came to faith out of atheism, and the argument I used to make, the one I made loudest, was that all of this was a fairy tale, a story frightened people told because they could not bear that death was the end. And I will not pretend that suspicion is foolish, because a hope this large could be wishful thinking, the longing does not prove the thing. But here is what stopped me, and it is where I will leave you. | cut | I came to faith out of atheism, and I take seriously the suspicion that all of this is a fairy tale, a story frightened people tell because they cannot bear that death is the end. And I will not pretend that suspicion is foolish, because a hope this large could be wishful thinking, the longing does not prove the thing. But here is where I will leave you. |
| end-times | `leaderPrimer` | The writer of this study has sat across from a man certain the European Union was Daniel's final kingdom on a five-year clock, and he has also stood at gravesides where people needed the actual Christian hope, resurrection and new creation, not a decoder ring, and the distance between those two conversations is what these five weeks exist to close. | generalized | Most pastors have heard someone certain the European Union was Daniel's final kingdom on a five-year clock, and most have also stood at gravesides where people needed the actual Christian hope, resurrection and new creation, not a decoder ring, and the distance between those two conversations is what these five weeks exist to close. |
| end-times | `faq[11].a` | It is a fair suspicion, and the study takes it seriously because Bell once made exactly that argument as an atheist, that the Christian hope was a fairy tale for people too afraid to face that death is the end. | cut | It is a fair suspicion, and the study takes it seriously, because Bell came to faith from atheism and the argument that the Christian hope is a fairy tale for people too afraid to face that death is the end deserves a real answer. |

### fatherhood (7)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| fatherhood | `summary` | He came to God out of atheism, with no inherited faith and no felt picture of what a good father even was, and he is now raising five sons of his own, fathering forward into a gap he carried into adulthood. He has stood at a kitchen table and corrected a child in anger he regretted before the words were finished. He has traded presence for provision and called it love. He has waited, more than once, for a word of blessing from his own father that never came. | generalized | He came to God out of atheism, and he is now raising five sons of his own, fathering forward into a gap. Many fathers have corrected a child in anger they regretted before the words were finished, traded presence for provision and called it love, and waited for a word of blessing that never came. |
| fatherhood | `sessions[0].teaching` | I was raised without a father at all, and I came to God out of atheism with no working picture of what a good father even was, so when I say I know the gap, I am not performing sympathy. | cut | I was raised without a father at all, and I came to God out of atheism, so when I say I know the gap, I am not performing sympathy. |
| fatherhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have made this trade myself and dressed it as devotion to my family, and the turning was not a technique I discovered but a sin I confessed. | generalized | Many fathers have made this trade and dressed it as devotion to family, and the turning is not a technique to discover but a sin to confess. |
| fatherhood | `sessions[2].teaching` | I have had to make that walk back to a child's room more times than I want to count, and every time the flesh tells me that admitting fault will cost me my authority. | generalized | Most fathers have to make that walk back to a child's room more times than they want to count, and every time the flesh says that admitting fault will cost them their authority. |
| fatherhood | `sessions[3].teaching` | I know that scoreboard from the inside. A man raised without a father does not stop looking for the blessing; he just looks for it everywhere, in every form of approval he can find, and never quite believes any of it. | cut | A man raised without a father does not stop looking for the blessing; he just looks for it everywhere, in every form of approval he can find, and never quite believes any of it. |
| fatherhood | `leaderPrimer` | He was raised without a father, came to God from atheism with no felt picture of what a good father even was, and is now raising five sons and getting it wrong often enough to know the territory from the inside; that is the posture you are being asked to lead from, fellow learner and not expert. | cut | He was raised without a father, came to God from atheism, and is now raising five sons; he writes as a fellow learner and not an expert, and that is the posture you are being asked to lead from. |
| fatherhood | `faq[7].a` | The writer came to God from atheism with no inherited faith at all, so the study assumes nothing and condescends to no one. | cut | The writer came to God from atheism, so the study assumes nothing and condescends to no one. |

### forgiveness (13)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| forgiveness | `summary` | He grew up without a father, and he has spent years learning what it is to forgive a man who never asked to be forgiven and never came back to ask. He has held a grudge and called it standing for justice. He writes from inside the unforgiveness. | generalized | He was raised without a father, and he does not stand above the long work of forgiving. Most of us have held a grudge and called it standing for justice. He writes from inside that struggle, not above it. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after a service that he could not forgive his father, and that he was tired of being told he had to, because every time someone said forgive they seemed to mean act like it never happened. He was not refusing to forgive. He was refusing a counterfeit, and no one had ever told him there was a real thing underneath it. | generalized | Many people say they cannot forgive a parent, and that they are tired of being told they have to, because every time someone says forgive they seem to mean act like it never happened. Often they are not refusing to forgive. They are refusing a counterfeit, and no one has ever told them there is a real thing underneath it. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has held a grudge for years and called it standing for justice. | generalized | Many of us have held a grudge for years and called it standing for justice. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a service. He told Bell he could not forgive his father, and that he was sick of being told he had to, because every time someone said the word forgive, what they seemed to mean was act like it never happened. He was not, it turned out, refusing to forgive. He was refusing a counterfeit, and no one had ever told him there was a real thing buried underneath the forgery. | generalized | Start with something many people say, that they cannot forgive a parent, and that they are sick of being told they have to, because every time someone says the word forgive, what they seem to mean is act like it never happened. Very often they are not refusing to forgive. They are refusing a counterfeit, and no one has ever told them there is a real thing buried underneath the forgery. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I have reached for all three. I grew up without a father, and for years I called my refusal to forgive him a stand for justice, when it was closer to a grudge I had grown comfortable carrying. I told myself that to forgive would be to excuse, that to release the debt would be to say the wound did not matter. It was a lie I told myself, and it kept me bound to the very man I was trying to be free of. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us have reached for all three. Many of us have called a refusal to forgive a stand for justice, when it was closer to a grudge we had grown comfortable carrying. We tell ourselves that to forgive would be to excuse, that to release the debt would be to say the wound did not matter. It is a lie, and it keeps us bound to the very person we are trying to be free of. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess I have been that servant more often than I would like to admit. I have clutched the hundred denarii someone owed me, the real wrong, the genuine debt, and I have throttled them with it in my heart for years, all while standing forgiven a debt I could never name the size of. The prison was real, and I had locked myself in it, and I had thrown away the key and called the throwing away justice. | generalized | And most of us have been that servant more often than we would like to admit. We have clutched the hundred denarii someone owed us, the real wrong, the genuine debt, and throttled them with it in our hearts for years, all while standing forgiven a debt we could never name the size of. The prison is real, and we lock ourselves in it, and throw away the key and call the throwing away justice. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[2].teaching` | But now comes the distinction that, when people finally see it, sets them free, and I have watched it set people free in real time. | generalized | But now comes the distinction that, when people finally see it, sets them free, and it has set a great many people free. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to tell you I know this one from the inside. The father who was not there never came back to say he was sorry. There was no scene of repentance, no reconciliation, no mended relationship, and for a long time I let that fact keep me locked up, telling myself I could not forgive a man who had never asked, as though his silence were a wall I could not climb. It was not his wall. It was mine. The day I understood that I could forgive him before God without ever reconciling with him, that I could release the debt without restoring a relationship that was never going to be safe or even possible, was the day the cell door opened. Not because he changed. He never did. But because I stopped waiting on a man who was never coming to set me free, and handed the whole thing to the God who judges justly. | generalized | Many people know this one from the inside. The parent who was not there never comes back to say he is sorry. There is no scene of repentance, no reconciliation, no mended relationship, and for a long time that fact keeps them locked up, telling themselves they cannot forgive someone who never asked, as though the silence were a wall they could not climb. It is not the other person's wall. It is their own. The day a person understands that they can forgive before God without ever reconciling, that they can release the debt without restoring a relationship that was never going to be safe or even possible, is the day the cell door opens. Not because the other person changed. Often they never do. But because they stop waiting on someone who is never coming to set them free, and hand the whole thing to the God who judges justly. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has used the language of forgiveness to avoid the harder work of confronting a wrong. | generalized | Many of us have used the language of forgiveness to avoid the harder work of confronting a wrong. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to confess that I have stood on the wrong side of this, that I have reached for cheap grace because the costly kind asked more of me than I wanted to give. I have called my silence peace when it was cowardice. I have used the soft language of forgiveness to avoid the hard work of confronting a wrong, telling myself I was being gracious when I was only being afraid. | generalized | And most of us have stood on the wrong side of this, reaching for cheap grace because the costly kind asked more of us than we wanted to give. We have called our silence peace when it was cowardice. We have used the soft language of forgiveness to avoid the hard work of confronting a wrong, telling ourselves we were being gracious when we were only being afraid. |
| forgiveness | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to stand inside all three of these, because I am not finished with any of them. There are wounds from a childhood without a father that I have forgiven and will keep forgiving, I suspect, for the rest of my life, handing the same old weight to God again every time it rises, and I have had to learn to call that obedience instead of failure. There have been seasons I held a fist of blame against God for the way my early life went, and the day I finally said it out loud to him, ugly and honest, was not the day I sinned against him but the day I started to heal. And there is plenty I have done that I have had to stop paying penance for, plenty of self-punishment I had to lay down by finally believing the cross meant what it said. | generalized | And we have to stand inside all three of these, because few of us are finished with any of them. Wounds from childhood are often forgiven and forgiven again for the rest of a life, the same old weight handed to God every time it rises, and we have to learn to call that obedience instead of failure. Many of us have held a fist of blame against God for the way our lives went, and the day we finally say it out loud to him, ugly and honest, is not the day we sin against him but the day we start to heal. And most of us have done things we have had to stop paying penance for, self-punishment we have had to lay down by finally believing the cross meant what it said. |
| forgiveness | `leaderPrimer` | Bell knows this territory from the fatherless side. He carried a fist of blame for a man who never came back to ask, and he called the grudge justice for years. He also knows the other prisoner in the room, the one who cannot forgive the person in the mirror and has been paying a private penance on a debt the cross already settled. | cut | Bell was raised without a father. The study also has in view the other prisoner in the room, the one who cannot forgive the person in the mirror and has been paying a private penance on a debt the cross already settled. |
| forgiveness | `faq[10].a` | He grew up without a father and held a fist of blame against a man who never came back to ask for it, and he called that grudge a stand for justice for years. He has used the soft language of grace to dodge confronting a wrong, and he has paid penance for sins God had already forgiven. He writes from inside the unforgiveness, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot help a wounded person from above without your help curdling into either shame or distance. | generalized | He was raised without a father, and he does not write as one who has finished forgiving. Most of us have called a grudge a stand for justice, used the soft language of grace to dodge confronting a wrong, and paid penance for sins God had already forgiven. He writes from inside that struggle, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot help a wounded person from above without your help curdling into either shame or distance. |

### friendship (17)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| friendship | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and knows the particular loneliness of the man who has learned to need no one. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the friend who let the friendship starve because he was busy, who kept the acquaintance and lost the brother, who confused being surrounded with being known. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not point from a safe distance. Most of us have been the friend who let the friendship starve because we were busy, who kept the acquaintance and lost the brother, who confused being surrounded with being known. |
| friendship | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell, without drama, that if he were in the hospital he was not sure who he would call, and then added quickly that he was fine, that he had plenty of people. He was describing a life full of contacts and empty of friends, and he is not rare. He is the shape of the age. | generalized | Many people, asked whom they would call if they landed in the hospital, are not sure, and then add quickly that they are fine, that they have plenty of people. They are describing a life full of contacts and empty of friends, and they are not rare. They are the shape of the age. |
| friendship | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell describes a man who had plenty of contacts and did not know who he would call from a hospital bed. | generalized | Bell describes the person with plenty of contacts who does not know whom he would call from a hospital bed. |
| friendship | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell says we did not lose friendship to bad luck, we traded it, and he counts himself first among the traders. | generalized | Bell says we did not lose friendship to bad luck, we traded it, and he does not exempt himself from the trade. |
| friendship | `sessions[0].teaching` | A man told Bell, without any drama in his voice, that he had recently wondered who he would actually call if he landed in the hospital, and had not been able to come up with a name he was sure of. Then he added, fast, the way people do, that he was fine, that he had plenty of people. Both things were true. He had plenty of people and not one he was sure of, a life full of contacts and empty of friends, and the reason his story matters is that he is not the exception. He is the shape of the age. | generalized | Ask people whom they would actually call if they landed in the hospital, and many cannot come up with a name they are sure of. Then they add, fast, the way people do, that they are fine, that they have plenty of people. Both things are true. They have plenty of people and not one they are sure of, a life full of contacts and empty of friends, and the reason it matters is that this is not the exception. It is the shape of the age. |
| friendship | `sessions[0].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because I know this ground from the underside. I came to faith out of atheism, and I was raised without a father, and one of the first things you learn in that soil is how to need no one, how to be self-contained, how to call your isolation strength. I have let friendships die because I was busy and told myself they would keep. I have kept the acquaintance and lost the brother. I have been the man surrounded and unknown, and I dressed it up as independence. So when I say we did not lose friendship to bad luck, I mean I did not. I traded it, one reasonable postponement at a time, for convenience and mobility and the thin frictionless company of a screen that asks nothing and gives nothing back. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it. Most of us have let friendships die because we were busy and told ourselves they would keep. We have kept the acquaintance and lost the brother. We have been surrounded and unknown, and dressed it up as independence. So when I say we did not lose friendship to bad luck, I mean it of all of us. We traded it, one reasonable postponement at a time, for convenience and mobility and the thin frictionless company of a screen that asks nothing and gives nothing back. |
| friendship | `sessions[1].teaching` | Here is where I have to be honest about myself, because I have loved my friends more lightly than Jonathan loved David, and I know why. | generalized | Here is where we have to be honest about ourselves, because most of us love our friends more lightly than Jonathan loved David, and we know why. |
| friendship | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have kept my friendships in the range where they ask nothing, and called it respecting their freedom, when often it was protecting my own. | generalized | Many of us keep our friendships in the range where they ask nothing, and call it respecting their freedom, when often it is protecting our own. |
| friendship | `sessions[2].teaching` | And now the reversal, which is the part I know from the inside and do not enjoy admitting. | generalized | And now the reversal, which is the part most of us know from the inside and do not enjoy admitting. |
| friendship | `sessions[2].teaching` | I have watched friends walk toward things I feared would cost them, and I have said nothing, and I have called my silence kindness, and it was not kindness. | generalized | Most of us have watched friends walk toward things we feared would cost them, and said nothing, and called our silence kindness, and it was not kindness. |
| friendship | `sessions[3].teaching` | Take the strong case for keeping God at a reverent distance, because it is serious and I feel its pull. | generalized | Take the strong case for keeping God at a reverent distance, because it is serious and its pull is real. |
| friendship | `sessions[3].teaching` | Here is where it turns for me, and where I have to admit my own resistance. Raised without a father, come to faith out of atheism, I am far more comfortable with God as a master I serve than as a friend who knows me, because a master keeps his distance and a friend comes close, and closeness is the thing the self-protected heart has trained itself to avoid. I can serve God from across the room. Being called his friend asks me to let him near, and something in me would rather keep him at the reverent distance where I am safe. But the gospel will not leave me there. The gospel is not mainly that God became my master, though he is my Lord. It is that the Holy One, without laying down one ounce of his holiness, came near enough to call me friend, and then walked out of that upper room and proved the word at the one place words are proved, laying down his life for the friends who ran. That is the friendship on offer. Not a buddy who spares me trouble. A friend who went into the grave ahead of me and calls me, still, by the name I did not dare claim and could never have earned. | generalized | Here is where it turns, and where many of us have to admit our own resistance. Many of us are far more comfortable with God as a master we serve than as a friend who knows us, because a master keeps his distance and a friend comes close, and closeness is the thing the self-protected heart has trained itself to avoid. We can serve God from across the room. Being called his friend asks us to let him near, and something in us would rather keep him at the reverent distance where we are safe. But the gospel will not leave us there. The gospel is not mainly that God became our master, though he is our Lord. It is that the Holy One, without laying down one ounce of his holiness, came near enough to call us friends, and then walked out of that upper room and proved the word at the one place words are proved, laying down his life for the friends who ran. That is the friendship on offer. Not a buddy who spares us trouble. A friend who went into the grave ahead of us and calls us, still, by the name we did not dare claim and could never have earned. |
| friendship | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to say plainly that I have failed at this more than I have succeeded. I have let good friendships die from nothing more dramatic than drift, told myself I would reconnect when things slowed down, and things never slowed down, and the friend and I became people who used to be close. I have chosen the couch over the call more times than I can count. So I am not preaching down at anyone. I am naming the fight I am still losing more often than I win. | generalized | And most of us have failed at this more than we have succeeded. We have let good friendships die from nothing more dramatic than drift, told ourselves we would reconnect when things slowed down, and things never slowed down, and we and the friend became people who used to be close. We have chosen the couch over the call more times than we can count. So I am not preaching down at anyone. I am naming a fight all of us keep losing more often than we win. |
| friendship | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and knows firsthand the loneliness of the man who has trained himself to need no one, and he names his own failures at friendship plainly, the calls not returned, the brothers let slip into contacts. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he names the common failures at friendship plainly, the calls not returned, the brothers let slip into contacts, without exempting himself. |
| friendship | `faq[8].a` | The real qualification is not a rich full circle of friends, which the writer openly admits he does not always have, but a willingness to be honest about your own loneliness and your own failures at friendship, the calls not returned, the brothers let slip into contacts. | cut | The real qualification is not a rich full circle of friends but a willingness to be honest about your own loneliness and your own failures at friendship, the calls not returned, the brothers let slip into contacts. |
| friendship | `faq[9].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and knows firsthand the loneliness of the man who has trained himself to need no one, and he names his own failures at friendship plainly, the friendships let die from drift, the couch chosen over the call, the brothers who became contacts. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he names the common failures at friendship plainly, the friendships let die from drift, the couch chosen over the call, the brothers who became contacts, without exempting himself. |
| friendship | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer puts himself inside all of this, a man who came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and knows the loneliness of needing no one, still learning the work he is asking of us, and so are we. | cut | Third, the writer puts himself inside all of this, a man who came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is still learning the work he is asking of us, and so are we. |

### fruit-of-the-spirit (21)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism and spent his first Christian years trying to manufacture the fruit by sheer effort, gritting his teeth toward patience with five sons in a small house, performing a kindness he did not feel, calling the performance growth. He does not point from a safe distance. He knows the exhaustion of the counterfeit, the self-produced virtue that looks like fruit and tastes like plastic. He writes from inside the failure. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he does not point from a safe distance. Many of us have spent years trying to manufacture the fruit by sheer effort, gritting our teeth toward patience, performing a kindness we did not feel, calling the performance growth, and we know the exhaustion of the counterfeit, the self-produced virtue that looks like fruit and tastes like plastic. He writes from inside that failure. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].summary` | For years after his conversion Bell tried to become a patient man the only way he knew, by force, setting his jaw against his own temper the way a man sets his shoulder against a stuck door, and he could hold it for an afternoon before it gave. He calls it the manufactured virtue, the thing that looks like fruit from a distance and turns out to be plastic when you bite it. | generalized | Many believers try for years to become patient the only way they know, by force, setting the jaw against their own temper the way a man sets his shoulder against a stuck door, and they can hold it for an afternoon before it gives. Bell calls it the manufactured virtue, the thing that looks like fruit from a distance and turns out to be plastic when you bite it. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he tried for years to manufacture patience by force and could hold it for an afternoon before it gave. | generalized | Bell says many of us try for years to manufacture patience by force and can hold it for an afternoon before it gives. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that his manufactured kindness fooled the neighbor and exhausted him. | generalized | Manufactured kindness can fool the neighbor and exhaust the one performing it. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].objections[1].response` | There are unbelievers kinder than many Christians, and any honest believer, Bell first among them, has been humbled by the patience of a person who claims no God at all. | cut | There are unbelievers kinder than many Christians, and any honest believer has been humbled by the patience of a person who claims no God at all. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].teaching` | For years after he came to faith from atheism, Bell tried to become a patient man the only way an ex-atheist self-made man knew how, by force. He set his jaw against his own temper the way you set your shoulder against a door that has swelled in its frame, and with five sons in a small and loud house he had endless occasion to try. He could hold it for an afternoon. By evening the door gave, and the temper he had been leaning against all day came through the frame at once, and he would lie awake ashamed and resolve to lean harder tomorrow. He calls what he was making the manufactured virtue. | generalized | Many believers try to become patient the only way a self-made person knows how, by force. They set the jaw against their own temper the way you set your shoulder against a door that has swelled in its frame, and a full and loud house gives endless occasion to try. They can hold it for an afternoon. By evening the door gives, and the temper they have been leaning against all day comes through the frame at once, and they lie awake ashamed and resolve to lean harder tomorrow. Bell calls it the manufactured virtue. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[0].teaching` | I know this from the inside. My manufactured kindness passed inspection everywhere but in my own chest, where I knew it was a costume, and the wearing of it exhausted me in a way the real thing never does, because the real thing is grown and the costume is carried. | generalized | Many of us know this from the inside. A manufactured kindness can pass inspection everywhere but in our own chest, where we know it is a costume, and the wearing of it exhausts us in a way the real thing never does, because the real thing is grown and the costume is carried. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[1].teaching` | Here I have to speak to the anxious, because I have pastored too many of them to be careless, and I have been one. | generalized | Here I have to speak to the anxious, because there are too many of them in every room to be careless. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[1].teaching` | There is real truth in it, and the wiring is real, and I have watched sincere Christians beaten with this text who needed a doctor and a friend, not a rebuke. | generalized | There is real truth in it, and the wiring is real, and sincere Christians have been beaten with this text who needed a doctor and a friend, not a rebuke. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[2].objections[1].response` | Bell would be the first to say his own kindness has often been exactly that, a costume worn for the audience. | generalized | Bell would be the first to say that much of our kindness has often been exactly that, a costume worn for the audience. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here is the reversal, and I have to stand inside it, because I am the man who is patient with strangers and short with his own sons, kind to the congregation and clipped with the people who share my roof. | generalized | And here is the reversal, and we have to stand inside it, because many of us are patient with strangers and short with our own children, kind to acquaintances and clipped with the people who share our roof. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[2].teaching` | I have provoked heaven for years and been met with long-suffering, presumed on a kindness meant to lead me to repentance, taken a goodness I returned with nothing, and then walked out of that ocean of mercy and been stingy with a thimble of it toward someone who merely annoyed me. | generalized | We have provoked heaven for years and been met with long-suffering, presumed on a kindness meant to lead us to repentance, taken a goodness we returned with nothing, and then walked out of that ocean of mercy and been stingy with a thimble of it toward someone who merely annoyed us. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[3].summary` | The most disciplined man Bell knew was a slave to the very thing he controlled, and the freest was not the one who suppressed his desires but the one whose deepest desire had been changed. | generalized | The most disciplined people are often slaves to the very thing they control, and the freest are not the ones who suppress their desires but the ones whose deepest desire has been changed. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[3].teaching` | But watch what willpower alone actually accomplishes, because I have watched it in myself and in the most disciplined man I ever knew, who controlled his appetite with an iron hand and was, underneath the control, a slave to the very thing he controlled. | generalized | But watch what willpower alone actually accomplishes, because it is visible in the most disciplined people, who control an appetite with an iron hand and are, underneath the control, slaves to the very thing they control. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[4].objections[0].response` | Bell would not deny it about himself. | generalized | Bell does not exempt himself. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Grant that the danger is real, because the framing can absolutely be abused exactly as described, turned into a blanket excuse that treats every failure as expected and demands no repentance, and Bell has seen that abuse and rejects it. | cut | Grant that the danger is real, because the framing can absolutely be abused exactly as described, turned into a blanket excuse that treats every failure as expected and demands no repentance, and Bell rejects that abuse. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism and spent his first Christian years manufacturing a plastic virtue that fooled everyone but him, and he still wakes up some mornings with none of the fruit and has to be given it again. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he writes as one who still needs the fruit to be given, not as a finished example. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `faq[10].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism and spent his first Christian years producing a plastic goodness that fooled everyone but him, gritting out patience with five sons in a small house, performing a kindness he did not feel, and he still wakes some mornings with none of the fruit and has to be given it again. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he writes as one who still needs the fruit to be given, not as one displaying a finished harvest. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer puts himself inside all of it, an ex-atheist who spent years manufacturing a plastic virtue that fooled everyone but him, and who still wakes up some mornings with none of the fruit, and so will we. | cut | Third, the writer puts himself inside all of it, a man who came to faith from atheism and still needs the fruit to be given, and so will we. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | It is honest about the war the fruit grows in, careful with those carrying real sorrow and anxiety, and it implicates the writer first, an ex-atheist who spent years manufacturing a virtue that fooled everyone but him. | cut | It is honest about the war the fruit grows in, careful with those carrying real sorrow and anxiety, and it implicates the writer first, a man who came to faith from atheism. |
| fruit-of-the-spirit | `promoKit.inviteCaption` | Written from inside the struggle, by a man who came to faith from atheism and spent years making a plastic virtue, not from a safe distance above it. | cut | Written from inside the struggle, by a man who came to faith from atheism, not from a safe distance above it. |

### generosity (15)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| generosity | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons on a pastor's income, and he knows the particular fear that makes a hand close. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the man who called his tightfistedness stewardship, who gave the round number that cost him nothing and felt generous doing it, who lay awake counting a security that was never going to save him. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he does not point from a safe distance. Most of us have called our tightfistedness stewardship, given the round number that cost us nothing and felt generous doing it, and lain awake counting a security that was never going to save us. |
| generosity | `sessions[0].teaching` | But here is the line the careful saver has to watch, and I have to watch it in myself first. | generalized | But here is the line the careful saver has to watch, and every one of us has to watch it in ourselves first. |
| generosity | `sessions[0].teaching` | I have made that bet. I came to faith from atheism, and I was raised without a father, and I will tell you plainly that a boy raised without a father learns early to build his own safety, and the habit does not die at conversion. I have called my tightfistedness stewardship. I have given the round, painless number and felt generous for it. I have lain awake counting a security that was never going to save me from the thing I was actually afraid of. | generalized | Most of us have made that bet. Many of us learned early to build our own safety, and the habit does not die at conversion. We have called our tightfistedness stewardship. We have given the round, painless number and felt generous for it. We have lain awake counting a security that was never going to save us from the thing we were actually afraid of. |
| generosity | `sessions[1].teaching` | To the comfortable giver, and I am often him, it says the gift you were quietly proud of, the one with the impressive number, may have cost you precisely nothing and impressed precisely no one but yourself, because the God who was actually watching read the cost and found it near zero. | generalized | To the comfortable giver, and most of us are often him, it says the gift you were quietly proud of, the one with the impressive number, may have cost you precisely nothing and impressed precisely no one but yourself, because the God who was actually watching read the cost and found it near zero. |
| generosity | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have written the check that looked generous and felt generous and changed nothing about my week, and called it giving, when Jesus would have called it leftovers. | generalized | Many of us have written the check that looked generous and felt generous and changed nothing about our week, and called it giving, when Jesus would have called it leftovers. |
| generosity | `sessions[2].teaching` | That is Paul's baseline for enough, and I do not know a soul in any room I have ever taught who lives within sight of it, and I include myself, standing well past it and still, on my worst nights, counting. | generalized | That is Paul's baseline for enough, and few of us live within sight of it, standing well past it and still, on our worst nights, counting. |
| generosity | `sessions[2].teaching` | Here I have to stand in it, because I know this itch from the inside. A boy raised without a father learns to build his own security young, and I have carried that habit straight through my conversion and into my ministry, and I have called the itch ambition, and I have called the discontent drive, and I have lain awake doing the math on a security that was never going to save me. | generalized | Here we have to stand in it, because most of us know this itch from the inside. Many of us learned to build our own security young and carried the habit straight through conversion, and we have called the itch ambition, and called the discontent drive, and lain awake doing the math on a security that was never going to save us. |
| generosity | `sessions[3].teaching` | But here is the honest thing, and I have to say it about my own house. | generalized | But here is the honest thing, and it has to be said about our own houses. |
| generosity | `sessions[3].teaching` | We have made our homes into places optimized for our own comfort and our own control, and we have given money precisely so that we would never have to give the harder things, and I know this because I have done it, given generously by check and stingily by door, felt generous while making sure my evenings stayed my own. | generalized | We have made our homes into places optimized for our own comfort and our own control, and we have given money precisely so that we would never have to give the harder things, and many of us know this because we have done it, given generously by check and stingily by door, felt generous while making sure our evenings stayed our own. |
| generosity | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to end inside this, not above it, because I began this study as a tightfisted man and I am still, on many days, a tightfisted man, a boy raised without a father who learned to hold on and never fully unlearned it. What loosens the grip in me is never the command and never the guilt. It is this. It is looking again at the one who was rich and became poor for me, and realizing that I have been clutching my planks in front of a man who gave me the whole ocean. | generalized | And we have to end inside this, not above it, because most of us are still, on many days, tightfisted, having learned to hold on and never fully unlearned it. What loosens the grip is never the command and never the guilt. It is this. It is looking again at the one who was rich and became poor for us, and realizing that we have been clutching our planks in front of a man who gave us the whole ocean. |
| generosity | `leaderPrimer` | He is not a wealthy man dispensing wisdom about generosity from a height. He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he will tell you that a boy raised without a father learns to build his own safety and does not easily unlearn it, and that he has called his own tightfistedness stewardship more times than he wants to admit. | generalized | He does not write as the generous one dispensing wisdom about generosity from a height. He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not exempt himself from the grip this study describes. |
| generosity | `faq[8].a` | The writer leads it from inside his own grip, a man who came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and has called his own tightfistedness stewardship more times than he wants to admit. | generalized | The writer leads it from inside the grip it describes, a man who came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and who does not exempt himself. |
| generosity | `faq[11].a` | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and says plainly that a boy raised without a father learns to build his own security and does not easily unlearn it, that he has called his own tightfistedness stewardship and given the painless round number and felt generous doing it. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not exempt himself from the tightfistedness called stewardship or the painless round number given with a feeling of generosity. |
| generosity | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer stands inside all of this, a man who came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and admits he has called his own tightfistedness stewardship more times than he wants to. | cut | Third, the writer stands inside all of this, a man who came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father. |
| generosity | `promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | It handles the hard things plainly, refuses both the prosperity preachers and the guilt merchants, and the writer implicates himself first, a man who admits he has called his own tightfistedness stewardship. | cut | It handles the hard things plainly, refuses both the prosperity preachers and the guilt merchants, and the writer implicates himself first. |

### gratitude (21)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| gratitude | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, spent years assuming the universe owed him and being quietly disappointed it had not paid, was raised without a father, and is now raising five sons at a table he keeps forgetting to see. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the man who counted his gifts as a technique to manage his mood and never once thanked the Giver. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is now raising five sons, and he does not point from a safe distance. Most of us have assumed the universe owed us and been quietly disappointed it had not paid, and many of us have counted our gifts as a technique to manage a mood and never once thanked the Giver. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell sat at a full table on an ordinary Thursday, five sons talking over each other, food he had not gone hungry to earn, and felt nothing, not sadness but the flat blindness of a man who takes the whole thing as owed. He tells it to name the one sin almost no one would call a sin. | generalized | It is possible to sit at a full table on an ordinary Thursday, children talking over each other, food you did not go hungry to earn, and feel nothing, not sadness but the flat blindness of a person who takes the whole thing as owed. Bell names it as the one sin almost no one would call a sin. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell came to faith from atheism, having spent years assuming the universe owed him and being disappointed it had not paid, and the entitled heart is not sad, it is blind, unable to see that the sunrise it did not schedule and the breath it did not earn were given. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and the entitled heart is not sad, it is blind, unable to see that the sunrise it did not schedule and the breath it did not earn were given. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell describes sitting at a full table and feeling nothing, taking the whole thing as owed. | generalized | Bell describes what it is to sit at a full table and feel nothing, taking the whole thing as owed. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he came to faith from atheism assuming the universe owed him and was disappointed it had not paid. | generalized | Many of us assume the universe owes us and are disappointed it has not paid. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell sat at a full table on an ordinary Thursday, five sons talking over each other, food he had not gone hungry to earn, a wife who had cooked it, and he felt nothing. Not sadness. Just the flatness of a man who has stopped seeing what is in front of him, who takes the whole thing as owed. He tells it because he wants to name the one sin most of us would never call a sin, the one that does not look like sin at all, the one that hides so well inside a respectable and comfortable life that we can carry it for decades and never once confess it. | generalized | Picture a full table on an ordinary Thursday, children talking over each other, food no one went hungry to earn, and the person at the head of it feeling nothing. Not sadness. Just the flatness of someone who has stopped seeing what is in front of him, who takes the whole thing as owed. Bell names it because it is the one sin most of us would never call a sin, the one that does not look like sin at all, the one that hides so well inside a respectable and comfortable life that we can carry it for decades and never once confess it. |
| gratitude | `sessions[0].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because the diagnosis names the writer before the reader. I came to faith from atheism, and for most of my early life I ran on an assumption I never examined, that the universe was in my debt, that a decent life was the baseline I was entitled to, and I lived in a low and constant disappointment that the payment kept coming up short. I kept an invoice against a God I did not even believe in, which tells you how deep the entitlement runs, that a man can bill a landlord he denies exists. And I have not left it behind as cleanly as I would like. The blindness returns at the full table, on the ordinary Thursday, in the health I assume and the people I take for granted, every time I receive the gift as though it were a wage. This is why the objection about mood misses the whole weight of it. The problem was never that I did not feel cheerful. The problem was that I could not see, that I stood inside a life dense with gifts and called the whole thing mine by right. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because the diagnosis names the writer before the reader. Most of us run on an assumption we never examine, that the universe is in our debt, that a decent life is the baseline we are entitled to, and we live in a low and constant disappointment that the payment keeps coming up short. People keep an invoice even against a God they do not believe in, which tells you how deep the entitlement runs, that a person can bill a landlord he denies exists. And none of us leaves it behind as cleanly as we would like. The blindness returns at the full table, on the ordinary Thursday, in the health we assume and the people we take for granted, every time we receive the gift as though it were a wage. This is why the objection about mood misses the whole weight of it. The problem was never that we did not feel cheerful. The problem was that we could not see, that we stood inside a life dense with gifts and called the whole thing ours by right. |
| gratitude | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell has counted his gifts as a technique and never thanked the Giver, and the counting without the Giver is a ledger, not a Eucharist. | generalized | Many of us have counted our gifts as a technique and never thanked the Giver, and the counting without the Giver is a ledger, not a Eucharist. |
| gratitude | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has counted his gifts as a technique and never thanked the Giver, and calls the counting without the Giver a ledger, not a Eucharist. | generalized | It is possible to count our gifts as a technique and never thank the Giver, and Bell calls the counting without the Giver a ledger, not a Eucharist. |
| gratitude | `sessions[1].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because I know the ledger better than I want to admit. I have kept the list. I have counted the gifts, dutifully, the way you would take a supplement, hoping it would lift the gray, and I have gotten to the bottom of the page having thanked no one at all, having performed gratitude at my own feelings and called it worship. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because most of us know the ledger better than we want to admit. We keep the list. We count the gifts, dutifully, the way you would take a supplement, hoping it will lift the gray, and we get to the bottom of the page having thanked no one at all, having performed gratitude at our own feelings and called it worship. |
| gratitude | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell has been the nine, cured and walking away, and he has been the man who confused thanking God in the loss with the lie of thanking him for it. | generalized | Most of us have been the nine, cured and walking away, and many have confused thanking God in the loss with the lie of thanking him for it. |
| gratitude | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has been both the nine who walked away cured and the man who confused thanking God in a loss with the lie of thanking him for it. | generalized | Most of us have been both the nine who walked away cured and the one who confused thanking God in a loss with the lie of thanking him for it. |
| gratitude | `sessions[2].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because I have failed at both ends. I have been the nine, cured and hurrying, receiving a hundred mercies a day and walking on without a backward glance, too comfortable to remember I was carried. And I have been the man who got the preposition wrong in the other direction, who in a hard season either could not thank God at all because I thought I was being asked to thank him for the hardness, or who faked a grotesque gratitude for a thing that was simply evil and called the faking faith. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because most of us have failed at both ends. We have been the nine, cured and hurrying, receiving a hundred mercies a day and walking on without a backward glance, too comfortable to remember we were carried. And we have gotten the preposition wrong in the other direction, in a hard season either unable to thank God at all because we thought we were being asked to thank him for the hardness, or faking a grotesque gratitude for a thing that was simply evil and calling the faking faith. |
| gratitude | `sessions[3].summary` | Bell has known grief that thanks did not erase, and he refuses to hand anyone a gratitude that skips their sorrow. | generalized | There is grief that thanks does not erase, and Bell refuses to hand anyone a gratitude that skips their sorrow. |
| gratitude | `sessions[3].teaching` | Here is where I stand inside it. I have known grief that gratitude did not erase and was never meant to, and I will not stand in front of a room and hand anyone a thanks that skips their sorrow. | generalized | Here is where we stand inside it. There is grief that gratitude does not erase and was never meant to, and I will not stand in front of a room and hand anyone a thanks that skips their sorrow. |
| gratitude | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell has run the practices as a technique and felt them go hollow, and the recovery is not a better method but a returned sight, the trained attention that receives all of it, the bread and the breath and the hard bread too, as gift from a Giver, and hands it back in thanks. | generalized | Many of us have run the practices as a technique and felt them go hollow, and the recovery is not a better method but a returned sight, the trained attention that receives all of it, the bread and the breath and the hard bread too, as gift from a Giver, and hands it back in thanks. |
| gratitude | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has run the practices as a technique and felt them go hollow, and says the recovery is not a better method but a returned sight. | generalized | Many of us have run the practices as a technique and felt them go hollow, and Bell says the recovery is not a better method but a returned sight. |
| gratitude | `sessions[4].teaching` | Here is where I have to end inside it, because I have run these practices as a technique and felt them go hollow in my hands. I have counted gifts the way you take a supplement, aimed at my own gray mood, and gotten to the bottom of the list having seen a hundred things and worshiped no one, and wondered why it felt like nothing. The problem was never the practice. The problem was where I had aimed it. And the recovery, when it came, was not a better method, not a more efficient journal or a cleverer habit. It was a returned sight. It was the slow retraining of my eyes, through the same old practices finally aimed at the right Person, to receive the whole of a life, the bread and the breath and the five loud sons and the health I assumed and the hard bread too, the losses and the grief and the seasons that did not pay, all of it, as gift from a Giver, and to hand it back to him in thanks. That is where this study leaves you, and it does not tie a bow on it, because the sight is not finished in five weeks and mine is not finished now. | generalized | Here is where we have to end inside it, because many of us have run these practices as a technique and felt them go hollow in our hands. We count gifts the way you take a supplement, aimed at our own gray mood, and get to the bottom of the list having seen a hundred things and worshiped no one, and wonder why it felt like nothing. The problem was never the practice. The problem was where we had aimed it. And the recovery, when it comes, is not a better method, not a more efficient journal or a cleverer habit. It is a returned sight. It is the slow retraining of our eyes, through the same old practices finally aimed at the right Person, to receive the whole of a life, the bread and the breath and the people around our table and the health we assumed and the hard bread too, the losses and the grief and the seasons that did not pay, all of it, as gift from a Giver, and to hand it back to him in thanks. That is where this study leaves you, and it does not tie a bow on it, because the sight is not finished in five weeks and no one's is finished now. |
| gratitude | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, spent years billing a universe he assumed owed him, was raised without a father, and still forgets to see the full table in front of him. He has kept the gratitude ledger with the Giver edited out. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not exempt himself from the blindness this study names. |
| gratitude | `faq[8].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, spent years assuming the universe owed him, was raised without a father, and still sits at a full table and forgets to see it. He has kept the gratitude ledger with the Giver edited out. He does not stand above the ingratitude he describes, he stands inside it, and the study asks every leader and every participant to do the same. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father. He does not stand above the ingratitude he describes, he stands inside it, and the study asks every leader and every participant to do the same. |
| gratitude | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer puts himself inside everything he says, a man who came from atheism assuming the world owed him and who still forgets to see the full table in front of him, and so will we, as fellow beggars learning to see, not experts dispensing cheer. | cut | Third, the writer puts himself inside everything he says, a man who came to faith from atheism, and so will we, as fellow beggars learning to see, not experts dispensing cheer. |

### grief (17)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| grief | `summary` | He has stood at gravesides and said the right words and driven home undone. He has prayed for people whose prayers were not answered the way they begged. He writes from inside the loss. | generalized | Pastors stand at gravesides and say the right words and drive home undone, and pray for people whose prayers are not answered the way they begged. He writes from inside that ministry, not above it. |
| grief | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after a funeral that he had not cried, that he felt he should not, that a Christian who really believed in heaven would not grieve like the people who have no hope. He was not stronger for the dry eyes.
