He was carrying something with no place to put it, and somebody had taught him that the carrying was unbelief. | generalized | Many believers leave a funeral dry-eyed and a little proud of it, convinced that a Christian who really believed in heaven would not grieve like the people who have no hope. They are not stronger for the dry eyes. They are carrying something with no place to put it, and somebody taught them that the carrying was unbelief. |
| grief | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has stood at a graveside, said all the right words, and driven home undone. | generalized | Pastors and mourners alike have stood at a graveside, said all the right words, and driven home undone. |
| grief | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a funeral. He told Bell, almost proud of it, that he had not cried, that he did not feel he should, that surely a Christian who really believed in heaven would not fall apart like the people who have no hope. He thought the dry eyes were strength. They were not. He was carrying something heavy with nowhere to set it down, and somewhere along the way someone had taught him that the carrying itself was a kind of unbelief. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure at a funeral, the believer who has not cried and is almost proud of it, who does not feel he should, who is sure a Christian who really believed in heaven would not fall apart like the people who have no hope. He thinks the dry eyes are strength. They are not. He is carrying something heavy with nowhere to set it down, and somewhere along the way someone taught him that the carrying itself was a kind of unbelief. |
| grief | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because I have failed at this from both directions. I have stood at gravesides and said all the right resurrection words and driven home in the dark completely undone, performing a peace at the graveside that I did not feel and would not let myself feel until the car door shut. | generalized | And here we have to stand inside it, because most of us have failed at this from both directions. Pastors stand at gravesides and say all the right resurrection words and drive home in the dark completely undone, performing a peace at the graveside they do not feel and will not let themselves feel until the car door shuts. |
| grief | `sessions[1].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits his own prayers in grief have been silent when they should have been loud, that he went quiet out of a false reverence. | generalized | Many of us have gone silent in grief when we should have been loud, quiet out of a false reverence. |
| grief | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess that I am bad at this, that for all my preaching I have gone silent toward God in my own grief out of a false and cowardly reverence, swallowing the why because it felt safer than saying it, as though God did not already know it was in there. I called the swallowing trust. It was fear. | generalized | And most of us are bad at this, going silent toward God in our grief out of a false and cowardly reverence, swallowing the why because it feels safer than saying it, as though God did not already know it was in there. We call the swallowing trust. It is fear. |
| grief | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has stood at gravesides where the sting felt very present. | generalized | Anyone who has stood at a graveside knows the sting can feel very present. |
| grief | `sessions[2].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits the gap between the doctrine and the graveside. | generalized | The study admits the gap between the doctrine and the graveside. |
| grief | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to be honest that I have stood at gravesides where this felt like a contradiction more than a comfort, where I preached death defeated over a small casket and felt the sting as present and sharp as anything I have ever felt, where the doctrine and the dirt did not seem to fit in the same hand. | generalized | And many who have stood at gravesides know this can feel like a contradiction more than a comfort, death preached as defeated over a small casket while the sting feels as present and sharp as anything a person has ever felt, the doctrine and the dirt not seeming to fit in the same hand. |
| grief | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has spoken resurrection hope over a casket and felt the goodbye as final anyway. | generalized | Pastors speak resurrection hope over a casket and can still feel the goodbye as final. |
| grief | `sessions[3].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits the gap between believing and feeling. | generalized | The study admits the gap between believing and feeling. |
| grief | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to tell you where this lives for me, because I have stood over a casket and spoken these very words, that Jesus died and rose and so will raise our friend, and I have meant every word, and I have still felt the goodbye land as final and absolute as a door closing, the hope in my mouth and the loss in my chest, both completely true at the same moment. That gap, between what I believe and what I feel, is where the grieving Christian lives, and I will not pretend it away. | generalized | And this is where it lives for many of us. A person can stand over a casket and speak these very words, that Jesus died and rose and so will raise our friend, and mean every word, and still feel the goodbye land as final and absolute as a door closing, the hope in the mouth and the loss in the chest, both completely true at the same moment. That gap, between what we believe and what we feel, is where the grieving Christian lives, and I will not pretend it away. |
| grief | `sessions[4].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits the comfort flowing through him has often surprised him rather than being planned. | generalized | The comfort that flows through a grieving person to others usually arrives by surprise rather than by plan. |
| grief | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to be honest that when this has happened in my own life it has never once been planned. I did not set out to turn my losses into a ministry. The comfort simply arrived in someone else through me when I was not trying, and it surprised me every time, and it felt less like an achievement and more like watching water find its way downhill, the thing I had been given quietly making its way to the next person in the dark. | generalized | And when this happens it is almost never planned. No one should set out to turn their losses into a ministry. The comfort simply arrives in someone else through us when we are not trying, and it surprises us every time, and it feels less like an achievement and more like watching water find its way downhill, the thing we were given quietly making its way to the next person in the dark. |
| grief | `leaderPrimer` | Bell has stood at gravesides saying true resurrection words and driven home undone, and he does not resolve these sessions. | generalized | Bell does not pretend true resurrection words undo the grave's sting, and he does not resolve these sessions. |
| grief | `faq[10].a` | He has stood at gravesides and said the right resurrection words and driven home undone. He has prayed over people whose prayers were not answered the way they begged. He has performed a peace at a funeral that he did not feel until the car door shut. He grieves from inside the loss, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot comfort a grieving person from above without your comfort curdling into either a platitude or a distance. | generalized | Pastors stand at gravesides and say the right resurrection words and drive home undone, pray over people whose prayers are not answered the way they begged, and perform a peace at a funeral they do not feel until the car door shuts. He writes from inside that ministry, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot comfort a grieving person from above without your comfort curdling into either a platitude or a distance. |

### habits-and-formation (20)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| habits-and-formation | `summary` | Bell came to faith out of atheism, argued his way in book by book, and assumed for years that he would be sanctified the same way he was converted, by getting his thinking straight, and he was not, and the gap between the theology he could pass a test on and the man he actually was is where this whole study was born. He writes from inside the failure. He is a pastor with a phone in his pocket that has formed him more than he wants to admit, a father of five sons who are learning what they love by watching what their father repeats. | generalized | Bell came to faith out of atheism, and this study lives in a gap most believers know, between the theology we could pass a test on and the people we actually are, because many of us assume we will be sanctified by getting our thinking straight, and we are not. He writes from inside that gap. He is a pastor and a father of five sons, and children learn what they love by watching what their parents repeat. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell spent his first years as a Christian assuming he would be sanctified the way he was converted, by thinking. He had argued his way in out of atheism, and it was natural to believe that if he stacked enough correct belief high enough he would come out holy. He could pass any doctrine test you handed him. He could not stop losing his temper with the people he loved, could not put down the habits he despised, could not become by thinking the man he could clearly describe. | generalized | Many Christians assume they will be sanctified by thinking, that if they stack enough correct belief high enough they will come out holy. They can pass any doctrine test you hand them. They cannot stop losing their temper with the people they love, cannot put down the habits they despise, cannot become by thinking the people they can clearly describe. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he assumed he would be sanctified the way he was converted, by thinking, and it did not work. | generalized | Bell says many of us assume we will be sanctified by thinking, and it does not work. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that his five sons are learning what to love by watching what their father repeats, not by hearing what he believes. | generalized | Children learn what to love by watching what their parents repeat, not by hearing what they believe. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell begins with a confession about his own head. For years after he came to faith out of atheism, he assumed that becoming a Christian was mostly a matter of getting his thinking straightened out. He had argued his way in, book by book, objection by objection, and it was natural to assume he would be sanctified the way he was converted, by acquiring correct beliefs and stacking them high enough to stand on. He could pass any doctrine test you put in front of him. And he could not stop losing his temper with the people he loved most, could not put down the habits he privately despised, could not become, by thinking, the man he could so clearly describe. That gap is where this whole study lives, and it is not his alone. | generalized | Begin with a confession many believers could make about their own heads. We assume that becoming a Christian is mostly a matter of getting our thinking straightened out, that we will be sanctified by acquiring correct beliefs and stacking them high enough to stand on. We can pass any doctrine test put in front of us. And we cannot stop losing our temper with the people we love most, cannot put down the habits we privately despise, cannot become, by thinking, the people we can so clearly describe. That gap is where this whole study lives, and it belongs to all of us. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].teaching` | Smith opens his book You Are What You Love with a question that took the floor out from under Bell, whether we might be shaped more by what we love than by what we know, whether a human being is at bottom not a thinking thing but a desiring one. | generalized | Smith opens his book You Are What You Love with a question that takes the floor out from under the head-first Christian, whether we might be shaped more by what we love than by what we know, whether a human being is at bottom not a thinking thing but a desiring one. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[0].teaching` | So here is where I have to stand inside it, because the writer is the first exhibit. My problem was never that I did not know enough. It was that my loves had been trained, for thirty years before I believed anything, by a thousand repetitions I never examined, and belief alone did not retrain them. | generalized | So here is where we have to stand inside it, writer and reader alike. Our problem was never that we did not know enough. It was that our loves had been trained, for years before we believed anything, by a thousand repetitions we never examined, and belief alone did not retrain them. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell picks up his phone the moment he wakes, before he prays, before he speaks to his wife, and he did not decide to, which is exactly the point. He calls it a liturgy, and he means the word precisely, a repeated bodily practice that trains a person to want something. | generalized | Most of us pick up the phone the moment we wake, before we pray, before we speak to anyone we love, and we did not decide to, which is exactly the point. Bell calls it a liturgy, and he means the word precisely, a repeated bodily practice that trains a person to want something. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[1].discussion[0].q` | Bell reaches for his phone before he prays or speaks to his wife, and says he did not decide to. | generalized | Many of us reach for the phone before we pray or speak to anyone, and we did not decide to. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[1].teaching` | Bell reaches for his phone the moment he wakes, before he prays, before he speaks to his wife, before his feet find the floor, and he did not decide to. That is the whole point, and it is worth sitting in the discomfort of it. He calls the phone a liturgy, and he means the word with precision, not loosely. | generalized | Most of us reach for the phone the moment we wake, before we pray, before we speak to anyone we love, before our feet find the floor, and we did not decide to. That is the whole point, and it is worth sitting in the discomfort of it. Bell calls the phone a liturgy, and he means the word with precision, not loosely. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[1].teaching` | And here I have to climb inside the indictment, because I am not describing other people. I am describing the pastor who tells his congregation to seek first the kingdom and then lies in the dark scrolling until his eyes burn, who has felt his own capacity for stillness and patience and undistracted presence erode year by year, who has watched his sons learn from him that the proper response to a moment of boredom or discomfort is to reach for the anesthetic in your pocket. | generalized | And here we have to climb inside the indictment, because this is not describing other people. It describes the pastor who tells his congregation to seek first the kingdom and then lies in the dark scrolling until his eyes burn, and the parent who feels a capacity for stillness and patience and undistracted presence erode year by year and watches the children learn that the proper response to a moment of boredom or discomfort is to reach for the anesthetic in your pocket. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[2].teaching` | Now the caution the whole session has been building toward, and I put myself first inside it, because I practice all of this badly and inconsistently and I am the last man who should sound like an expert. | generalized | Now the caution the whole session has been building toward, and I put myself first inside it, because none of us practices this well enough to sound like an expert. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[3].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because I have run to both dodges to avoid the actual work. I have hidden my laziness behind grace, telling myself that resting in God's finished work meant I did not need to train, when really I just did not want to. And I have hidden my pride behind effort, practicing hard and then measuring, ranking, feeling secretly that my discipline had bought me something, which is earning wearing the mask of devotion. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because most of us have run to both dodges to avoid the actual work. We have hidden our laziness behind grace, telling ourselves that resting in God's finished work meant we did not need to train, when really we just did not want to. And we have hidden our pride behind effort, practicing hard and then measuring, ranking, feeling secretly that our discipline had bought us something, which is earning wearing the mask of devotion. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell has watched it a hundred times, the person moved by a teaching on the disciplines who goes home and builds a program fit for a monastery, an hour of prayer, a day of fasting, a stack of reading, keeps it for eleven days, collapses, and concludes that the whole thing is not for them. | generalized | It happens again and again, the person moved by a teaching on the disciplines who goes home and builds a program fit for a monastery, an hour of prayer, a day of fasting, a stack of reading, keeps it for eleven days, collapses, and concludes that the whole thing is not for them. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[4].teaching` | Bell has watched the failure play out a hundred times, and it always looks the same. | generalized | The failure plays out again and again, and it always looks the same. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[4].teaching` | And Bell wants to say this to every one of those people, because he has been one of them. | cut | And Bell wants to say this to every one of those people. |
| habits-and-formation | `sessions[4].teaching` | I have to say this last part as a man still learning it, because I am the one who keeps building the monastery and keeps watching it fall, who has more than once mistaken the intensity of my intentions for the strength of my practice and been humbled by a Tuesday. | generalized | I have to say this last part as one still learning it, because most of us keep building the monastery and watching it fall, mistaking the intensity of our intentions for the strength of our practice until a Tuesday humbles us. |
| habits-and-formation | `leaderPrimer` | He is a pastor who reaches for his phone before he prays, who keeps building monasteries and watching them fall, who is still, at this age, a beginner at the very things he is teaching. | generalized | He writes as a pastor who is still a beginner at the very things he is teaching. |
| habits-and-formation | `faq[8].a` | He reaches for his phone before he prays. He keeps building monastic programs and watching them collapse by the eleventh day. He came to faith from atheism assuming he would be sanctified by thinking and discovered, slowly and humbly, that he was not. He teaches this as a beginner who is still being trained more than he is training, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot call people to be formed from a posture that pretends you already are. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he teaches this as a beginner who is still being trained more than he is training, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot call people to be formed from a posture that pretends you already are. |
| habits-and-formation | `facilitatorScript.open` | Second, this is not a room where the disciplined look down on the struggling, because the writer struggles more than most, and we will keep it a place where you can admit a failure without it becoming a verdict. | generalized | Second, this is not a room where the disciplined look down on the struggling, because the writer does not stand above the struggle, and we will keep it a place where you can admit a failure without it becoming a verdict. |

### heaven-and-hell (24)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| heaven-and-hell | `summary` | James Bell came to faith out of atheism, and the doctrine of hell was the thing he held hardest against God, so he does not pretend it is comfortable now. He takes the objection at full weight because he once made it himself. And he does not stand outside any of this. He was raised without a father and has buried people he loved, and he has felt the particular ache of grieving a presence he barely had. He has wanted to flee a world that breaks his heart, and he has wanted to give up the horizon and call it wisdom. | cut | James Bell came to faith out of atheism, and he does not pretend the doctrine of hell is comfortable. He takes the objection at full weight. And he does not stand outside any of this. He was raised without a father. Most of us have wanted to flee a world that breaks our hearts, or to give up the horizon and call it wisdom. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[0].objections[0].response` | Bell grants more of this than you expect, because he made the argument himself as an unbeliever. | cut | Bell grants more of this than you expect, as a man who came to faith from atheism. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[0].teaching` | I have stood at too many gravesides and watched people comfort each other with a hope smaller than the one they were promised. She is an angel now. He is looking down on us. At peace, free of that broken body at last. And I have wanted to say, gently, that the Christian hope is better than that, and harder, and more solid. | generalized | Many of us have stood at gravesides and watched people comfort each other with a hope smaller than the one they were promised. She is an angel now. He is looking down on us. At peace, free of that broken body at last. And it needs to be said, gently, that the Christian hope is better than that, and harder, and more solid. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he spent years preaching a gospel of evacuation, getting souls to a safe place when they die. | generalized | Many pulpits have preached a gospel of evacuation, getting souls to a safe place when they die. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have to implicate myself here, because I spent years preaching a gospel that was really just about getting people's souls to a safe place when they died, a gospel of evacuation. | generalized | We have to implicate ourselves here, because many of us have preached or believed a gospel that was really just about getting people's souls to a safe place when they died, a gospel of evacuation. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[1].teaching` | I shrank the gospel to fit a funeral, and the real thing is the size of the universe. | generalized | We shrank the gospel to fit a funeral, and the real thing is the size of the universe. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].summary` | Hell is the hardest doctrine in the faith, and as an atheist it was the single thing he held hardest against God, the proof, as he saw it then, that the God of the Bible was a moral monster. | generalized | Hell is the hardest doctrine in the faith, the doctrine many skeptics hold hardest against God, the proof, as they see it, that the God of the Bible is a moral monster. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].summary` | What changed for him was not solving it but meeting the One who warned of the cliff and then went over it himself, into the dark, so that we would not have to. | generalized | What changes things is not solving it but meeting the One who warned of the cliff and then went over it himself, into the dark, so that we would not have to. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].discussion[0].q` | Bell, a former atheist, says hell was the single thing he held hardest against God. | generalized | Many skeptics say hell is the single thing they hold hardest against God. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].objections[0].response` | Bell takes this at full weight because he made it himself as an atheist, and he will not insult you by shrinking it. | cut | Bell takes this at full weight, as a man who came to faith from atheism, and he will not insult you by shrinking it. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].teaching` | It was, when I was an atheist, the single thing I held against the whole religion, the proof, as I saw it, that the God of the Bible was a moral monster, and I will not pretend now that I have made it comfortable. | generalized | It is, for many skeptics, the single thing they hold against the whole religion, the proof, as they see it, that the God of the Bible is a moral monster, and I will not pretend that I have made it comfortable. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].teaching` | It is to refuse both flinching and gloating, to take the question as seriously as my younger self took it, and to lay the historic options in front of you at their full strength, so that you decide before God and not before a caricature. | generalized | It is to refuse both flinching and gloating, to take the question as seriously as the skeptic takes it, and to lay the historic options in front of you at their full strength, so that you decide before God and not before a caricature. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].teaching` | I felt the full weight of that as an unbeliever, and I will not insult you by pretending it is a small thing. | generalized | Skeptics feel the full weight of that, and I will not insult you by pretending it is a small thing. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[2].teaching` | I came to faith as a man who hated this doctrine. I will tell you what changed. Not that I solved it. I did not. What changed is that I met the One who spoke of it, and saw that he spoke of it the way a man speaks of a cliff to a child he loves, and then walked toward the cliff himself and went over it, into the dark, so that I would not have to. | generalized | Many come to faith hating this doctrine. Here is what changes. Not that anyone solves it. What changes is meeting the One who spoke of it, and seeing that he spoke of it the way a man speaks of a cliff to a child he loves, and then walked toward the cliff himself and went over it, into the dark, so that we would not have to. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[3].teaching` | I have buried people I loved. I was raised without a father, and I have felt the particular ache of grieving a presence I barely had, and I will not stand here and tell you that the doctrine of the intermediate state makes the grief go away. | cut | I was raised without a father, and I will not stand here and tell you that the doctrine of the intermediate state makes the grief go away. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell, who has lived enough of the long defeat to be tempted by both wrong answers, finds the true hope rebuking both. | generalized | Bell finds the true hope rebuking both, and it rebukes all of us who are tempted by either. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Bell will not pretend it removes the pain, because it does not, and he has lived enough of the long defeat to know better than to offer you that. | cut | Bell will not pretend it removes the pain, because it does not. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[4].teaching` | Tolkien had a phrase for this that has carried me through years I could not see the end of. | generalized | Tolkien had a phrase for this that has carried many believers through years they could not see the end of. |
| heaven-and-hell | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to put myself inside this last, because I am the man who has lived enough of the long defeat to be tempted by both wrong answers. I have wanted to flee, to stop caring about a world that breaks my heart, to pull my five sons close and let the rest of it go dark. And I have wanted, in my tired and faithless hours, to give up the horizon altogether and just make things a little nicer and call it gospel. The true hope rebukes both my temptations. It will not let me flee, because the world is going to be healed and I am called to plant the first seeds of it now. And it will not let me shrink, because there is a morning coming that is larger than every defeat I will suffer between now and the grave. | generalized | And we have to put ourselves inside this last, because most of us have lived enough of the long defeat to be tempted by both wrong answers. We have wanted to flee, to stop caring about a world that breaks our hearts, to pull our families close and let the rest of it go dark. And we have wanted, in our tired and faithless hours, to give up the horizon altogether and just make things a little nicer and call it gospel. The true hope rebukes both temptations. It will not let us flee, because the world is going to be healed and we are called to plant the first seeds of it now. And it will not let us shrink, because there is a morning coming that is larger than every defeat we will suffer between now and the grave. |
| heaven-and-hell | `leaderPrimer` | Bell held hell against God for years before he believed anything at all. It was the wall his atheism was built into, and he does not pretend the wall was stupid. When the skeptic in your room raises it, they are making his old argument, so let it be made at full strength and honor it before any answer. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he does not pretend the objection is stupid. When the skeptic in your room raises it, let it be made at full strength and honor it before any answer. |
| heaven-and-hell | `leaderPrimer` | Bell has buried people he loved and grieved a father he barely had, and he writes toward the morning without pretending the night is short. | cut | Bell was raised without a father, and he writes toward the morning without pretending the night is short. |
| heaven-and-hell | `faq[4].a` | Then you are in good company, because James Bell came to faith out of atheism and hell was the single thing he held hardest against God, and he will not pretend now that he has made it easy. | generalized | Then you are in good company, because many skeptics hold hell hardest against God, and James Bell, who came to faith out of atheism, will not pretend he has made it easy. |
| heaven-and-hell | `faq[6].a` | Bell, who was raised without a father and has buried people he loved, will not pretend the doctrine removes the pain. | cut | Bell, who was raised without a father, will not pretend the doctrine removes the pain. |
| heaven-and-hell | `facilitatorScript.open` | He came to faith out of atheism, and hell was the thing he held hardest against God. He was raised without a father and has buried people he loved. | cut | He came to faith out of atheism, and he does not pretend hell is an easy doctrine. He was raised without a father. |

### holy-spirit (24)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| holy-spirit | `summary` | The new birth, regeneration, the Spirit who raises the spiritually dead, including the writer, who was an atheist until the Spirit did the one thing argument never could. | cut | The new birth, regeneration, the Spirit who raises the spiritually dead and does the one thing argument never could. |
| holy-spirit | `summary` | He spent years certain there was no God and no soul and no Spirit, and the thing that finally undid him was not a better argument. It was a presence he had no category for. He writes from inside the new birth he did not author and cannot explain. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he writes from inside a new birth he did not author and cannot fully explain. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he can preach the Trinity and then pray and live as though the Spirit were not in the room. | generalized | Many of us can confess the Trinity and then pray and live as though the Spirit were not in the room. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I can preach the Trinity on a Sunday, all three persons, fully God, and then pray that same afternoon as though I were shouting up at a distant sky, and live the whole week as though the house were empty, as though the God who raised Jesus from the dead were not at that moment dwelling in me. The forgetting is not only theirs. It is mine. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because many of us can confess the Trinity on a Sunday, all three persons, fully God, and then pray that same afternoon as though we were shouting up at a distant sky, and live the whole week as though the house were empty, as though the God who raised Jesus from the dead were not at that moment dwelling in us. The forgetting is not only theirs. It is ours. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].aim` | To take the new birth as the Spirit's work and not ours, to see regeneration as God raising the spiritually dead rather than coaching the spiritually struggling, and to let the writer's own conversion from atheism stand as evidence that the Spirit does what argument never could. | cut | To take the new birth as the Spirit's work and not ours, to see regeneration as God raising the spiritually dead rather than coaching the spiritually struggling, and to see that the Spirit does what argument never could. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell stands inside this with his own story, because he was an atheist, certain, intelligent, unmoved by the arguments he had heard and answered. What changed him was not a better syllogism. He was made alive by a presence he had no category for, and he knows, all the way down, that he did not author it. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and the point holds for every convert. The new birth is not produced by a better syllogism. The dead are made alive by the Spirit, and no one who has been raised authored it. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].discussion[3].q` | Bell was an atheist who had heard the arguments and answered them, and what finally changed him was not a better argument but a presence. | generalized | Bell says the new birth is not finally the product of a better argument but of a presence, the Spirit who makes the dead alive. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].teaching` | And here I have to put my own story on the table, because this is not abstract for me. I was an atheist. Not a casual one, a convinced one. I had heard the arguments for God and I had answers for them, and the answers were not stupid, and the people making the arguments rarely moved me, because you do not argue a dead man into breathing. I was not waiting for better evidence. I was, in the language of Scripture, dead in my trespasses, and a dead man does not know he is dead, and cannot want to be otherwise. What changed me was not a syllogism. It was not a debate I lost. It was a presence I had no category for, a wind that began to blow on a man who had not asked for it and could not have produced it, and when I came to faith I knew, all the way down in a place arguments do not reach, that I had not done this. I had been done unto. I was raised. I still cannot fully explain it, and the inability to explain it is itself part of the evidence, because you cannot explain a wind you did not call. | generalized | And this is not abstract. A convinced skeptic can hear every argument for God and have answers for them, and the answers are not stupid, and the people making the arguments rarely move him, because you do not argue a dead man into breathing. He is not waiting for better evidence. He is, in the language of Scripture, dead in his trespasses, and a dead man does not know he is dead, and cannot want to be otherwise. What changes such a person is not a syllogism. It is not a debate lost. It is the Spirit, a wind that begins to blow on a man who has not asked for it and could not have produced it, and those who have been raised know, all the way down in a place arguments do not reach, that they did not do it. They were done unto. They were raised. No one can fully explain it, and the inability to explain it is itself part of the evidence, because you cannot explain a wind you did not call. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].teaching` | They are raised, suddenly, by a power outside themselves, the way I was raised, the way you were raised if you have been. | generalized | They are raised, suddenly, by a power outside themselves, the way every believer has been raised. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[1].goingDeeper` | Weigh as well the pastoral stakes, because how you answer shapes how you evangelize and how you pray, and consider why the writer, an atheist made alive, found in his own experience a stronger witness to the priority of grace than any argument supplied, since he knows he did not begin the thing that saved him. | generalized | Weigh as well the pastoral stakes, because how you answer shapes how you evangelize and how you pray, and consider why converts from unbelief so often find in their own experience a stronger witness to the priority of grace than any argument supplies, since no one begins the thing that saved him. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell names his own decades, the sins he assumed grace would have removed by now and has not, and tells the truth the church hides, that sanctification is slow, that the impatience to be done with it is itself a refusal to walk. | generalized | Bell tells the truth the church hides, that many believers carry sins they assumed grace would have removed by now, that sanctification is slow, that the impatience to be done with it is itself a refusal to walk. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[2].discussion[3].q` | Bell admits there are sins he assumed grace would have removed by now and has not. | generalized | Many believers carry sins they assumed grace would have removed by now. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here is where I have to tell the truth the church works hard to hide, because I am decades into this now and there are sins in me I genuinely assumed grace would have removed by this point, and it has not, not yet, not all the way. There are patterns I have brought to God a thousand times that are smaller than they were and not gone. And the temptation, when the change is that slow, is one of two things, either to give up and conclude the whole thing does not work, or to start faking it, to perform the patience I do not have and hang plastic fruit on a branch that is not bearing, so that at least it looks alive. | generalized | And here is where we have to tell the truth the church works hard to hide, because many believers are decades into this and still carry sins they genuinely assumed grace would have removed by now, and it has not, not yet, not all the way. There are patterns brought to God a thousand times that are smaller than they were and not gone. And the temptation, when the change is that slow, is one of two things, either to give up and conclude the whole thing does not work, or to start faking it, to perform a patience we do not have and hang plastic fruit on a branch that is not bearing, so that at least it looks alive. |
| holy-spirit | `sessions[3].teaching` | It runs straight through the middle of the godly, and some of the most careful, Christ-loving, Bible-saturated believers I know land on opposite sides of it, and a study that pretended otherwise would be lying to you about the body of Christ. | generalized | It runs straight through the middle of the godly, and some of the most careful, Christ-loving, Bible-saturated believers land on opposite sides of it, and a study that pretended otherwise would be lying to you about the body of Christ. |
| holy-spirit | `leaderPrimer` | The writer of these essays came out of atheism into a faith he could not have argued himself into, and what he found waiting was not a force or an influence but a person, the one Jesus promised would not leave us as orphans (John 14:18), and a man raised without a father does not use the word orphan carelessly. | cut | The writer of these essays came out of atheism into faith, and the Spirit is not a force or an influence but a person, the one Jesus promised would not leave us as orphans (John 14:18). |
| holy-spirit | `faq[2].a` | The writer, an atheist made alive, found in his own experience a strong witness to the priority of grace, because he knows he did not begin the thing that saved him, and yet he truly came. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and the priority of grace means no convert began the thing that saved him, and yet each one truly came. |
| holy-spirit | `faq[3].a` | Because it is the clearest evidence he has for what the second session claims, that the new birth is the Spirit's work and not the sinner's achievement. Bell was not a casual skeptic but a convinced atheist who had heard the arguments for God and had answers for them, and what finally changed him was not a syllogism he lost but a presence he had no category for, a wind that blew on a man who had not asked for it and could not have produced it. | cut | Because it illustrates what the second session claims, that the new birth is the Spirit's work and not the sinner's achievement. Bell came to faith from atheism, and no one who comes from unbelief can take credit for the wind that raised him. |
| holy-spirit | `faq[5].a` | The writer admits there are sins in him he assumed grace would have removed by now and has not, and names the two temptations the slowness produces, to give up and conclude the gospel does not work, or to fake it and hang plastic fruit on a branch that is not bearing. | generalized | The study admits that many believers carry sins they assumed grace would have removed by now, and names the two temptations the slowness produces, to give up and conclude the gospel does not work, or to fake it and hang plastic fruit on a branch that is not bearing. |
| holy-spirit | `faq[10].a` | He admits he can preach the Trinity and then pray as though shouting at an empty sky, that he has lived whole weeks as though the indwelling God were not in the room, and that he came to faith from atheism by a wind he did not summon and cannot fully explain. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the forgetting this study names, the Trinity confessed on Sunday and the indwelling God ignored all week. |
| holy-spirit | `faq[11].a` | It grounds the personhood and deity of the Spirit in specific claims that can be examined, that he can be lied to and grieved and resisted, things you can only do to a someone, and it tells the story of a convinced atheist who was not argued into faith but changed by a presence he had no category for and did not want. You can weigh that story and find it insufficient, and the study does not pretend it is a proof. | generalized | It grounds the personhood and deity of the Spirit in specific claims that can be examined, that he can be lied to and grieved and resisted, things you can only do to a someone, and it describes a new birth that no argument produces, the work of a presence the unbeliever has no category for. You can weigh that claim and find it insufficient, and the study does not pretend it is a proof. |
| holy-spirit | `facilitatorScript.open` | He came to faith from atheism by a presence he could not explain, and he admits he can know the doctrine and still live as though God were far off. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from knowing the doctrine and still living as though God were far off. |
| holy-spirit | `cadences[0].plan[2]` | Week 3: Session 2, Born of the Spirit, with extended time on the new birth as resurrection and the writer's own conversion from atheism. | cut | Week 3: Session 2, Born of the Spirit, with extended time on the new birth as resurrection. |
| holy-spirit | `bibliography[11].note` | The account of a mind that resisted God and was finally drawn by grace it did not summon, a companion to the writer's own conversion from unbelief in session 2. | cut | The account of a mind that resisted God and was finally drawn by grace it did not summon, a companion to the account of the new birth in session 2. |

### hospitality (14)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| hospitality | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was welcomed to a table before he believed a word of it, and knows what it is to be the stranger somebody made room for. He also knows the reflex to keep the door shut, to reserve the table for people like him, to call a clean house a good excuse and a busy week a mercy to himself. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the reflex most of us know, to keep the door shut, to reserve the table for people like us, to call a clean house a good excuse and a busy week a mercy to ourselves. |
| hospitality | `sessions[0].summary` | A woman told Bell she was not gifted in hospitality, and what she meant was that her house was too small, her cooking too plain, her life too busy to host the kind of evening she saw in magazines. She had absorbed the great modern substitution, that hospitality is entertaining, a performance staged for people who will stage one back. | generalized | Many people say they are not gifted in hospitality, and what they mean is that the house is too small, the cooking too plain, life too busy to host the kind of evening they see in magazines. They have absorbed the great modern substitution, that hospitality is entertaining, a performance staged for people who will stage one back. |
| hospitality | `sessions[0].discussion[3].q` | Bell says the woman who felt ungifted at hospitality had confused it with a performance she could never stage well enough. | generalized | Bell says those who feel ungifted at hospitality have often confused it with a performance they could never stage well enough. |
| hospitality | `sessions[0].teaching` | A woman told Bell, with a small apology in her voice, that she was simply not gifted in hospitality. When he asked what she meant, it came out as a list of deficiencies. Her house was too small. Her cooking was too plain. Her weeks were too full to stage the kind of evening she saw in the magazines and on the screens, the long table and the candles and the effortless host moving through a beautiful room. She was not being falsely modest. She had absorbed, as almost all of us have, the great modern substitution, the quiet swapping of one thing for another so complete that we no longer notice it happened. | generalized | Many people will say, with a small apology in their voice, that they are simply not gifted in hospitality. Ask what they mean, and it comes out as a list of deficiencies. The house is too small. The cooking is too plain. The weeks are too full to stage the kind of evening they see in the magazines and on the screens, the long table and the candles and the effortless host moving through a beautiful room. They are not being falsely modest. They have absorbed, as almost all of us have, the great modern substitution, the quiet swapping of one thing for another so complete that we no longer notice it happened. |
| hospitality | `sessions[0].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because I know the closed circle from the inside. I have reserved my table for people like me and called a full week a mercy to myself. I have let a house that was not clean enough be the reason no one came, which means I let my own image be more important than someone else's welcome. The showroom standard did not make me more hospitable. It made me less, because it set a bar I could always find a reason not to clear. And under all of it sits the thing I keep forgetting, the thing that turns the whole matter over. I was the stranger. I came to faith from atheism, and before I believed a word of it, someone set a place for me at a table I had done nothing to earn a seat at. That is not just my story. It is everyone's, because God did not wait until we were presentable to welcome us. | cut | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because most of us know the closed circle from the inside. We have reserved our tables for people like us and called a full week a mercy to ourselves. We have let a house that was not clean enough be the reason no one came, which means we let our own image be more important than someone else's welcome. The showroom standard did not make us more hospitable. It made us less, because it set a bar we could always find a reason not to clear. And under all of it sits the thing we keep forgetting, the thing that turns the whole matter over. We were the stranger. That is everyone's story, because God did not wait until we were presentable to welcome us. |
| hospitality | `sessions[1].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside the text, because I am a screener. I want the credentials before the welcome. I want to know who someone is, whether they are safe and worth my time and unlikely to cost me too much, before I will make room, and I have called that instinct wisdom more times than it deserved the name. The story indicts me. | generalized | And here is where we have to stand inside the text, because most of us are screeners. We want the credentials before the welcome. We want to know who someone is, whether they are safe and worth our time and unlikely to cost us too much, before we will make room, and we have called that instinct wisdom more times than it deserved the name. The story indicts us. |
| hospitality | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here is where I have to put myself in the story, because I know which side of that table I would have been on, and it is not the side I would like to claim. I would have stood with the critics. I keep my own table respectable. I sort my welcome, quietly and without ever saying it aloud, by who seems to deserve a chair, who will reflect well on me, who has cleaned up enough to belong. And in doing that I forget the one thing that should make it impossible, which is that I was seated by a host who ate with me while I was still his enemy. | generalized | And here is where we have to put ourselves in the story, because most of us know which side of that table we would have been on, and it is not the side we would like to claim. We would have stood with the critics. We keep our own tables respectable. We sort our welcome, quietly and without ever saying it aloud, by who seems to deserve a chair, who will reflect well on us, who has cleaned up enough to belong. And in doing that we forget the one thing that should make it impossible, which is that we were seated by a host who ate with us while we were still his enemies. |
| hospitality | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside the indictment, because my home has been a fortress more than it has been an open door. I have treated my house as the place I go to shut the world out, to recover from people, to be private and undisturbed, and I have handed the welcome of the stranger to the church's programs with a quiet relief, glad that someone else was doing it so that I did not have to open my door. | generalized | And here is where we have to stand inside the indictment, because for most of us the home has been a fortress more than it has been an open door. We have treated our houses as the place we go to shut the world out, to recover from people, to be private and undisturbed, and we have handed the welcome of the stranger to the church's programs with a quiet relief, glad that someone else was doing it so that we did not have to open our doors. |
| hospitality | `sessions[4].teaching` | But now watch how fast the wise thing becomes the excuse, because it does, and I know the trick from the inside. | generalized | But now watch how fast the wise thing becomes the excuse, because it does, and most of us know the trick from the inside. |
| hospitality | `sessions[4].teaching` | And here is where I stand inside it, because I have used every one of these excuses. I have hidden in the complexity of the issue to avoid the simplicity of the person. I have called my locked door a boundary. I have admired the biblical strangers and declined the ones on my own street. | generalized | And here is where we stand inside it, because most of us have used every one of these excuses. We have hidden in the complexity of the issue to avoid the simplicity of the person. We have called a locked door a boundary. We have admired the biblical strangers and declined the ones on our own street. |
| hospitality | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism and was welcomed to a table before he believed, and he knows the fortress reflex because it is his own. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the fortress reflex. |
| hospitality | `faq[10].a` | What the study offers instead is a particular claim, that the deepest human need is to be welcomed rather than screened, seated rather than sorted, and that the writer himself was welcomed to a table while he was still an atheist, before he believed a word of it and with nothing asked in return. | cut | What the study offers instead is a particular claim, that the deepest human need is to be welcomed rather than screened, seated rather than sorted. |
| hospitality | `facilitatorScript.open` | He came to faith from atheism and was welcomed to a table before he believed a word of it, and he knows the reflex to keep the door shut because it is his own. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the reflex to keep the door shut. |
| hospitality | `promoKit.inviteCaption` | Written from inside the fortress instinct, by someone who was welcomed to a table before he believed. | cut | Written from inside the fortress instinct, not above it. |

### identity (12)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| identity | `summary` | He came to faith from atheism, which means he spent years building a self with no God in the picture, looking inward for a foundation and finding sand. He was raised without a father, which means the question of who he was had a hole in the middle of it where an inherited name should have been. He has performed a self he was not sure was real. He has measured his worth by the room's reaction and gone home empty. | cut | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not exempt himself from the performed self this study names, the worth measured by the room's reaction and carried home empty. |
| identity | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell came to faith from atheism, which meant building a self with no God in the picture and finding sand underneath. | generalized | Bell argues that a self built with no God in the picture finds sand underneath. |
| identity | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I know this floor better than I would like, because I came to faith from atheism, which meant I spent years doing exactly what the age commands. I looked inward for the foundation of who I was, with no God in the picture, no image I was made in, no name given from outside, just me and my feelings and my will, and I built a self on that and stood on it, and what I found underneath, when I was honest, was sand. The harder I looked inside for solid ground, the more the ground turned out to be more looking. There was no bottom. And on top of the missing bottom there was a second hole, because I was raised without a father, which means the question who am I had a wound in the middle of it where an inherited name should have been, a place where most people get handed at least a starting answer and I got silence. So when I say the inward self cannot finally hold your weight, I am not theorizing. I stood on it. I felt it give. | generalized | And many of us know this floor better than we would like, because we have done exactly what the age commands. We looked inward for the foundation of who we were, with no God in the picture, no image we were made in, no name given from outside, just ourselves and our feelings and our will, and we built a self on that and stood on it, and what we found underneath, when we were honest, was sand. The harder we looked inside for solid ground, the more the ground turned out to be more looking. There was no bottom. And for those raised without a father there can be a second hole on top of the missing bottom, a wound in the middle of the question who am I where an inherited name should have been, a place where most people get handed at least a starting answer and they got silence. So when this study says the inward self cannot finally hold your weight, it is not theorizing. We have stood on it. We have felt it give. |
| identity | `sessions[1].summary` | He grants it completely, and he refuses to stand outside it, because he has done it, measured his worth by the room's reaction, gone home from applause feeling empty, performed a self he was not sure was real. | generalized | He grants it completely, and he refuses to stand outside it, because most of us have done it, measured our worth by the room's reaction, gone home from applause feeling empty, performed a self we were not sure was real. |
| identity | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has gone home from applause feeling empty and performed a self he was not sure was real. | generalized | Many of us have gone home from applause feeling empty, having performed a self we were not sure was real. |
| identity | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I will not stand outside this and diagnose it from a safe distance, because I have lived inside it, and parts of me still do. I have measured my worth by the reaction in a room, watched faces to see whether the self I was presenting was working, adjusted on the fly to get the response I needed. I have walked off a stage with applause still in my ears and driven home feeling completely empty, because the applause was for a self I had performed, and a performed self cannot receive love, only validation, and validation does not feed you, it only quiets the hunger for an hour and then the hunger comes back worse. I have presented a version of myself that I was not sure was real, and then felt the particular loneliness of being approved of for something that was not quite me, which is somehow lonelier than not being approved of at all. So when I describe the exhaustion of the performed self, I am describing my own. | generalized | And I will not stand outside this and diagnose it from a safe distance, because none of us lives outside it. We measure our worth by the reaction in a room, watch faces to see whether the self we are presenting is working, adjust on the fly to get the response we need. We can walk away from applause and drive home feeling completely empty, because the applause was for a self we performed, and a performed self cannot receive love, only validation, and validation does not feed you, it only quiets the hunger for an hour and then the hunger comes back worse. We present a version of ourselves we are not sure is real, and then feel the particular loneliness of being approved of for something that is not quite us, which is somehow lonelier than not being approved of at all. So when I describe the exhaustion of the performed self, I am not describing someone else. |
| identity | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I know how hard that is to receive, because I came to faith from atheism, which means I spent years convinced that if my identity was going to mean anything, I had to build it myself, with no help from above, and the hardest thing in my conversion was not believing God existed. It was believing that the deepest thing about me could be a gift, that I did not have to earn my way to being someone, that the restlessness I had been trying to outwork was a door and not a defect. To stop achieving and start receiving felt, at first, like helplessness, like giving up control, like admitting I could not do the one thing I had built my whole self around doing. It was. That is exactly what it was. And it was the beginning of rest. | generalized | And this is hard to receive, especially for anyone who has spent years convinced that if their identity was going to mean anything, they had to build it themselves, with no help from above. For many, the hardest thing in coming to faith is not believing God exists. It is believing that the deepest thing about them could be a gift, that they do not have to earn their way to being someone, that the restlessness they have been trying to outwork is a door and not a defect. To stop achieving and start receiving feels, at first, like helplessness, like giving up control, like admitting we cannot do the one thing we have built our whole selves around doing. It is. That is exactly what it is. And it is the beginning of rest. |
| identity | `sessions[3].teaching` | I have stood on selves that collapsed. I built them out of intellect and argument and the approval of rooms, and I felt them give way and felt myself going down with them, because I had nothing underneath. The thing that finally held was not a better self I managed to build. It was a name I was given, a fact done to me, a life relocated into Christ and hidden there. And when I fail now, and I do, I am learning, slowly, to do what Luther did, to stop reaching for a feeling or a performance to save me and to return instead to the fact. | generalized | Many of us have stood on selves that collapsed. We built them out of intellect and argument and the approval of rooms, and we felt them give way and felt ourselves going down with them, because we had nothing underneath. The thing that finally holds is not a better self we manage to build. It is a name we are given, a fact done to us, a life relocated into Christ and hidden there. And when we fail, and we do, we can learn, slowly, to do what Luther did, to stop reaching for a feeling or a performance to save us and to return instead to the fact. |
| identity | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I know that ledger, because I have kept it. I have served and needed it noticed. I have given and waited for the credit. I have done the right thing and felt the quiet resentment when no one saw, which is the surest sign that the giving was about me, that I was performing love rather than pouring it out, because love poured out from a secure self does not keep score, and mine was counting. | generalized | And most of us know that ledger, because we have kept it. We have served and needed it noticed. We have given and waited for the credit. We have done the right thing and felt the quiet resentment when no one saw, which is the surest sign that the giving was about us, that we were performing love rather than pouring it out, because love poured out from a secure self does not keep score, and ours was counting. |
| identity | `leaderPrimer` | Bell spent years building a self with no God in the picture, an atheist manufacturing his own foundation, and he was raised without a father, so the question of who he was had a hole where an inherited name should have sat. He has performed a self he was not sure was real and gone home empty from rooms that applauded it. When he says the self we were sold is exhausting, that is autobiography, and yours should join it early. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not exempt himself from the performed self. When he says the self we were sold is exhausting, he writes from inside it, and your own honesty should join his early. |
| identity | `faq[0].a` | The writer puts himself inside the struggle from the start, admitting he came to faith from atheism and built a self with no God in the picture, that he has measured his worth by the room's reaction and driven home empty, that he has performed a self he was not sure was real and kept a secret ledger on his own good deeds. | cut | The writer puts himself inside the struggle from the start, a man who came to faith from atheism and does not exempt himself from the performed self or the secret ledger kept on our own good deeds. |
| identity | `faq[10].a` | He came to faith from atheism and built a self on sand. He was raised without a father, with a hole where an inherited name should have been. He has measured his worth by the room and gone home empty, clutched selves that collapsed and fallen with them. | cut | He came to faith from atheism. He was raised without a father. He does not exempt himself from measuring worth by the room or from clutching selves that collapse. |

### knowing-god (28)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| knowing-god | `summary` | Bell knows that pew. He came to God out of atheism, with no inherited faith and a trained suspicion of every claim about the divine, and he had to learn the difference between an argument that convinced him God exists and a Father who knew his name. He is not standing outside this study explaining God to people who find him distant. He spent years finding him distant. He writes from inside the long walk from knowing about God to knowing him, and the leader will lead from there too. | cut | Bell came to God out of atheism, and he is not standing outside this study explaining God to people who find him distant. He writes from inside the long walk from knowing about God to knowing him, and the leader will lead from there too. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].summary` | He grants the danger completely, because he came to faith through argument, through reading, through the hard work of being convinced, and he will not despise the very thing that brought him in. | generalized | He grants the danger completely, and he will not despise the reading and argument and hard work of being convinced that have brought many people to faith. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].discussion[2].q` | Bell admits he came to faith through argument and reading and will not despise the very thing that brought him in. | generalized | Bell will not despise the argument and reading that have brought many people to faith. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell says he has known a great deal about God on mornings when God himself felt completely absent. | generalized | Many believers have known a great deal about God on mornings when God himself felt completely absent. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].objections[0].response` | He came to faith from atheism, and he came through thinking, through argument and reading and the slow work of being convinced against his will, so he will not despise the mind, and neither does this study. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he will not despise the mind, and neither does this study. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].teaching` | I grant the danger, and I grant it without flinching, because I am the last person who gets to despise the mind. I did not drift into faith. I argued my way in, against my own resistance, out of atheism, through reading and thinking and the slow miserable work of being convinced of something I did not want to be true. The very thing that brought me in was the thing this objection is afraid I will now belittle, and I will not. | cut | I grant the danger, and I grant it without flinching, because this study will not despise the mind. Reading and thinking and the slow work of being convinced have brought many people to faith, often against their own resistance, and that is the very thing this objection is afraid I will now belittle, and I will not. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I know it from the inside, because I have stood in front of people and taught the truth about God flawlessly on mornings when God himself felt utterly absent to me, when I could recite him and not find him, when the facts were all in place and the One the facts were about seemed to be in another country. | generalized | And many who teach know it from the inside, having stood in front of people and taught the truth about God flawlessly on mornings when God himself felt utterly absent, able to recite him and not find him, the facts all in place and the One the facts were about seeming to be in another country. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he spent years treating the Trinity as a doctrine to defend rather than a God to adore, and could win the argument while never having worshiped the God he was defending. | generalized | It is possible to spend years treating the Trinity as a doctrine to defend rather than a God to adore, winning the argument while never having worshiped the God being defended. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[1].teaching` | But I have walked you through all of that, the councils and the categories, and I have to confess that I spent years doing exactly that and missing the entire point, because here is the heart of it, the thing that turns the Trinity from a doctrine you tolerate into a God you adore. | generalized | But I have walked you through all of that, the councils and the categories, and it is possible to spend years doing exactly that and miss the entire point, because here is the heart of it, the thing that turns the Trinity from a doctrine you tolerate into a God you adore. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[1].teaching` | I spent years able to win the argument and never once having worshiped the God I was defending. Do not make my mistake. | generalized | It is possible to spend years able to win the argument and never once worship the God you are defending. Do not make that mistake. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell takes the strong form of the wound, because he lived it. He was raised without a father, and he will not pretend the word is warm for everyone. | cut | Bell takes the strong form of the wound. He was raised without a father, and he will not pretend the word is warm for everyone. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he came to God with no working picture of a good father and has prayed to a Father he could barely believe in. | generalized | Many people come to God with no working picture of a good father and pray, for a long time, to a Father they can barely believe in. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].objections[1].response` | Bell will not wave this away, because the hole is real and he carries the same one, and a heavenly Father does not retroactively give you the childhood you should have had. | cut | Bell will not wave this away, because the hole is real, and a heavenly Father does not retroactively give you the childhood you should have had. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].teaching` | But for others of you, and I am one of them, the word is not a warm room. | cut | But for others of you, the word is not a warm room. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].teaching` | I was raised without a father. I came to God out of atheism, with no inherited faith and no working picture of what a good father even was, so when I tell you I understand the flinch, I am not performing sympathy from a safe distance. I am telling you my own. And I will not do the thing the church too often does, which is to wave the wound away, to rush past it to the comforting doctrine as if the pain were an inconvenience to the lesson. | cut | I was raised without a father, and I came to God out of atheism. I will not do the thing the church too often does, which is to wave the wound away, to rush past it to the comforting doctrine as if the pain were an inconvenience to the lesson. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here is where I have to step inside this rather than stand outside it, because I am not teaching this from the far shore of having figured it out. I came to God with no working picture of a good father, and there were years when I prayed to a Father I could barely believe in, saying the word with a part of me bracing for him to be like the absence I had known. The fact of my adoption was true on those mornings whether or not it felt true, and slowly, slowly, the running Father in the story began to overtake the absent father in my history, not by erasing the wound but by being, simply, real, and present, and unlike the man I had feared he would be. | generalized | And here is where I have to step inside this rather than stand outside it, because none of us teaches this from the far shore of having figured it out. Many come to God with no working picture of a good father, and pray for years to a Father they can barely believe in, saying the word with a part of them bracing for him to be like the absence they have known. The fact of their adoption is true on those mornings whether or not it feels true, and slowly, slowly, the running Father in the story can overtake the absent father in their history, not by erasing the wound but by being, simply, real, and present, and unlike the man they had feared he would be. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he wanted, as a skeptic, a God he could keep abstract and at a distance, and that a God with a face is harder to avoid than a God who stays a cloud. | generalized | Bell says many of us would rather keep God abstract and at a distance, because a God with a face is harder to avoid than a God who stays a cloud. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[3].objections[0].response` | Bell grants the integrity of this, because it is the honest agnostic position and he held something close to it himself, and he agrees that if knowing God were a matter of us reasoning our way up to the infinite, the humble conclusion would indeed be that we cannot, that the cloud is too high and our guesses are just guesses. | cut | Bell grants the integrity of this, because it is the honest agnostic position, and he agrees that if knowing God were a matter of us reasoning our way up to the infinite, the humble conclusion would indeed be that we cannot, that the cloud is too high and our guesses are just guesses. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[3].teaching` | I want to grant the integrity of that, fully, because I held a version of it myself, and because it is, in fact, exactly the right conclusion, if you start where the agnostic starts. | cut | I want to grant the integrity of that, fully, because it is, in fact, exactly the right conclusion, if you start where the agnostic starts. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[3].teaching` | The second escape is to keep God abstract on purpose, and I have to confess this one, because it was mine. As a skeptic, I wanted a God I could keep at a distance, kept vague, because a God who is a cloud makes no demands. You cannot disappoint a cloud. But a God with a face is harder to avoid. A God who looked at people and wept, who said follow me, has come close enough to ask something of you, and part of me preferred the cloud precisely because it left me alone. | generalized | The second escape is to keep God abstract on purpose, and it tempts skeptics and believers alike. Many of us want a God we can keep at a distance, kept vague, because a God who is a cloud makes no demands. You cannot disappoint a cloud. But a God with a face is harder to avoid. A God who looked at people and wept, who said follow me, has come close enough to ask something of you, and part of us prefers the cloud precisely because it leaves us alone. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he can teach the Trinity flawlessly and still relate to God as a subject he has mastered rather than a fellowship he has been welcomed into. | generalized | It is possible to explain the Trinity flawlessly and still relate to God as a subject mastered rather than a fellowship you have been welcomed into. |
| knowing-god | `sessions[4].teaching` | I can teach all of this. I can lay out Nicaea and Chalcedon, walk you through the radiance and the exact imprint, explain why God can be love only because he is three-in-one, and do it flawlessly, and still, on a given week, relate to God as a subject I have mastered rather than a fellowship I have been welcomed into. I can stand in the doorway of the room, holding a perfect description of what is inside, and not go in. | generalized | Those of us who teach can lay out Nicaea and Chalcedon, walk you through the radiance and the exact imprint, explain why God can be love only because he is three-in-one, and do it flawlessly, and still, on a given week, relate to God as a subject we have mastered rather than a fellowship we have been welcomed into. We can stand in the doorway of the room, holding a perfect description of what is inside, and not go in. |
| knowing-god | `leaderPrimer` | Bell admits he can teach Nicaea flawlessly on a morning when God feels like a subject he has mastered rather than a Father he has come to, and you should take his admission as your assignment. | generalized | Bell warns that a teacher can explain Nicaea flawlessly on a morning when God feels like a subject mastered rather than a Father come to, and you should take that warning as your assignment. |
| knowing-god | `leaderPrimer` | Bell was raised without a father and came to God from atheism with no working picture of what a good one was, and he prayed to a Father he could barely believe in long before any warmth came. | cut | Bell was raised without a father and came to God from atheism, and he will not pretend the word is easy. |
| knowing-god | `faq[0].a` | If you came braced for a seminar, you will find instead a study that treats the highest doctrine the church holds as the actual shape of a Christian life, and that implicates the writer first, who admits he can teach all of it flawlessly and still relate to God as a subject he has mastered rather than a Father he has come to know. | generalized | If you came braced for a seminar, you will find instead a study that treats the highest doctrine the church holds as the actual shape of a Christian life, and that implicates the writer first, who does not exempt himself from relating to God as a subject mastered rather than a Father known. |
| knowing-god | `faq[8].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, so he is not naive about the doubt, he lived it, and he admits he preferred God abstract precisely because a vague God makes no demands. | generalized | The writer came to faith from atheism, so he is not naive about the doubt, he lived it, and he names how many of us prefer God abstract precisely because a vague God makes no demands. |
| knowing-god | `faq[9].a` | The writer admits he has known a great deal about God on mornings God himself felt absent, that he came to faith from atheism with no felt warmth, that he can teach the highest doctrine of God and still stand in the doorway of the room it describes rather than step inside. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from knowing a great deal about God on mornings God himself feels absent, or from standing in the doorway of the room the doctrine describes rather than stepping inside. |
| knowing-god | `faq[10].a` | He was raised without a father, so the word Father is hard for him too.
