### lament (14)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| lament | `summary` | He was raised without a father, and he came to God out of atheism, and he has prayed prayers that got no answer he could see. He has stood at gravesides and had nothing to say that did not sound like a lie. He writes from inside the loss. | cut | He was raised without a father, and he came to God out of atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the unanswered prayer and the graveside silence this study names. He writes from inside it, not above it. |
| lament | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell, after a funeral, that he had stopped coming to church because there was no room in it for how he actually felt. He was not angry at God. He was grieving, and every song the church sang told him to be grateful, and he had nothing left to be grateful with. He was not faithless. He had simply been handed a worship that ran in one direction only, upward, and his grief had nowhere to go. | generalized | Many grieving people stop coming to church because there is no room in it for how they actually feel. They are not angry at God. They are grieving, and every song the church sings tells them to be grateful, and they have nothing left to be grateful with. They are not faithless. They have simply been handed a worship that runs in one direction only, upward, and their grief has nowhere to go. |
| lament | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has stood at gravesides with nothing true to say and reached for a comfortable line anyway. | generalized | Many of us, standing beside someone in grief with nothing true to say, have reached for a comfortable line anyway. |
| lament | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man after a funeral. He told Bell he had stopped coming to church because there was no room in it for how he actually felt. He was not angry at God, he said. He was grieving, and every song the church put in his mouth told him to be grateful, and he had run clean out of gratitude. He was not a faithless man. He had simply been handed a worship that ran in one direction only, upward, all ascent and no descent, and his grief had found no place to land. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, the grieving man who has stopped coming to church because there is no room in it for how he actually feels. He is not angry at God. He is grieving, and every song the church puts in his mouth tells him to be grateful, and he has run clean out of gratitude. He is not a faithless man. He has simply been handed a worship that runs in one direction only, upward, all ascent and no descent, and his grief has found no place to land. |
| lament | `sessions[0].teaching` | Walter Brueggemann named this decades ago, the costly loss of lament, and the cost is exactly what that man at the funeral was describing. | generalized | Walter Brueggemann named this decades ago, the costly loss of lament, and the cost is exactly what that grieving man describes. |
| lament | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this failure, because I have committed it. I have stood at the edge of a grave with a family I love and felt the silence press in, and reached, God forgive me, for a comfortable line, a reason, a tidy verse, because I could not bear to let the silence be the silence. I gave an answer when I should have given my presence and my tears. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this failure, because most of us have committed it. We have stood beside a grieving family and felt the silence press in, and reached, God forgive us, for a comfortable line, a reason, a tidy verse, because we could not bear to let the silence be the silence. We gave an answer when we should have given our presence and our tears. |
| lament | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has tried to force the turn, to tack a hopeful ending onto a grief that had not earned one, because the dark felt like a failure. | generalized | Many of us try to force the turn, to tack a hopeful ending onto a grief that has not earned one, because the dark feels like a failure. |
| lament | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess that I am the one who forces the turn. I have done it in my own prayers, tacking a hopeful ending onto a grief that had not earned one because the open wound felt like a failure of faith. I have done it to other people, hurrying them toward the silver lining because I could not bear to leave them in the dark. | generalized | And most of us have to confess that we are the ones who force the turn. We do it in our own prayers, tacking a hopeful ending onto a grief that has not earned one because the open wound feels like a failure of faith. We do it to other people, hurrying them toward the silver lining because we cannot bear to leave them in the dark. |
| lament | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he is the friend, that he has explained someone's suffering to defend God when he should have sat in the ash and said nothing. | generalized | Most of us have been the friend, explaining someone's suffering to defend God when we should have sat in the ash and said nothing. |
| lament | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here I have to admit, with no distance at all, that I am the friend. I am Eliphaz. I have come to people in genuine agony and felt the silence press in and reached for a reason, an explanation, a way to account for their pain that would let God off the hook and let me feel useful, and I have done it precisely when the holiest thing available to me was to shut my mouth and sit in the ash. I have defended God to people God was closer to than I was. The most spiritual thing the friends ever did was the silence, and the least spiritual thing was every word after, and that is the pattern of the church, and it is my pattern. | generalized | And here we have to admit, with no distance at all, that we are the friends. We are Eliphaz. We have come to people in genuine agony and felt the silence press in and reached for a reason, an explanation, a way to account for their pain that would let God off the hook and let us feel useful, and we have done it precisely when the holiest thing available to us was to shut our mouths and sit in the ash. We have defended God to people God was closer to than we were. The most spiritual thing the friends ever did was the silence, and the least spiritual thing was every word after, and that is the pattern of the church, and it is ours. |
| lament | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to say that this is the only thing that has ever held me at a graveside, the only thing I have left when the explanations run out, which they always do. Not a reason for the loss. A God who lost. A God who wept, who was forsaken, who prayed the lament I am too broken to finish, and who then, and this is the hope we hold for next time, walked out of the grave he went into, so that the dark, though it is real and though we are in it, does not get the final word. | generalized | And this is the only thing that holds at a graveside, the only thing left when the explanations run out, which they always do. Not a reason for the loss. A God who lost. A God who wept, who was forsaken, who prayed the lament we are too broken to finish, and who then, and this is the hope we hold for next time, walked out of the grave he went into, so that the dark, though it is real and though we are in it, does not get the final word. |
| lament | `sessions[4].teaching` | The study does not fix that, and a study that claimed to would be lying to you, and I have done enough lying at gravesides to refuse to do it here. | cut | The study does not fix that, and a study that claimed to would be lying to you, and too many comfortable lies have already been told at gravesides. |
| lament | `leaderPrimer` | Bell comes to this as a pastor who has stood at gravesides with nothing true to say, a man who came to God from atheism and was raised without a father, which means the unanswered question is not a genre to him. It has his fingerprints on it. | cut | Bell comes to this as a pastor, a man who came to God from atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not treat the unanswered question as a genre. |
| lament | `faq[10].a` | He has prayed prayers that got no answer he could see, and stood at gravesides with nothing true to say and reached for a comfortable line anyway. He writes from inside the loss, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot help a grieving person from above without your help curdling into either shame or distance, the very thing Job's friends did. | cut | He does not exempt himself from the unanswered prayer or the comfortable line reached for at a graveside. He writes from inside it, and the study asks every leader to do the same, because you cannot help a grieving person from above without your help curdling into either shame or distance, the very thing Job's friends did. |

### manhood (12)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| manhood | `summary` | He knows the ache of a chair at the table that was always empty, and he knows the fear of a man who has no template for the thing he is trying to become for his boys. He does not write from a safe distance. He has been the man who confused controlling a room with leading it, who went silent for days rather than say he was afraid, who reached for anger because it felt more like strength than the truth did. He writes from inside the indictment, and so will you. | cut | He does not write from a safe distance. Many of us have been the man who confused controlling a room with leading it, who went silent for days rather than say he was afraid, who reached for anger because it felt more like strength than the truth did. He writes from inside that indictment, and so will you. |
| manhood | `sessions[0].summary` | A young man told Bell he did not know how to be a man because everyone who had tried to tell him was selling something. One voice said a real man dominates, wins, never backs down, takes what he wants. The other said the whole idea was toxic and he should apologize for having shoulders. He was standing between two caricatures, and neither one was going to make him a man. | generalized | Many young men say they do not know how to be a man because everyone who has tried to tell them was selling something. One voice says a real man dominates, wins, never backs down, takes what he wants. The other says the whole idea is toxic and they should apologize for having shoulders. They are standing between two caricatures, and neither one is going to make them men. |
| manhood | `sessions[0].teaching` | A young man sat across from Bell and said, with more honesty than most men manage, that he did not know how to be a man, because everyone who had ever tried to tell him was selling something. One set of voices told him a real man dominates, wins, never backs down, takes what he wants and calls the taking strength. The other set told him the whole idea of manhood was toxic and he should more or less apologize for having shoulders. He was standing exactly in the middle of the culture war over masculinity, being handed two cartoons, and it had not occurred to anyone that neither cartoon was going to make him a man. | generalized | Start with a familiar young man, the one who will say, with more honesty than most men manage, that he does not know how to be a man, because everyone who has ever tried to tell him was selling something. One set of voices tells him a real man dominates, wins, never backs down, takes what he wants and calls the taking strength. The other set tells him the whole idea of manhood is toxic and he should more or less apologize for having shoulders. He is standing exactly in the middle of the culture war over masculinity, being handed two cartoons, and it has not occurred to anyone that neither cartoon is going to make him a man. |
| manhood | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside it, because I know both counterfeits from the inside. Raised without a father, I had no template, so I built one out of whatever looked like strength, and for years the thing that looked most like strength to me was control, the managed room, the winning argument, the anger that felt more like power than the truth ever did. I have also gone the other way, gone silent and passive and called it keeping the peace, when it was really just Adam in the garden again, present and useless, letting the thing go wrong because speaking up felt like too much risk. | generalized | And here is where we have to stand inside it, because most men know both counterfeits from the inside. A boy raised without a father has no template, so he builds one out of whatever looks like strength, and for many men the thing that looks most like strength is control, the managed room, the winning argument, the anger that feels more like power than the truth ever does. And many of us have also gone the other way, gone silent and passive and called it keeping the peace, when it was really just Adam in the garden again, present and useless, letting the thing go wrong because speaking up felt like too much risk. |
| manhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | He grew up with an empty chair at the head of the table, and he learned early what he now wants every man in the room to hear, that the wound a missing father leaves does not announce itself. | cut | An empty chair at the head of the table teaches something he now wants every man in the room to hear, that the wound a missing father leaves does not announce itself. |
| manhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know this because I lived it. I told myself for years that growing up without a father had made me strong, self-reliant, that it was a fact about my past and nothing more, and the whole time it was shaping how I led, how I loved, how desperately I needed to be respected, how hard it was for me to depend on anyone or believe anyone would stay. | generalized | Many men raised without a father tell themselves for years that it made them strong, self-reliant, that it is a fact about their past and nothing more, and the whole time it is shaping how they lead, how they love, how desperately they need to be respected, how hard it is for them to depend on anyone or believe anyone will stay. |
| manhood | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to sit down inside that, because my anger is almost never like the whip. My anger is almost always about me, my honor, my being disrespected, my will crossed, and then I reach for the temple story to make my temper holy, which is exactly the theft the whip was driving out. | generalized | And we have to sit down inside that, because our anger is almost never like the whip. Our anger is almost always about us, our honor, our being disrespected, our will crossed, and then we reach for the temple story to make our temper holy, which is exactly the theft the whip was driving out. |
| manhood | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I know this one from the inside too, because a man raised without a father learns self-reliance the way other boys learn to ride a bike, early and permanently, and for years I wore my ability to carry everything alone as a badge, and it was really just a very tall wall, and there were seasons it nearly buried me. | generalized | A man raised without a father often learns self-reliance the way other boys learn to ride a bike, early and permanently, and wears his ability to carry everything alone as a badge for years, when it is really just a very tall wall, and there are seasons it nearly buries him. |

| manhood | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons with no template he was handed, only the one God gave him. He is not the man who figured manhood out. He is a man learning to father in real time, and that is exactly the qualification. |
| manhood | `faq[9].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons with no template he was ever handed, and he does not stand outside the struggles he describes. ### marriage (19)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |

| marriage | `summary` | The writer is married, has five sons, and writes from inside the room where two people who love each other still wound each other on a Tuesday over nothing. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the husband who kept a record he swore he had forgiven, who confused leading with getting his way, who let a screen become the easier thing to turn toward. He writes from inside the indictment. | cut | The writer is a pastor with five sons, and he writes toward the room where two people who love each other still wound each other on a Tuesday over nothing. He does not point from a safe distance. He writes from inside that indictment. |
| marriage | `sessions[0].summary` | A man sat across from Bell after fourteen years of marriage and said, with the calm of someone reporting weather, that he and his wife had simply grown apart, that the arrangement no longer worked for either of them. He was not cruel. He was honest about a thing he had assumed all along, that marriage is a contract, and a contract is kept while it delivers and dissolved when it does not. | generalized | Many people, years into a marriage, will say with the calm of someone reporting weather that they and their spouse have simply grown apart, that the arrangement no longer works for either of them. They are not cruel. They are honest about a thing they assumed all along, that marriage is a contract, and a contract is kept while it delivers and dissolved when it does not. |
| marriage | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has kept his own marriage as a contract more often than he wants to admit, measuring, withdrawing, keeping a quiet ledger. | generalized | Many of us have kept our marriages as contracts more often than we want to admit, measuring, withdrawing, keeping a quiet ledger. |
| marriage | `sessions[0].teaching` | A man sat across from Bell after fourteen years of marriage and said, with the flat calm of someone reporting the weather, that he and his wife had simply grown apart, that the arrangement no longer worked for either of them. He was not cruel and he was not lying. He was telling the truth about a thing he had assumed from the day he said his vows, that marriage is a contract, and a contract is a sensible thing you keep while it delivers and dissolve when it stops. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, the man years into a marriage who says, with the flat calm of someone reporting the weather, that he and his wife have simply grown apart, that the arrangement no longer works for either of them. He is not cruel and he is not lying. He is telling the truth about a thing he has assumed from the day he said his vows, that marriage is a contract, and a contract is a sensible thing you keep while it delivers and dissolve when it stops. |
| marriage | `sessions[0].teaching` | Here is where I have to stand inside it, because the law names the writer before the reader. I have kept my own marriage as a contract more often than I want to put in print. I have measured, withdrawn affection and called it being busy, rationed kindness to match what I felt I had received, kept a quiet ledger I would have denied keeping. I have five sons watching how their father treats their mother, learning what a covenant looks like from a man who keeps forgetting it is one. | generalized | Here is where we have to stand inside it, because the law names the writer before the reader. Many of us have kept our marriages as contracts more often than we want to admit. We have measured, withdrawn affection and called it being busy, rationed kindness to match what we felt we had received, kept a quiet ledger we would have denied keeping. And children are watching, learning what a covenant looks like from parents who keep forgetting it is one. |
| marriage | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell once went three days without a real word to his wife over something so small that by day two he could no longer remember what it was, only that he was right and she had not yet admitted it. Bell calls it the small war. |

| money | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has felt his chest loosen at a number in an account and called the loosening peace, mistaking arithmetic for contentment. | generalized | Many of us have felt our chests loosen at a number in an account and called the loosening peace, mistaking arithmetic for contentment. |
| money | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know this from the inside. I have felt my chest loosen at a number in an account and called the loosening peace. It was not peace. It was a circumstance, and circumstances turn, and the peace turned with it, which is how I learned it had never been peace at all. | generalized | Most of us know this from the inside. We have felt our chests loosen at a number in an account and called the loosening peace. It was not peace. It was a circumstance, and circumstances turn, and the peace turned with it, which is how we learn it had never been peace at all. |
| money | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell stands inside it, having built barns he called prudence while his soul went unaddressed. | generalized | Bell stands inside it, because most of us have built barns we called prudence while our souls went unaddressed. |
| money | `sessions[2].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits he has built barns he called prudence while his soul went unaddressed. | generalized | Most of us have built barns we called prudence while our souls went unaddressed. |
| money | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to say it before I say it of anyone else, because I have built barns I called prudence while my soul went years without being addressed. I have planned for the body's future in detail and left the soul's future to chance, as if the shorter life were the one that needed the planning. The rich fool is not a stranger I get to judge from a distance. He is the man I become every time I confuse securing my circumstances with tending my soul. | generalized | And it has to be said of us before it is said of anyone else, because most of us have built barns we called prudence while our souls went years without being addressed, planning for the body's future in detail and leaving the soul's future to chance, as if the shorter life were the one that needed the planning. The rich fool is not a stranger we get to judge from a distance. He is who we become every time we confuse securing our circumstances with tending our souls. |
| money | `sessions[3].summary` | Bell stands inside it, a man who has lain awake doing arithmetic and calling the lying awake responsibility. | generalized | Bell stands inside it, because many of us have lain awake doing arithmetic and called the lying awake responsibility. |
| money | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has lain awake doing arithmetic and called the lying awake responsibility. | generalized | Many of us have lain awake doing arithmetic and called the lying awake responsibility. |
| money | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to climb down into this one, because money-anxiety is my native tongue. I have lain awake at three in the morning doing arithmetic, running the numbers against the bills against the future, and I have called the lying awake responsibility. I have called it being a good provider, being careful, looking after my five sons, and all of that language was a costume over a simpler thing, a fear that the Father would not come through, that it was finally up to me, that if I stopped worrying the bottom would fall out. The worry felt like love. It was actually a quiet accusation against God, a refusal to believe He knew I was here. And the worst of it is that the worry never once helped, never added a dollar or an hour, only taxed the very strength I needed for the day in front of me. | generalized | And we have to climb down into this one, because for many of us money-anxiety is a native tongue. We lie awake at three in the morning doing arithmetic, running the numbers against the bills against the future, and we call the lying awake responsibility. We call it being a good provider, being careful, looking after the children, and all of that language is a costume over a simpler thing, a fear that the Father will not come through, that it is finally up to us, that if we stopped worrying the bottom would fall out. The worry feels like love. It is actually a quiet accusation against God, a refusal to believe He knows we are here. And the worst of it is that the worry never once helps, never adds a dollar or an hour, only taxes the very strength we need for the day in front of us. |
| money | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell stands inside it, a man whose fist has had to be pried open finger by finger. | generalized | Bell stands inside it, because most of our fists have to be pried open finger by finger. |
| money | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits his own fist has had to be pried open finger by finger. | generalized | For most of us the fist has to be pried open finger by finger. |
| money | `sessions[4].discussion[4].behind` | Bell does not pretend his hand opened easily, it had to be pried, finger by finger. | generalized | The study does not pretend the hand opens easily, it has to be pried, finger by finger. |
| money | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to be honest about my own hand, because it has never opened easily. It has had to be pried open, finger by finger, over years, by a God patient enough to do it slowly. I have given reluctantly and called it stewardship. I have calculated the gift down to what I could spare without feeling it and called the calculation wisdom. The truth is that my fist was closed because I was afraid, because the money felt like the wall between my five sons and the dark, and to open my hand felt like lowering the wall. What I could not see, for a long time, is that the wall was also a cage, that the hand closed around my security was a hand I no longer owned, and that the prying open, which felt like loss every single time, was God setting me free. | generalized | And we have to be honest about our own hands, because they rarely open easily. Most have to be pried open, finger by finger, over years, by a God patient enough to do it slowly. We give reluctantly and call it stewardship. We calculate the gift down to what we can spare without feeling it and call the calculation wisdom. The truth is that the fist stays closed because we are afraid, because the money feels like the wall between the people we love and the dark, and to open the hand feels like lowering the wall. What we cannot see, often for a long time, is that the wall is also a cage, that the hand closed around our security is a hand we no longer own, and that the prying open, which feels like loss every single time, is God setting us free. |
| money | `leaderPrimer` | He has done the midnight arithmetic and called it prudence, and he is a father of five sons who has watched provision shade into accumulation without ever feeling the border crossing. | cut | He is a father of five sons, and he knows how easily provision shades into accumulation without anyone feeling the border crossing. |
| money | `faq[10].a` | He has lain awake doing arithmetic he called prudence, felt his chest loosen at a number and called it peace, given reluctantly and called it stewardship, watched his own fist have to be pried open finger by finger. | generalized | He does not exempt himself from the arithmetic called prudence, the chest that loosens at a number, the reluctant giving called stewardship, or the fist that has to be pried open finger by finger. |
| money | `facilitatorScript.open` | He has lain awake doing arithmetic he called prudence, felt his chest loosen at a number and called it peace, watched his own fist have to be pried open. | generalized | He does not exempt himself from the arithmetic called prudence or the fist that has to be pried open. |

### new-believer (46)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |

| new-believer | `summary` | He knows what it is to stand outside the whole thing certain it was a fairy tale, and he knows the strange morning he woke up unable to deny it any longer. He did not grow up in this. He arrived as a stranger and had to learn the first steps himself, badly, with no one to teach him. He writes from inside the beginning, not above it. | cut | He knows what it is to stand outside the whole thing certain it was not true. He writes from inside the beginning, not above it. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell after a baptism that he felt nothing now, that the high of the first week had faded, and he wanted to know if it had been real. | generalized | Many new believers say, a few weeks past baptism, that they feel nothing now, that the high of the first week has faded, and they want to know if it was real. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell, who came to faith from atheism, did not feel born again the morning he could no longer deny Christ. He felt afraid. | cut | (sentence removed) |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell came to faith from atheism and says he did not feel born again that first morning, he felt afraid. | generalized | Many new believers do not feel born again at first, and some feel afraid. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].discussion[4].behind` | The aim is to free them from the script by admitting the writer's own beginning was strange and frightened, not cinematic. | generalized | The aim is to free them from the script by admitting that real beginnings are often strange and frightened, not cinematic. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].objections[0].response` | The morning Bell could no longer deny Christ, he did not feel joy. He felt fear, and a kind of grief for the certainty he was losing. He was as born again that frightened morning as he has ever been since. | generalized | For many, the first morning of faith brings not joy but fear, and a kind of grief for the certainty they are losing, and they are as born again that frightened morning as they will ever be. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].teaching` | A man told Bell after his baptism that the high had worn off, that the first week felt like fire and now he felt nothing, and he wanted to know whether the whole thing had been real. It is the question almost every new believer asks within the first month, and the question hides a better one underneath it. He was asking how do I feel. The question he needed was what actually happened. | generalized | A new believer a few weeks past baptism says the high has worn off, that the first week felt like fire and now he feels nothing, and he wants to know whether the whole thing was real. It is the question almost every new believer asks within the first month, and the question hides a better one underneath it. He is asking how do I feel. The question he needs is what actually happened. |
| new-believer | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because I did not grow up in this. I came to faith from atheism, and I can tell you the morning I could no longer honestly deny Christ was not a warm morning. I did not feel joy. I felt fear, and something close to grief, because a certainty I had built my whole mind on was crumbling and I did not yet have anything to put in its place. I was as born again that frightened morning as I have ever been since. If I had measured the reality of it by the feeling, I would have concluded that nothing happened, when in fact the largest thing that has ever happened to me was happening right then. | generalized | And for many, especially those who come to faith out of unbelief, the beginning is not a warm morning. They do not feel joy. They feel fear, and something close to grief, because a certainty they had built their whole minds on is crumbling and they do not yet have anything to put in its place. They are as born again that frightened morning as they will ever be. If they measured the reality of it by the feeling, they would conclude that nothing happened, when in fact the largest thing that has ever happened to them is happening right then. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].summary` | A new believer told Bell she had tried to read the Bible and gave up somewhere in the ancient laws, certain the failure was hers. It was not hers. She started on page one and walked straight into a wall almost every beginner hits. | generalized | Many new believers try to read the Bible and give up somewhere in the ancient laws, certain the failure is theirs. It is not theirs. They start on page one and walk straight into a wall almost every beginner hits. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell, who first opened a Bible as an atheist trying to disprove it, read to win an argument and found instead that he was the one being read. | generalized | Many who open it to win an argument find instead that they are the ones being read. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell first opened a Bible as an atheist trying to disprove it and found he was the one being read. | generalized | Many who open the Bible to disprove it find they are the ones being read. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].objections[1].response` | Bell came at the Bible from exactly your angle. He opened it as an atheist intending to expose it as a clumsy human invention, expecting to win. | cut | Bell, who came to faith from atheism, takes your angle seriously. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].objections[1].response` | Second, and this is what surprised Bell, the truest test of the book is not won at arm's length but by reading it. | cut | Second, the truest test of the book is not won at arm's length but by reading it. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].objections[1].response` | He sat down to read it as an opponent and found it reading him, naming things about himself no human author could have known to write. | generalized | Many who sit down to read it as opponents find it reading them, naming things about them no human author could have known to write. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].teaching` | A new believer told Bell she had given the Bible an honest try and quit somewhere in the middle of the ancient laws, the endless instructions about sacrifices and festivals, and she had concluded the failure was hers, that she was too slow or too unspiritual to read God's book. The failure was not hers. She had done exactly what almost every beginner does, which is to open the Bible at page one and try to read it front to back like a novel, and she had walked straight into the wall that stops nearly everyone. | generalized | Many new believers give the Bible an honest try and quit somewhere in the middle of the ancient laws, the endless instructions about sacrifices and festivals, and conclude the failure is theirs, that they are too slow or too unspiritual to read God's book. The failure is not theirs. They have done exactly what almost every beginner does, which is to open the Bible at page one and try to read it front to back like a novel, and they have walked straight into the wall that stops nearly everyone. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to tell you how I came to this book, because it was not as a believer looking for comfort. I first opened a Bible as an atheist, intending to read it the way you read an opponent's argument, looking for the seams and the contradictions so I could put the whole thing down for good. I came to win. And the strangest thing happened, the thing I still cannot fully explain. As I read it to expose it, I began to feel that it was reading me, that it knew things about the human heart, about my heart, that the men who supposedly cobbled it together could not have known to write. I sat down as the one doing the examining and slowly realized I was the one being examined. I am not telling you that to settle every historical question, because those questions are real and you should ask them and write them down. I am telling you because some things about this book are only learned from the inside, with it open. So open Mark. Ask what it says. And let the Word do to you what it did to me. | generalized | And some come to this book not as believers looking for comfort but as skeptics, reading it the way you read an opponent's argument, looking for the seams and the contradictions so they can put the whole thing down for good. And a strange thing can happen. As they read it to expose it, they begin to feel that it is reading them, that it knows things about the human heart, about their own hearts, that the men who supposedly cobbled it together could not have known to write. They sit down as the ones doing the examining and slowly realize they are the ones being examined. That does not settle every historical question, because those questions are real and you should ask them and write them down. But some things about this book are only learned from the inside, with it open. So open Mark. Ask what it says. And let the Word do its work in you. |
| new-believer | `sessions[1].scriptureStudy[1].note` | Scripture is not inert text, it reads the reader, which is exactly what surprised the atheist who opened it to disprove it. | generalized | Scripture is not inert text, it reads the reader, which is exactly what surprises many who open it to disprove it. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].summary` | A new believer told Bell he did not know how to pray, that he opened his mouth and felt like he was doing it wrong, like everyone else had a script he had never been given. | generalized | Many new believers say they do not know how to pray, that they open their mouths and feel like they are doing it wrong, like everyone else has a script they were never given. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell, who spent years certain there was no one to talk to, says the hardest and best thing he ever did was open his mouth to a God he was not sure was there, and keep doing it until he was. | generalized | Bell says one of the hardest and best things a doubter can do is open his mouth to a God he is not sure is there, and keep doing it until he is. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell spent years sure there was no one on the other end, and says the hardest thing he ever did was speak to a God he was not sure was there. | generalized | Bell says one of the hardest things a doubter can do is speak to a God he is not sure is there. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].objections[1].response` | Bell grants how reasonable that sounds, because he lived inside that exact thought for years as an atheist, and he will not pretend it away with a slogan. | cut | Bell grants how reasonable that sounds, as a man who came to faith from atheism, and he will not pretend it away with a slogan. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].objections[1].response` | Second, Bell would tell you what he found, that the only way he ever learned there was someone on the other end was by speaking into what felt like silence and continuing until the silence stopped feeling empty. | new-believer | `sessions[2].teaching` | A new believer told Bell, with real embarrassment, that he did not know how to pray, that he would open his mouth and immediately feel he was doing it wrong, as though everyone else in the building had been handed a script he never received. Hear the assumption underneath that, because it is nearly universal among beginners. He assumed prayer was a performance with rules, and that his job was to perform it correctly. | generalized | Many new believers will admit, with real embarrassment, that they do not know how to pray, that they open their mouths and immediately feel they are doing it wrong, as though everyone else in the building had been handed a script they never received. Hear the assumption underneath that, because it is nearly universal among beginners. They assume prayer is a performance with rules, and that their job is to perform it correctly. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].teaching` | And prayer is not merely talking to yourself, which answers the skeptic, though here I have to be honest in a way a lifelong believer might not be, because I spent years certain there was no one to talk to. | cut | And prayer is not merely talking to yourself, which answers the skeptic, though here I have to be honest in a way a lifelong believer might not be, because I came to faith from atheism, certain there was no one to talk to. |
| new-believer | `sessions[2].teaching` | And let me tell you the hardest and best thing I ever did, as a man who came to all of this from atheism. The hardest thing was opening my mouth to a God I was not sure was there. It felt absurd. It felt like talking to an empty room, and every instinct I had trained for years told me I was performing a superstition. But I kept doing it, haltingly, saying the doubt out loud as part of the prayer, telling God I was not even sure he existed, and I want you to know that you are allowed to do that, that some of the most honest prayers in Scripture are people telling God they are not sure he is listening, and he kept those prayers in the book. I spoke into what felt like silence, and I kept speaking, and somewhere along the way the silence stopped feeling empty. I cannot hand you that across the table. | generalized | And here is one of the hardest and best things a doubter can do. Open your mouth to a God you are not sure is there. It will feel absurd. It will feel like talking to an empty room, and every instinct you have trained may tell you that you are performing a superstition. But keep doing it, haltingly, saying the doubt out loud as part of the prayer, telling God you are not even sure he exists, because you are allowed to do that, and some of the most honest prayers in Scripture are people telling God they are not sure he is listening, and he kept those prayers in the book. Many who have spoken into what felt like silence, and kept speaking, have found that somewhere along the way the silence stopped feeling empty. No one can hand you that across the table. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].summary` | A new believer told Bell he loved Jesus but was done with church, that he had been burned and would just follow God on his own from now on. | new-believer | `sessions[3].summary` | Bell, who came in as an outsider with no Christian background and every reason to keep his distance, says the people of God were the proof of the gospel he could not argue away, ordinary, flawed, and unaccountably kind to a stranger. | generalized | Bell says the people of God can be the proof of the gospel a skeptic cannot argue away, ordinary, flawed, and unaccountably kind to a stranger. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell came in as an outsider with no Christian background, and says the ordinary kindness of flawed church people was proof of the gospel he could not argue away. | generalized | Bell says the ordinary kindness of flawed church people can be proof of the gospel a skeptic cannot argue away. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].teaching` | A new believer said to Bell, with the particular firmness of someone defending a wound, that he loved Jesus but was finished with church, that he had been burned, had seen the hypocrisy from the inside, and would simply follow God on his own from here. Bell understood the wound completely and refused the conclusion entirely, and we have to learn to do both at once, because if we only validate the hurt we abandon the person to a slow starvation, and if we only argue for church we trample a real injury. | generalized | Many new believers will say, with the particular firmness of someone defending a wound, that they love Jesus but are finished with church, that they have been burned, have seen the hypocrisy from the inside, and will simply follow God on their own from here. The right response understands the wound completely and refuses the conclusion entirely, and we have to learn to do both at once, because if we only validate the hurt we abandon the person to a slow starvation, and if we only argue for church we trample a real injury. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].teaching` | There is no such thing, and Bell likes to say that if you ever found one, the moment you joined it would no longer qualify, because you would have brought your own imperfection through the door. | generalized | There is no such thing, and the old line holds, that if you ever found one, the moment you joined it would no longer qualify, because you would have brought your own imperfection through the door. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to tell you what finally undid my own resistance, because I came into all of this as a total outsider, an atheist with no Christian background, no childhood church, no warm memory to draw me in, and every intellectual reason to keep the religious people at arm's length. What I could not get around, what I genuinely could not argue away, was the people. Ordinary people, not impressive, not eloquent, often plainly flawed, who were unaccountably kind to a stranger who had nothing to offer them and made no secret of his doubt. They fed me. They sat with me. They told me the truth and kept coming back. I had arguments against the existence of God. I did not have an argument against a community that loved like that, for no reason that the world's economy could explain. The people of God were the proof of the gospel I could not refute. | generalized | And for many who come from outside, the thing they cannot get around, the thing they genuinely cannot argue away, is the people. Ordinary people, not impressive, not eloquent, often plainly flawed, who are unaccountably kind to a stranger who has nothing to offer them and makes no secret of his doubt. They feed him. They sit with him. They tell him the truth and keep coming back. A skeptic may have arguments against the existence of God. He rarely has an argument against a community that loves like that, for no reason that the world's economy can explain. The people of God are often the proof of the gospel a skeptic cannot refute. |
| new-believer | `sessions[3].scriptureStudy[3].note` | Jesus makes communal love the visible proof of discipleship, exactly the proof the outsider could not argue away. | generalized | Jesus makes communal love the visible proof of discipleship, exactly the proof an outsider cannot argue away. |
| new-believer | `sessions[4].summary` | A new believer asked Bell, after a few good weeks, what the goal was, when the arriving would happen, when he would be finished. The honest answer was that he had not signed up for an event, he had set out on a road. | generalized | After a few good weeks, many new believers ask what the goal is, when the arriving will happen, when they will be finished. The honest answer is that they have not signed up for an event, they have set out on a road. |
| new-believer | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell, decades in now, will tell you he has not arrived, that he still repents of the same things, that the road is longer than he thought and the Companion kinder. | generalized | Bell will not pretend he has arrived. Believers decades in still repent of the same things, and find the road longer than they thought and the Companion kinder. |
| new-believer | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell, decades in, says he has not arrived, still repents of the same things, and has found the road longer than he thought and the Companion kinder. | generalized | Believers decades in still repent of the same things, and find the road longer than they thought and the Companion kinder. |
| new-believer | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Bell will tell you plainly that decades in he still repents of the same things he repented of at the beginning. | generalized | Believers decades into the road will tell you plainly that they still repent of the same things they repented of at the beginning. |
| new-believer | `sessions[4].teaching` | After a few good weeks, a new believer asked Bell a question that sounds innocent and reveals everything. He wanted to know what the goal was. And the honest answer, the one that disappoints before it comforts, is that he had not signed up for an event. ### parenting-formation (25)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| parenting-formation | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, which means he began fathering with no map, no memory of a man who stayed, only a hole where the model should have been. He has five sons. He does not point from a safe distance. He has been the father who confused his child's performance with his own worth, who disciplined out of his own anger and called it correction, who tried to force a faith that can only be given, who let a screen become the easier thing to hand a boy, who gripped a growing son too tightly out of his own fear. | providence | `summary` | He came to God from atheism, and the problem of evil was the wall he could not get over for years. He has buried people he loved and prayed for healing that did not come. He may have done nothing wrong. | raising-believers | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he once answered a child's hard question badly, with a slogan, and lay awake about it. | generalized | Many parents have answered a child's hard question badly, with a slogan, and lain awake about it. |

| raising-believers | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits that with his own sons he has wanted to build the walls high and keep the hard world out, and has had to learn that the walls do not finally hold. | generalized | Many parents want to build the walls high and keep the hard world out, and have to learn that the walls do not finally hold. |
| raising-believers | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to tell you that my own instinct, with five sons, runs hard toward the walls. I want to build them high. I want to keep the cold world and its clever objections away from my boys, and I have had to learn, slowly and against everything in me, that the walls do not finally hold, that the most loving thing is not to keep my sons in a perfect climate but to give them roots deep enough to stand in a hard one, and to let them feel a little of the cold while I am still here to stand with them in it. | generalized | And for most parents the instinct runs hard toward the walls. We want to build them high. We want to keep the cold world and its clever objections away from our children, and we have to learn, slowly and against everything in us, that the walls do not finally hold, that the most loving thing is not to keep our children in a perfect climate but to give them roots deep enough to stand in a hard one, and to let them feel a little of the cold while we are still here to stand with them in it. |
| raising-believers | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this last and most, because with five sons I have more to entrust than most, and my hands do not want to open. I have parented from fear and called it love. I have gripped my boys as though my grip were what kept them, and I have had to learn, am still learning, that the grip is the problem, that the most faithful thing I can do is the thing that feels most like failure, to open my hands and give my sons back to the God who gave them to me, who loves them more than I am able to, and who can reach them in any far country I cannot follow them into. | generalized | And I have to stand inside this last and most, because with five sons I have more to entrust than most. Every parent who has parented from fear and called it love, who has gripped a child as though the grip were what kept them, has to learn, and keep learning, that the grip is the problem, that the most faithful thing a parent can do is the thing that feels most like failure, to open the hands and give the children back to the God who gave them, who loves them more than we are able to, and who can reach them in any far country we cannot follow them into. |
| raising-believers | `leaderPrimer` | The writer of this study is raising five sons and getting it wrong regularly, and he was himself raised with no father to hand him anything, which taught him the truth this study rests on, that God parents the unparented and is not limited by what a household manages to transmit. | cut | The writer of this study is raising five sons, and he was himself raised without a father, and the study rests on the truth that God parents the unparented and is not limited by what a household manages to transmit. |
