### resurrection (15)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| resurrection | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he did not arrive by feeling. He arrived by being unable, finally, to explain the evidence any other way, and even then with doubt still in the room. He is not standing outside the skeptic's position lecturing it. He held it. He argued it. He is writing back to the man he used to be. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism. He is not standing outside the skeptic's position lecturing it. He held it. He is writing back to the man he used to be. |
| resurrection | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell says he held the skeptic's position himself, argued it, and did not leave it by feeling but by being unable to explain the evidence away. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he asks that the resurrection be weighed as evidence rather than settled by feeling. |
| resurrection | `sessions[0].teaching` | I held that position myself, for years. I came to faith out of atheism, and I did not arrive by sentiment or by a warm feeling at a service. I argued against this claim. I assumed, as many honest people do, that it was the kind of thing you either believed or you did not, a matter of the heart and not of the evidence, sealed off in a private compartment where reasons do not reach. So I am not standing outside the skeptic's position lecturing it from a safe distance. I lived inside it. I am writing back to the man I used to be. But something finally cracked that compartment open for me, and it is the thing I want to set before you tonight, because it is strange, and the strangeness is the point. | cut | I came to faith out of atheism, so I am not standing outside the skeptic's position lecturing it from a safe distance. I am writing back to the man I used to be. Many honest people assume that this is the kind of thing you either believe or you do not, a matter of the heart and not of the evidence, sealed off in a private compartment where reasons do not reach. But something cracks that compartment open, and it is the thing I want to set before you tonight, because it is strange, and the strangeness is the point. |
| resurrection | `sessions[1].teaching` | Here is the move that changed how I thought about this, back when I was the one arguing against it. You do not have to believe the Bible is the inspired word of God to ask the historical question. For years I assumed you did, that the whole case for the resurrection rested on first accepting Scripture as divinely authoritative, which made the argument circular and let me dismiss it without effort. | generalized | Here is a move that changes how many skeptics think about this. You do not have to believe the Bible is the inspired word of God to ask the historical question. Many assume you do, that the whole case for the resurrection rests on first accepting Scripture as divinely authoritative, which makes the argument circular and lets them dismiss it without effort. |
| resurrection | `sessions[2].teaching` | That is a strong theory. I have felt its pull. | generalized | That is a strong theory. Many skeptics have felt its pull. |
| resurrection | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits that as a skeptic he expected the gospels to read like propaganda and was unsettled to find them keeping the embarrassing details instead. | generalized | Many skeptics expect the gospels to read like propaganda and are unsettled to find them keeping the embarrassing details instead. |
| resurrection | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to be honest about what this did to me, because it is part of why I am no longer where I was. As a skeptic, I assumed I knew what the gospels were before I had really read them. I assumed they were propaganda, ancient spin, a smooth and self-serving legend constructed to sell a religion, and I dismissed them on that basis without much actual reading. And then I read them, finally, as documents, and they did not read the way propaganda reads. They were full of the wrong details, the embarrassing ones, the fearful disciples and the denying leader and the doubting apostles and the women whose word the culture would not credit, all kept in, none of it tidied up. That texture unsettled me, because it is not the texture of invention. | generalized | And this is worth saying plainly to the skeptic, because it is where many skeptics stop being where they were. Many assume they know what the gospels are before they have really read them. They assume they are propaganda, ancient spin, a smooth and self-serving legend constructed to sell a religion, and dismiss them on that basis without much actual reading. But read them as documents and they do not read the way propaganda reads. They are full of the wrong details, the embarrassing ones, the fearful disciples and the denying leader and the doubting apostles and the women whose word the culture would not credit, all kept in, none of it tidied up. That texture should unsettle a skeptic, because it is not the texture of invention. |
| resurrection | `sessions[3].teaching` | But it is a reason, a real one, to do the thing the skeptic in me finally did, which is to stop dismissing these documents unread and start reading them for what they actually are, testimony, with all the stubborn awkwardness of memory on it, to be weighed like any other evidence, honestly, and on its own strange and unflattering terms. | cut | But it is a reason, a real one, for a skeptic to stop dismissing these documents unread and start reading them for what they actually are, testimony, with all the stubborn awkwardness of memory on it, to be weighed like any other evidence, honestly, and on its own strange and unflattering terms. |
| resurrection | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Bell came to faith from atheism, and no one closed him like a deal. He was unable, finally, to explain the evidence away, and even then it took time, and there was doubt in the room when he decided, and there still is some. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he will not close anyone like a deal. |
| resurrection | `sessions[4].teaching` | No one closed me like a deal. No one pressed me into a decision in a heightened moment. What happened to me was slower and stranger and harder. I found, over time, that I could not explain the evidence away, that every natural account I reached for explained some of the facts and broke on others, and that the explanation I had spent years refusing was, in the end, the one that bore the weight of all of them at once. And even then I did not arrive in triumph. I arrived with doubt still in the room, and there is some there still, because honest faith and honest doubt are not as far apart as either side likes to pretend. | cut | I will not close you like a deal or press you into a decision in a heightened moment. The only conclusion worth reaching about a thing this large is a slow one, reached by finding, over time, whether the evidence can be explained away, and honest faith and honest doubt are not as far apart as either side likes to pretend. |
| resurrection | `sessions[4].teaching` | And whichever way you finally land, land there by weighing, in freedom, in your own time, with your own mind, the way the first Christians dared you to, and the way the man I used to be finally, and against all his expectations, did. | cut | And whichever way you finally land, land there by weighing, in freedom, in your own time, with your own mind, the way the first Christians dared you to. |
| resurrection | `leaderPrimer` | Bell examined this evidence as an atheist trying to explain it away, and he could not, and he walked into the faith through that failure with doubt still in his luggage. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he writes as a weigher, not a prosecutor. |
| resurrection | `faq[0].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism and says plainly that no one closed him like a deal, that he arrived slowly, against his own expectations, and with doubt still in the room. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he will not close anyone like a deal. |
| resurrection | `faq[3].a` | The writer held the skeptic's position himself, argued it, and left it only when he could not explain the evidence away, so he writes back to the skeptic as someone who knows the position from the inside, not as someone dismissing it from a distance. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, so he writes back to the skeptic as someone who knows the position from the inside, not as someone dismissing it from a distance. |
| resurrection | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer of this study came to faith from atheism, and he did not arrive by feeling or by pressure. He argued against this. He believes now, but as a man who knows the skeptic's position from the inside and keeps some doubt in the room. So will I. | cut | Third, the writer of this study came to faith from atheism, and he does not ask anyone to leave their doubt at the door. Neither will I. |

### sabbath (13)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| sabbath | `summary` | Bell is terrible at this. He is a pastor whose one day off bleeds into hospital visits and half-answered emails, who has preached on rest with a phone buzzing in his pocket, who has called his inability to stop a calling. He came to faith from atheism, where work was the only god on offer and you were what you made. He was raised without a father, which taught him early that the weight was his alone to carry and no one was coming to lift it. He has five sons who needed a father who could stop, and a God who keeps inviting him to a rest he keeps declining. | cut | Bell is a pastor, a vocation that is supposed to know something about rest, and he does not exempt himself from the refusal to stop that this study names. He came to faith from atheism, he was raised without a father, and he has five sons. |
| sabbath | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to climb inside this before I say one more word, because I am the chief offender in any room I lead on this subject. I am a pastor, which is supposed to mean something about rest, and I am terrible at it. My one day off bleeds into hospital visits and half-answered emails and a phone I cannot leave in another room. I have preached entire sermons on the gift of rest with the thing buzzing in my pocket the whole time. I came to faith out of atheism, where the only god on offer was work and you were precisely and only what you produced, and I have never fully stopped bowing to that god even after I stopped believing in him. I was raised without a father, which taught me young that the weight was mine alone, that no one was coming to lift it, that if I set it down it would crush whatever was beneath it. So when I refuse to rest, I dress it up. I call it diligence. I call it sacrifice. I call it my calling. | generalized | And we have to climb inside this before another word is said, because pastors are often the chief offenders on this subject. A pastor is supposed to know something about rest, and many are terrible at it, the one day off bleeding into hospital visits and half-answered emails and a phone that cannot be left in another room, whole sermons on the gift of rest preached with the thing buzzing in a pocket. Most of us have bowed at some point to the god whose only doctrine is that you are precisely and only what you produce, and we keep bowing even after we stop believing in him. So when we refuse to rest, we dress it up. We call it diligence. We call it sacrifice. We call it a calling. |
| sabbath | `sessions[1].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits he measures his value by his usefulness and cannot rest because resting makes him feel worthless. | generalized | Many of us measure our value by our usefulness and cannot rest because resting makes us feel worthless. |
| sabbath | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I cannot teach this from a clean place, because I am as enslaved to it as anyone in the room, maybe more. I came to faith from atheism, and in that world the only god available was work, and the entire doctrine of that god was that you are what you produce, full stop, no soul, no Sabbath, no rest, just output until you stop and then you are nothing. I stopped believing in that god, but I never fully stopped serving him. I was raised without a father, and the lesson a fatherless boy learns early and deep is that the weight is yours alone, that nobody is coming to carry it, that if you put it down it falls and you with it. So rest, for me, has never felt like obedience. It has felt like negligence, like dropping something I am responsible to hold. I measure my worth by my usefulness, and so when I am not being useful I do not feel restful, I feel worthless, which is exactly why I cannot stop. | generalized | And I cannot teach this from a clean place, because none of us stands outside it. Many of us serve the god whose entire doctrine is that you are what you produce, full stop, no soul, no Sabbath, no rest, just output until you stop and then you are nothing, and we keep serving him long after we stop believing in him. So rest has never felt like obedience. It has felt like negligence, like dropping something we are responsible to hold. We measure our worth by our usefulness, and so when we are not being useful we do not feel restful, we feel worthless, which is exactly why we cannot stop. |
| sabbath | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he keeps treating rest as a discipline to master rather than a person to come to. | generalized | Many of us keep treating rest as a discipline to master rather than a person to come to. |
| sabbath | `sessions[2].discussion[4].behind` | Bell confesses he tries to be good at Sabbath as one more achievement instead of receiving Christ as his rest. | generalized | Many of us try to be good at Sabbath as one more achievement instead of receiving Christ as our rest. |
| sabbath | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here is where I have to indict myself again, because I am a Pharisee about this more than I would like to admit. I do not turn the Sabbath into a cage for other people. I turn it into a cage for myself. I try to be good at rest. I read the books on Sabbath, I make the rules for my own day off, I evaluate whether I rested correctly, and when I fail I feel the guilt of a man who has broken a rule, which means I have made the gift into a test all over again, just a private one. I keep treating rest as a discipline to master, one more thing to achieve and be graded on, when Jesus is standing there saying the rest is not a thing you achieve, it is a person you come to. I do not need to get better at Sabbath. I need to come to its Lord. And so do you. | generalized | And here is where we have to indict ourselves, because most of us are Pharisees about this more than we would like to admit. We may not turn the Sabbath into a cage for other people. We turn it into a cage for ourselves. We try to be good at rest. We read the books on Sabbath, make the rules for our own day off, evaluate whether we rested correctly, and when we fail we feel the guilt of someone who has broken a rule, which means we have made the gift into a test all over again, just a private one. We keep treating rest as a discipline to master, one more thing to achieve and be graded on, when Jesus is standing there saying the rest is not a thing you achieve, it is a person you come to. We do not need to get better at Sabbath. We need to come to its Lord. |
| sabbath | `sessions[3].discussion[4].behind` | Bell admits his own day off bleeds away because he will not defend it. | generalized | Many of us let the day off bleed away because we will not defend it. |
| sabbath | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here, as always, I am preaching to myself before anyone else, and failing in real time. My day off is a theory I admire and a practice I do not defend. It starts with good intentions and bleeds away by ten in the morning, a hospital call here, an urgent email there, a sermon idea I cannot leave alone, until the day is gone and I have stopped for nothing, and I tell myself it was unavoidable, that ministry is just like this, that the needs do not wait. But the truth is I do not defend the day because some part of me does not want it defended. I am afraid of the quiet. I was raised without a father, and a fatherless boy learns to fill every silence with motion because the silence is where the absence lives, where the questions with no answers wait, and I have spent a lifetime outrunning that quiet with productive noise. And I have five sons who have needed a father who could stop, who could be still and present and unproductive with them, and I have too often given them a man in motion instead, a man answering one more thing, and I will answer for that. So when I tell you it takes courage to be unproductive, I am not speaking from a height. I am speaking as a man who has run from the quiet his whole life and is only now, slowly, learning to stop. | generalized | And here, as always, I am preaching to myself before anyone else. For many of us the day off is a theory we admire and a practice we do not defend. It starts with good intentions and bleeds away by ten in the morning, a call here, an urgent email there, an idea we cannot leave alone, until the day is gone and we have stopped for nothing, and we tell ourselves it was unavoidable, that the work is just like this, that the needs do not wait. But the truth is we do not defend the day because some part of us does not want it defended. We are afraid of the quiet, because the silence is where the absences live, where the questions with no answers wait, and many of us have spent a lifetime outrunning that quiet with productive noise. And the people we love, our children most of all, need someone who can stop, who can be still and present and unproductive with them, and too often we give them someone in motion instead, answering one more thing. So when I say it takes courage to be unproductive, I am not speaking from a height. I am speaking as one who knows how easy it is to run from the quiet. |
| sabbath | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to end where I began, inside my own failure, because I am still learning to enter this rest and I am not there. I came to faith from atheism, where there was no finished work, only the work I could manage before I ran out of time, and the deepest habit of that old self has never fully died. I keep picking the burden back up. I keep trying to justify my existence with one more sermon, one more book, one more soul helped, one more thing done well, as if the verdict were still open, as if it is finished had not already been spoken over me. I was raised without a father, and the project of proving I was worth something began so early I cannot remember a time before it, and laying it down feels like falling, like dropping the one thing that has held me up. But the whole testimony of this study, and the whole testimony of the gospel, is that the thing holding me up was never my striving. It was always the finished work of Christ, the rest that has been standing open since the seventh day, and my striving has only ever kept me from entering it. | generalized | And I have to end where I began, inside the failure, because I am still learning to enter this rest and I am not there. Most of us keep picking the burden back up. We keep trying to justify our existence with one more task, one more achievement, one more person helped, one more thing done well, as if the verdict were still open, as if it is finished had not already been spoken over us. For many, the project of proving they are worth something began so early they cannot remember a time before it, and laying it down feels like falling, like dropping the one thing that has held them up. But the whole testimony of this study, and the whole testimony of the gospel, is that the thing holding us up was never our striving. It was always the finished work of Christ, the rest that has been standing open since the seventh day, and our striving has only ever kept us from entering it. |
| sabbath | `leaderPrimer` | Bell is a pastor, a vocation that is supposed to know something about rest, and he is honest that he is bad at it, the day off bleeding into hospital calls, the phone buzzing through his own sermons on stopping. Raised without a father, he learned early that the weight was his alone and no one was coming to lift it, so setting it down has felt like negligence ever since, and his five sons have too often gotten a man in motion when they needed a father who could be present and unproductive. If any of that is your story, say so, because a leader performing a balance he does not have will empty this study of its power by the second week. | cut | Bell is a pastor, a vocation that is supposed to know something about rest, and he does not exempt himself from the refusal to stop that this study names. If that refusal is your story too, say so, because a leader performing a balance he does not have will empty this study of its power by the second week. |
| sabbath | `faq[0].a` | The study names our refusal to rest honestly, including as a failure of trust and a hidden idolatry of our own output, but it does so starting with the writer, who confesses he is terrible at this, not by pointing down at the tired. | generalized | The study names our refusal to rest honestly, including as a failure of trust and a hidden idolatry of our own output, but it does so starting with the writer, who does not exempt himself, not by pointing down at the tired. |
| sabbath | `faq[8].a` | He is a pastor whose day off bleeds away into work, who has preached on rest with a phone buzzing in his pocket, who came to faith from atheism where work was the only god and never fully stopped serving it, who was raised without a father and learned young that the weight was his alone to carry, who has five sons who needed a father who could stop. | cut | He is a pastor, he came to faith from atheism, he was raised without a father, and he has five sons, and he does not exempt himself from the refusal to stop that this study names. |

### sermon-on-the-mount (18)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| sermon-on-the-mount | `summary` | He has nursed an anger he called principle. He has loved his money and called it stewardship. He has built on sand and decorated the house so no one could tell. He preaches this sermon as a man it convicts. | generalized | Most of us know the anger nursed and called principle, the money loved and called stewardship, the house built on sand and decorated so no one could tell. He preaches this sermon as a man it convicts. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside the indictment, because I preach this kingdom upside down and I keep living right side up. I say I believe the meek inherit the earth, and I grab. I say the poor in spirit are blessed, and I come to God with my hands full, rehearsing my résumé, trying to be rich in spirit, trying to bring him something that will make me worth keeping. I chase the security and the standing and the being right that this sermon does not call blessed even once, and I call it responsibility. | generalized | And here we have to stand inside the indictment, because we preach and sing this kingdom upside down and keep living right side up. We say we believe the meek inherit the earth, and we grab. We say the poor in spirit are blessed, and we come to God with our hands full, rehearsing our résumés, trying to be rich in spirit, trying to bring him something that will make us worth keeping. We chase the security and the standing and the being right that this sermon does not call blessed even once, and we call it responsibility. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[0].caseStudy` | A man who had clawed his way up from nothing came to Bell after a service, successful by every measure the world keeps, and said he could not understand why the Beatitudes left him cold. He had read them many times. He admired them. But blessed are the poor in spirit, he said, felt like a foreign country, because everything in his life had been built on the opposite, on confidence, on bringing something to the table, on never being caught empty-handed. He had made himself rich in spirit on purpose, and it had worked, and now the words of Jesus would not land. Bell did not tell him to try harder to feel poor in spirit, because that would only have been one more achievement. He asked the man instead where, in his whole self-made life, he had ever once come to anyone, God included, with nothing to bring. The man could not name a place. That, Bell said gently, is not a character flaw to fix. It is the door you have never walked through, and the kingdom is on the other side of it, and it only opens to the empty-handed. | generalized | A man in your group who clawed his way up from nothing, successful by every measure the world keeps, says he cannot understand why the Beatitudes leave him cold. He has read them many times. He admires them. But blessed are the poor in spirit, he says, feels like a foreign country, because everything in his life has been built on the opposite, on confidence, on bringing something to the table, on never being caught empty-handed. He has made himself rich in spirit on purpose, and it has worked, and now the words of Jesus will not land. Do not tell him to try harder to feel poor in spirit, because that would only be one more achievement. Ask him instead where, in his whole self-made life, he has ever once come to anyone, God included, with nothing to bring. If he cannot name a place, say gently that this is not a character flaw to fix. It is the door he has never walked through, and the kingdom is on the other side of it, and it only opens to the empty-handed. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[1].teaching` | Now there are two wrong ways to receive this, and I have tried both. | generalized | Now there are two wrong ways to receive this, and most of us have tried both. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess where this lands on me, because it lands hard. I have nursed angers I called principle, contempt I dressed up as discernment, a settled wish that certain people were smaller, and I have congratulated myself for never raising a hand. I have used people in my mind and kept my record outwardly clean and called myself faithful. The interior law finds me out, and that is mercy, even when it does not feel like it. Because the point is not to leave me crushed and it is not to let me cheat. The point is to drive me, exposed and honest, to the only One who can give a man a new heart, since I have proven beyond argument that I cannot manufacture one myself. | generalized | And we have to confess where this lands on us, because it lands hard. Many of us have nursed angers we called principle, contempt we dressed up as discernment, a settled wish that certain people were smaller, and congratulated ourselves for never raising a hand. We have used people in our minds and kept our records outwardly clean and called ourselves faithful. The interior law finds us out, and that is mercy, even when it does not feel like it. Because the point is not to leave us crushed and it is not to let us cheat. The point is to drive us, exposed and honest, to the only One who can give a person a new heart, since we have proven beyond argument that we cannot manufacture one ourselves. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[1].caseStudy` | A woman in a group Bell once led was, by every visible measure, a model of self-control. She did not gossip. She did not lose her temper. She was the one everyone described as gracious. And in the session on the deeper law she went quiet, and afterward she told Bell the truth she had never said aloud, that beneath the calm surface she kept a private ledger of contempt, a running verdict on nearly everyone she met, a settled wish that certain people would simply be diminished, and that she had spent years priding herself on never letting any of it show. She had kept the outside spotless. Jesus, she said, had just told her the inside was where she actually lived, and the inside was a war. Bell did not rush to comfort her, because the exposure was the mercy. He told her that the contempt was not worse for having been seen, it had been there the whole time, and that a God who only judged the surface would have left it to rot. The naming was the first thing that had ever made healing possible. | generalized | A woman in your group is, by every visible measure, a model of self-control. She does not gossip. She does not lose her temper. She is the one everyone describes as gracious. And in the session on the deeper law she goes quiet, and afterward she tells you the truth she has never said aloud, that beneath the calm surface she keeps a private ledger of contempt, a running verdict on nearly everyone she meets, a settled wish that certain people would simply be diminished, and that she has spent years priding herself on never letting any of it show. She has kept the outside spotless. Jesus, she says, has just told her the inside is where she actually lives, and the inside is a war. Do not rush to comfort her, because the exposure is the mercy. Tell her that the contempt is not worse for having been seen, it has been there the whole time, and that a God who only judged the surface would have left it to rot. The naming is the first thing that makes healing possible. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because I am so quick to keep an enemy. I have nursed grudges like heirlooms. I have returned the blow and called it standing up for myself, and felt the vengeance deform me even as I justified it, and I have decided, quietly, that certain people are simply beyond the reach of my love, which is to say I have set myself up as a more selective lover than God. | generalized | And here we have to stand inside it, because most of us are so quick to keep an enemy. We nurse grudges like heirlooms. We return the blow and call it standing up for ourselves, and feel the vengeance deform us even as we justify it, and we decide, quietly, that certain people are simply beyond the reach of our love, which is to say we set ourselves up as more selective lovers than God. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[2].caseStudy` | A man told Bell he had been betrayed by a business partner who took what they had built together, lied about it, and walked away unscathed and prospering, while the man lost nearly everything. For years he had rehearsed the betrayal at night, refined his case, kept his contempt sharp, and called it justice. He could not understand why the wound would not heal, and he wanted Bell to agree that his hatred was righteous. Bell told him the hatred might be understandable and was still killing him, that he had let his betrayer go on shaping who he became long after the betrayal ended, and that the love Jesus commanded was not a feeling of warmth toward the man but a refusal to let the vengeance own his soul any longer. Bell did not tell him to pretend the wrong was small or to forgo any lawful claim. He told him to begin praying for the man's actual good, by name, not because he felt it but because the prayer was the only thing that had ever loosened a grip like that. Months later the man said the situation had not changed and he had, that the prayer he could barely choke out at first had slowly stopped the bleeding. | generalized | A man in your group was betrayed by a business partner who took what they had built together, lied about it, and walked away unscathed and prospering, while the man lost nearly everything. For years he has rehearsed the betrayal at night, refined his case, kept his contempt sharp, and called it justice. He cannot understand why the wound will not heal, and he wants the group to agree that his hatred is righteous. Tell him the hatred may be understandable and is still killing him, that he has let his betrayer go on shaping who he becomes long after the betrayal ended, and that the love Jesus commands is not a feeling of warmth toward the man but a refusal to let the vengeance own his soul any longer. Do not tell him to pretend the wrong was small or to forgo any lawful claim. Invite him to begin praying for the man's actual good, by name, not because he feels it but because that prayer is how a grip like his begins to loosen. The situation may not change, and he may. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he seeks the security first and tells himself he will get to the kingdom once it is nailed down. | generalized | Many of us seek the security first and tell ourselves we will get to the kingdom once it is nailed down. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I am as divided as anyone. I have asked money to be my security and called it stewardship. I have measured my worth by what I had and called it success. I seek the rest first, the cushion and the plan and the margin, and I tell myself that once those are nailed down I will really pursue the kingdom, which is exactly the order Jesus forbids. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us are as divided as anyone. We have asked money to be our security and called it stewardship. We have measured our worth by what we had and called it success. We seek the rest first, the cushion and the plan and the margin, and tell ourselves that once those are nailed down we will really pursue the kingdom, which is exactly the order Jesus forbids. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[3].caseStudy` | A couple in a study Bell led were generous givers, careful savers, debt-free, and by any reasonable standard responsible with money, and they came to him troubled, because the session on treasure had unsettled them and they could not say why. They did not love money, they insisted. They were just being wise. Bell asked them a single question. What were they most afraid would happen, in the quiet of the night, the fear that drove the careful planning. They admitted, slowly, that underneath all the prudence sat a low constant dread that it would not be enough, that something would come and take it all, and that they checked the accounts the way some people check a lock, not out of need but to feel the floor was still there. That dread, Bell said, is the tell. The money was not the sin. The job they had handed it, to be the floor under their lives, the proof that they would be all right, was the sin, because that floor belongs to God alone, and money has never once been able to hold it. He did not tell them to stop saving. He asked them to give away, that month, an amount large enough to feel, as a way of telling the truth about who the floor really was. | generalized | A couple in your group are generous givers, careful savers, debt-free, and by any reasonable standard responsible with money, and they come to you troubled, because the session on treasure has unsettled them and they cannot say why. They do not love money, they insist. They are just being wise. Ask them a single question. What are they most afraid will happen, in the quiet of the night, the fear that drives the careful planning. If they admit, slowly, that underneath all the prudence sits a low constant dread that it will not be enough, that something will come and take it all, and that they check the accounts the way some people check a lock, not out of need but to feel the floor is still there, name that dread as the tell. The money is not the sin. The job they have handed it, to be the floor under their lives, the proof that they will be all right, is the sin, because that floor belongs to God alone, and money has never once been able to hold it. Do not tell them to stop saving. Ask them to give away, this month, an amount large enough to feel, as a way of telling the truth about who the floor really is. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to walk myself up to this gate and tell the truth, because I have loved the wide road. I have wanted the grace that forgives without the grace that changes. I have judged specks with a log in my eye and called it standing for truth. I have prayed faithlessly, as though God begrudged me. And I have, more times than I want to admit, chosen the easy, agreeable, costless version of following Jesus and called it the real thing, because the broad road is comfortable and crowded and the narrow one is lonely and hard. The sermon will not let me stay there. | generalized | And we have to walk ourselves up to this gate and tell the truth, because most of us have loved the wide road. We have wanted the grace that forgives without the grace that changes. We have judged specks with a log in our eye and called it standing for truth. We have prayed faithlessly, as though God begrudged us. And we have, more times than we want to admit, chosen the easy, agreeable, costless version of following Jesus and called it the real thing, because the broad road is comfortable and crowded and the narrow one is lonely and hard. The sermon will not let us stay there. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[4].caseStudy` | A longtime church member told Bell, almost proudly, that he had never had a crisis of faith, never doubted, never struggled, that following Jesus had cost him nothing and felt entirely natural. He expected Bell to be glad. Instead Bell sat with it, troubled, and finally asked him a gentle and unwelcome question. | generalized | A longtime church member in your group says, almost proudly, that he has never had a crisis of faith, never doubted, never struggled, that following Jesus has cost him nothing and feels entirely natural. He expects the room to be glad. Instead, sit with it, and ask him a gentle and unwelcome question. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[4].caseStudy` | The man bristled, then went quiet. The wide gate, Bell told him later, is not marked rebellion. | generalized | He may bristle, then go quiet. The wide gate is not marked rebellion. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[5].teaching` | And here, finally, is where I have to stand inside it, because this is the most exposing word in the whole sermon for a man like me, a man who has spent his life hearing these words and handling these words and preaching these words. I have built houses that look fine in the sunshine. I have admired this sermon from the pulpit and not done it in the kitchen. I have heard the command to love my enemy and nursed my grudge, heard the warning about treasure and clutched my security, heard the call to the narrow gate and strolled the wide one, and decorated the front of the house so beautifully that no one, sometimes not even me, could see there was sand underneath. And the rain is coming for my house too. | generalized | And here, finally, is where I have to stand inside it, because this is the most exposing word in the whole sermon for anyone who handles and preaches these words. Most of us have built houses that look fine in the sunshine. We have admired this sermon from the pulpit and the pew and not done it in the kitchen. We have heard the command to love our enemy and nursed our grudge, heard the warning about treasure and clutched our security, heard the call to the narrow gate and strolled the wide one, and decorated the front of the house so beautifully that no one, sometimes not even we ourselves, could see there was sand underneath. And the rain is coming for every house, mine too. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `sessions[5].caseStudy` | A woman who had attended church faithfully for thirty years sat in the final session of a study like this one and wept, not from sorrow but from a kind of recognition. She had heard the Sermon on the Mount preached more times than she could count, could recite much of it, had taught it to children. And she realized, sitting there, that she had been a hearer for three decades and could not name the place where the hearing had become doing. Her house, she said, looked beautiful. She had decorated the front of it for thirty years. She had no idea what was underneath. Bell did not tell her she was lost, because that was not his to say and not the point. He told her the storm had not yet come for her in the way it eventually comes for everyone, and that the gift of this moment was that she could find out now, before the rain, what she had been building on, while there was still time to build differently. He pointed her back to the first beatitude, that the rock is not the quality of the house but Christ underneath it, and that coming to him poor in spirit was not the end of thirty years but the foundation she could finally lay. | generalized | A woman in your group who has attended church faithfully for thirty years sits in the final session and weeps, not from sorrow but from a kind of recognition. She has heard the Sermon on the Mount preached more times than she can count, can recite much of it, has taught it to children. And she realizes, sitting there, that she has been a hearer for three decades and cannot name the place where the hearing became doing. Her house, she says, looks beautiful. She has decorated the front of it for thirty years. She has no idea what is underneath. Do not tell her she is lost, because that is not yours to say and not the point. Tell her the storm has not yet come for her in the way it eventually comes for everyone, and that the gift of this moment is that she can find out now, before the rain, what she has been building on, while there is still time to build differently. Point her back to the first beatitude, that the rock is not the quality of the house but Christ underneath it, and that coming to him poor in spirit is not the end of thirty years but the foundation she can finally lay. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `leaderPrimer` | Bell preaches this sermon as a man it convicts, an angry man who has called his anger principle, a man who has loved money and filed the love under stewardship. | generalized | Bell preaches this sermon as a man it convicts, and he does not exempt himself from the anger called principle or the love of money filed under stewardship. |
| sermon-on-the-mount | `faq[9].a` | He has nursed an anger he called principle, loved his money and called it stewardship, judged a speck with a log in his own eye, walked the wide road, and built houses that look fine in fair weather. | generalized | He does not exempt himself from the anger called principle, the money loved and called stewardship, the speck judged with a log in the eye, the wide road, the house that looks fine in fair weather. |

### singleness (23)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| singleness | `summary` | This study is written by a married man, and that is exactly the problem it means to confront first. Bell is a pastor with a wife and five sons, and he writes from inside the honest admission that men like him have built the church for men like him. He has planned the sermon series around marriage and the potluck around families and the small group around couples, and he has left the single people in his church to feel like the meeting had not started yet, like they were waiting for their real life to begin. He does not point at that failure from a safe distance. He is the one who committed it. So he writes not as an authority on a life he has not lived but as a brother repenting of a blindness he did live, and he lets the single voices in Scripture, Paul and Jesus and the prophet Jeremiah, carry the weight he cannot. | cut | This study is written by a pastor, and the church pastors have built is exactly the problem it means to confront first. Bell is a pastor with five sons, and he writes from inside the honest admission that the church has been built around couples and families. Pastors have planned the sermon series around marriage and the potluck around families and the small group around couples, and left the single people in their churches to feel like the meeting had not started yet, like they were waiting for their real life to begin. He does not point at that failure from a safe distance. He writes as a brother repenting of a blindness the whole church has lived, and he lets the single voices in Scripture, Paul and Jesus and the prophet Jeremiah, carry the weight. |
| singleness | `sessions[0].summary` | A woman in her late thirties told Bell, after another wedding where she had been seated at the table in the back with the other unmarried guests, that she had begun to feel like a problem the church did not know how to solve. She was describing a message she had received a hundred small ways, that the single life is a phase you pass through on the way to the real thing, and that until you arrive you are somehow unfinished. | generalized | Many single adults, seated again at the table in the back with the other unmarried guests at another wedding, begin to feel like a problem the church does not know how to solve. They are not bitter. They are describing a message they have received a hundred small ways, that the single life is a phase you pass through on the way to the real thing, and that until you arrive you are somehow unfinished. |
| singleness | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that as a married pastor he has built the church around couples and families and left single people feeling like the meeting had not started yet. | generalized | Pastors and churches have built church life around couples and families and left single people feeling like the meeting had not started yet. |
| singleness | `sessions[0].teaching` | A woman in her late thirties told Bell, after another wedding where she had again been seated at the table in the back with the other unmarried guests, that she had begun to feel like a problem the church did not know how to solve. She is reporting, accurately, a message she has received in a hundred small and unintended ways, that the single life is a phase you pass through on the road to the real thing, and that until you reach it you are somehow unfinished, a sentence with no period, a house with the framing up and no one living in it. |
| singleness | `sessions[0].teaching` | I am a married pastor with a wife and five sons, and men like me built the church for men like me. | singleness | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have to say plainly that as a married man I have been part of the machinery of the first lie. | singleness | `sessions[2].teaching` | The demand is heavy, heaviest of all for the person who did not choose the single life and did not want it, and I will not stand here as a married man and pretend the cost is small. | cut | The demand is heavy, heaviest of all for the person who did not choose the single life and did not want it, and I will not stand here and pretend the cost is small. |

### suffering (20)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| suffering | `summary` | The writer came to faith out of atheism, was raised without a father, and has buried people he loved and sat with people who wanted to die, and he does not point at pain from a safe distance. He has prayed the prayer that was answered with silence. He has offered a grieving person the sentence he later wished he could take back. | cut | The writer came to faith out of atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not point at pain from a safe distance. Most of us have prayed the prayer that was answered with silence, and offered a grieving person a sentence we later wished we could take back. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].summary` | A woman sat across from Bell a week after her husband's funeral and said the thing people are not supposed to say in a church, that she did not want comfort, she wanted an explanation, and that if God had one she would like to hear it now. He did not have one to give her. | generalized | People a week past a funeral often say the thing no one is supposed to say in a church, that they do not want comfort, they want an explanation, and that if God has one they would like to hear it now. No one has one to give them. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell takes the strong case for the atheist here, the one he once made himself, that a world with this much undeserved agony in it is hard to square with a God who is both good and able. | cut | Bell takes the strong case for the atheist here, that a world with this much undeserved agony in it is hard to square with a God who is both good and able. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell says he has offered a grieving person a sentence he later wished he could take back. | generalized | Most of us have offered a grieving person a sentence we later wished we could take back. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].teaching` | A woman sat across from Bell a week after burying her husband and said the sentence you are not supposed to say inside a church. She said she did not want comfort. She wanted an explanation, and if God had one, she would like to hear it now. He did not have one to give her, and the honest thing was to say so and stay in the chair rather than reach for something to fill the silence. | generalized | People a week past a funeral often say the sentence you are not supposed to say inside a church. They do not want comfort. They want an explanation, and if God has one, they would like to hear it now. No one has one to give them, and the honest thing is to say so and stay in the chair rather than reach for something to fill the silence. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].teaching` | Take the strong case for the other side now, the atheist's case, because I once made it myself before I believed any of this, and it deserves to be stated at full strength rather than knocked down as a cartoon. | cut | Take the strong case for the other side now, the atheist's case, because it deserves to be stated at full strength rather than knocked down as a cartoon. |
| suffering | `sessions[0].teaching` | I have to stand inside this, because I have been the friend more than I have been Job. I have sat beside real grief and reached for the sentence that made me more comfortable and the other person more alone. I have explained a pain that only needed company. The temptation to answer is almost always the temptation to flee, to fix the ache so I do not have to feel it with you. And the gospel underneath this whole study is that God did the opposite of what I keep doing. | generalized | We have to stand inside this, because most of us have been the friend more than we have been Job. We have sat beside real grief and reached for the sentence that made us more comfortable and the other person more alone. We have explained a pain that only needed company. The temptation to answer is almost always the temptation to flee, to fix the ache so we do not have to feel it with someone. And the gospel underneath this whole study is that God did the opposite of what we keep doing. |
| suffering | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know that fear from the inside. There have been seasons I prayed carefully, offering God a managed and acceptable version of myself, because the true version felt too dangerous to say out loud, and all the while I called that reticence reverence. It was not reverence. It was distance. | generalized | Many of us know that fear from the inside. There are seasons we pray carefully, offering God a managed and acceptable version of ourselves, because the true version feels too dangerous to say out loud, and all the while we call that reticence reverence. It is not reverence. It is distance. |
| suffering | `sessions[2].summary` | Bell says that for most of his years as an atheist the cross meant nothing to him, and then one day it meant everything, and the hinge was suffering. He had assumed that if there were a God, he would be the kind of being who stayed above the mess, serene, untouched, explaining the world's agony from a safe distance or ignoring it altogether. The Christian claim turned out to be stranger than his atheism had prepared him for. | cut | Many skeptics assume that if there were a God, he would be the kind of being who stayed above the mess, serene, untouched, explaining the world's agony from a safe distance or ignoring it altogether. The Christian claim is stranger than that assumption prepares anyone for. |
| suffering | `sessions[2].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he once assumed that if God existed he would stay above the mess, serene and untouched. | generalized | Many people assume that if God exists he stays above the mess, serene and untouched. |
| suffering | `sessions[2].teaching` | Bell says that for most of the years he did not believe, the cross meant nothing to him. It was a piece of religious furniture, a symbol other people found moving, an execution he could not see the point of. And then one day it meant everything, and the hinge on which that turned was suffering. Because here is what his atheism had quietly assumed about God, in the years he was arguing against him. He assumed that if there were a God at all, he would be the kind of being who stayed above the mess. | cut | For many people who do not believe, the cross means nothing. It is a piece of religious furniture, a symbol other people find moving, an execution they cannot see the point of. Because here is what unbelief quietly assumes about God. It assumes that if there were a God at all, he would be the kind of being who stayed above the mess. |
| suffering | `sessions[2].teaching` | And on that assumption the problem of pain really is unanswerable, because a God who could stop your child's suffering and merely watches from his untroubled height is a monster, and Bell wanted no part of him. What broke the assumption was discovering that the Christian claim about God was stranger, and harder, than anything his unbelief had prepared him for. | cut | And on that assumption the problem of pain really is unanswerable, because a God who could stop your child's suffering and merely watches from his untroubled height is a monster, and no one should want any part of him. What breaks the assumption is discovering that the Christian claim about God is stranger, and harder, than anything unbelief prepares a person for. |
| suffering | `sessions[2].teaching` | I know the difference this makes because I have needed it, not as an argument but as company. | generalized | Many who suffer know the difference this makes, because they have needed it, not as an argument but as company. |
| suffering | `sessions[3].teaching` | Take the strong case against everything I have just said, because it needs to be stated at full strength and I have stood near enough graves to feel it. | generalized | Take the strong case against everything I have just said, because it needs to be stated at full strength and anyone who has stood near a grave can feel it. |
| suffering | `sessions[3].teaching` | So here is the turn, and it is where I have had to live myself, because I have prayed the three-times prayer and gotten the answer Paul got. | generalized | So here is the turn, and it is where many of us have had to live, praying the three-times prayer and getting the answer Paul got. |
| suffering | `sessions[4].teaching` | Take the strong case against all of this, the skeptic's case, and take it seriously, because I made it myself for years and a hope that cannot survive the question is not worth having. | cut | Take the strong case against all of this, the skeptic's case, and take it seriously, because a hope that cannot survive the question is not worth having. |
| suffering | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith out of atheism partly over this very question, he was raised without a father, he has buried people he loved and sat with people who wanted to die, and he does not pretend to stand on the far side of suffering dispensing answers. | cut | He came to faith out of atheism and was raised without a father, and he does not pretend to stand on the far side of suffering dispensing answers. |
| suffering | `faq[9].a` | The writer came to faith out of atheism partly over the problem of suffering, was raised without a father, and has buried people he loved and sat with people who wanted to die, and he refuses to stand on the far side of pain dispensing answers he has not had to live. He admits to praying the prayer that was met with silence, to handing a grieving person a sentence he later wished he could take back, to wanting a God too strong to be wounded. | cut | The writer came to faith out of atheism and was raised without a father, and he refuses to stand on the far side of pain dispensing answers. He does not exempt himself from the prayer met with silence, the sentence handed to a grieving person and later regretted, the wish for a God too strong to be wounded. |
| suffering | `faq[10].a` | It does not pretend the question is stupid, because the writer once asked it himself as an unbeliever. | cut | It does not pretend the question is stupid, because the writer came to faith from atheism. |
| suffering | `faq[11].a` | The single most common way we fail the suffering is by reaching for a sentence that eases our own discomfort and leaves them more alone, and the study names that impulse honestly, because the writer confesses to it himself. | generalized | The single most common way we fail the suffering is by reaching for a sentence that eases our own discomfort and leaves them more alone, and the study names that impulse honestly, because most of us have given in to it. |

### the-beatitudes (6)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-beatitudes | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, and he knows what it is to be the person the religious room had already counted out. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father. |
| the-beatitudes | `sessions[0].teaching` | I came to faith from atheism, from outside the religious room entirely, the kind of person the impressive Christians of my acquaintance had already quietly counted out. | cut | I came to faith from atheism, from outside the religious room entirely. |
| the-beatitudes | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know this one from the inside, because I am fluent in the performance of having it together, in filling my own emptiness with usefulness and hurrying my own grief along so I could get back to being fine. | generalized | Most of us know this one from the inside, fluent in the performance of having it together, filling our own emptiness with usefulness and hurrying our own grief along so we can get back to being fine. |
| the-beatitudes | `sessions[2].teaching` | I know this appetite intimately, the reach for one more accomplishment or one more bit of recognition to fill a hole that was never shaped for any of it, and the reliable arrival of the same emptiness by the next morning. | generalized | Most of us know this appetite intimately, the reach for one more accomplishment or one more bit of recognition to fill a hole that was never shaped for any of it, and the reliable arrival of the same emptiness by the next morning. |
| the-beatitudes | `sessions[3].teaching` | It is about me, on the days I hold a small debt against someone while standing in the wreckage of an enormous one God has already torn up. | generalized | It is about us, on the days we hold a small debt against someone while standing in the wreckage of an enormous one God has already torn up. |
| the-beatitudes | `faq[11].a` | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, which means he did not arrive at these blessings as a man who had cleared the bar but as exactly the kind of person the religious room had already counted out, the one with empty hands who found the door held open anyway. | cut | He came to faith from atheism and was raised without a father, which means he did not arrive at these blessings as a man who had cleared the bar but as the one with empty hands who found the door held open anyway. |

### the-captive-church (21)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-captive-church | `summary` | He came to faith out of atheism into a Christianity already fused with a flag. He grew up inside the fusion. He has wanted the mountain. He has run the double standard, excused his own side, baptized his own politics, and kept high places he swore were not high places. | cut | He came to faith out of atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the captivity this study names, the wanting of the mountain, the double standard, the excusing of one's own side, the baptizing of one's own politics, the high places sworn not to be high places. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he came to faith into a Christianity already fused with a flag, and that for years he wanted the mountain and told himself he wanted it for Jesus. | cut | Many of us have wanted the mountain and told ourselves we wanted it for Jesus. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I have wanted the shortcut. I will not pretend I am writing from outside the temptation, looking down at the people who fell. I came to faith out of atheism into a Christianity already fused with a flag, and the first thing the fusion taught me, though nobody said it directly, was that the kingdom advances when our side wins. It was in the air of the building. For years I wanted the mountain. I wanted the church to have the levers and the votes and the cultural weight, and I told myself I wanted them for Jesus. | cut | And I have to stand inside this, because none of us is immune to wanting the shortcut. I will not pretend I am writing from outside the temptation, looking down at the people who fell. Much of the church has been fused with a flag so long that the fusion teaches, though nobody says it directly, that the kingdom advances when our side wins. It is in the air of the building. Many of us have wanted the mountain. We have wanted the church to have the levers and the votes and the cultural weight, and told ourselves we wanted them for Jesus. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].summary` | A man left Bell's office certain he had been insulted, because he heard the phrase Christian nationalism and what he heard was an accusation against everyone who ever wept at a military funeral or stood for a flag with a hand over the heart. His grandfather had landed at Normandy. | generalized | Many people hear the phrase Christian nationalism as an accusation against everyone who ever wept at a military funeral or stood for a flag with a hand over the heart, against a grandfather who landed at Normandy. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].discussion[0].q` | The man who left Bell's office heard the phrase Christian nationalism as an insult to his grandfather who landed at Normandy. | generalized | Many people hear the phrase Christian nationalism as an insult to a grandfather who landed at Normandy. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell says he has to indict his own tribe first, that the prophet who only names the other side is a partisan with a Bible, and that the fusion he knows best is the one he grew up inside. | generalized | Bell says we have to indict our own tribe first, that the prophet who only names the other side is a partisan with a Bible, and that the fusion we know best is the one we grew up inside. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | A man left my office last spring certain I had insulted his grandfather. He had heard me say the phrase Christian nationalism in a sermon, and what he heard was an accusation against everyone who ever stood for a flag with a hand over the heart, everyone who wept at a military funeral, everyone who believed this country had been given something by God worth keeping. His grandfather had landed at Normandy. He was not going to sit still while his pastor called that idolatry. I did not call it idolatry. But I understood why he heard it that way, because the phrase has been ruined in both directions. To the man in my office it is a slur. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, the man in the pews who hears the phrase Christian nationalism in a sermon and leaves certain his grandfather has been insulted. What he heard was an accusation against everyone who ever stood for a flag with a hand over the heart, everyone who wept at a military funeral, everyone who believed this country had been given something by God worth keeping. His grandfather landed at Normandy. He is not going to sit still while his pastor calls that idolatry. The pastor need not have called it idolatry. But it is easy to understand why he heard it that way, because the phrase has been ruined in both directions. To the man in the pew it is a slur. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | He sensed that the people warning loudest about this often have a contempt for him that has nothing to do with theology, and he was frequently right about that. | generalized | He senses that the people warning loudest about this often have a contempt for him that has nothing to do with theology, and he is frequently right about that. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have lived inside it. I am not describing strangers. | generalized | Many of us have lived inside it. It is not a description of strangers. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have to indict my own tribe first, because the prophet who only names the other side is not a prophet but a partisan with a Bible. The fusion I know best is the one I grew up inside, the one that fused Jesus with my country and my politics, and I held it for years without noticing, because you do not notice the water you were born in. Naming it in others is cheap. Naming it in myself is the only naming that costs anything. | generalized | We have to indict our own tribe first, because the prophet who only names the other side is not a prophet but a partisan with a Bible. The fusion each of us knows best is the one we grew up inside, the one that fused Jesus with our country and our politics, and we can hold it for years without noticing, because you do not notice the water you were born in. Naming it in others is cheap. Naming it in ourselves is the only naming that costs anything. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[1].goingDeeper` | The first is the error of the man in the office, hearing every mention of the danger as an attack on love of country, which collapses a real and named idolatry into mere patriotism. | generalized | The first is the error of the man in the pew, hearing every mention of the danger as an attack on love of country, which collapses a real and named idolatry into mere patriotism. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[3].discussion[3].q` | Bell confesses he has used this verse to excuse his cowardice, hiding behind the two drawers when he did not want the cost of saying that all of it, public and private, belongs to God. | generalized | Many of us have used this verse to excuse cowardice, hiding behind the two drawers when we did not want the cost of saying that all of it, public and private, belongs to God. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[3].teaching` | I have preached this line as if Jesus came to teach us to keep our heads down. I was wrong, and the text knew it before I did. | generalized | Many of us have heard this line preached as if Jesus came to teach us to keep our heads down. That reading is wrong, and the text knew it before we did. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to confess where I land, because I have used this verse to excuse my cowardice. I have hidden behind the two drawers when I did not want the cost of saying that all of it, the public and the private, the coin and the soul, belongs to God and must answer to him. The comfortable reading let me off the hook the cross had put me on. | generalized | And we have to confess where we land, because many of us have used this verse to excuse cowardice. We have hidden behind the two drawers when we did not want the cost of saying that all of it, the public and the private, the coin and the soul, belongs to God and must answer to him. The comfortable reading let us off the hook the cross had put us on. |
| the-captive-church | `sessions[4].teaching` | I have been captive. I want to say that plainly at the end, because this whole study has been an indictment, and the prophet who is not inside the indictment is a fraud. I grew up in the fusion. I want to say that plainly at the end, because this whole study has been an indictment, and the prophet who is not inside the indictment is a fraud. | the-captive-church | `leaderPrimer` | Bell grew up inside the fusion and came to faith from atheism into a Christianity already wearing a flag, and he confesses to wanting the mountain, to running the double standard, to keeping high places he swore were not high places. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from wanting the mountain, running the double standard, keeping high places sworn not to be high places. |
| the-captive-church | `faq[9].a` | The author ends inside the captivity, not outside it, writing toward the free church and not from it, confessing that he grew up in the fusion, has wanted the mountain, has run the double standard and kept high places he swore were not high places. | cut | The author ends inside the captivity, not outside it, writing toward the free church and not from it, and he does not exempt himself from the wanting of the mountain, the double standard, or the high places sworn not to be high places. |
