### the-church (25)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-church | `summary` | Bell writes as a working pastor who has watched the church fail up close, who has been the failure more than once, and who loves her anyway, the way you love a family you cannot disown because it is the only family there is. He does not stand outside this. He has been the hypocrite in the pulpit. He writes from inside the body. | cut | Bell writes as a working pastor who does not exempt himself from the church's failures, and who loves her anyway, the way you love a family you cannot disown because it is the only family there is. He does not stand outside this. He writes from inside the body. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell he loved Jesus but was finished with the church, that he could worship God just fine on a Saturday morning with his coffee and his Bible. Bell did not argue with him. He asked him a question instead. | generalized | Many people say they love Jesus but are finished with the church, that they can worship God just fine on a Saturday morning with coffee and a Bible. The answer is not an argument but a question. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has spent whole seasons treating the church as a service he produced rather than a people he belonged to. | generalized | Pastors and members alike can spend whole seasons treating the church as a service produced rather than a people they belong to. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where Bell starts, with a man who loved Jesus and was finished with the church. He told Bell he could worship God just fine on his own, a Saturday morning, good coffee, an open Bible, no need for the building or the crowd or the disappointment. And Bell did not argue with him, because the man was not wrong about the disappointment. He asked him one question instead. Where, in the entire New Testament, do you find a single Christian living that way. The man could not name one, because there is not one. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, the man who loves Jesus and is finished with the church. He can worship God just fine on his own, he says, a Saturday morning, good coffee, an open Bible, no need for the building or the crowd or the disappointment. There is no need to argue with him, because he is not wrong about the disappointment. Ask him one question instead. Where, in the entire New Testament, do you find a single Christian living that way. He will not be able to name one, because there is not one. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because the consumer instinct is not only out in the pews, it has lived in me, in the pulpit. I have spent whole seasons treating the church as a service I produced rather than a people I belonged to, measuring a Sunday by whether it went well the way you measure a performance, thinking of the congregation as an audience to be moved rather than a body I was a limb of, bleeding and fed by the same blood. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because the consumer instinct is not only out in the pews, it lives in the pulpit too. Pastors can spend whole seasons treating the church as a service they produce rather than a people they belong to, measuring a Sunday by whether it went well the way you measure a performance, thinking of the congregation as an audience to be moved rather than a body they are a limb of, bleeding and fed by the same blood. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].caseStudy` | A woman in Bell's congregation, call her Marta, told him she had not missed a Sunday in eleven years and yet did not know a single person's last name. She arrived at the last possible minute, sat in the back, slipped out during the final song, and went home. She was, by every visible measure, a faithful churchgoer, and she was, by her own quiet admission, completely alone. | generalized | A woman in your group, call her Marta, says she has not missed a Sunday in eleven years and yet does not know a single person's last name. She arrives at the last possible minute, sits in the back, slips out during the final song, and goes home. She is, by every visible measure, a faithful churchgoer, and she is, by her own quiet admission, completely alone. |
| the-church | `sessions[0].caseStudy` | She had perfect attendance and no belonging. She was a hand that came to the building every week and never let itself be attached to the arm. | generalized | She has perfect attendance and no belonging. She is a hand that comes to the building every week and never lets itself be attached to the arm. |
| the-church | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has been more in love with the church he imagined leading than the church God actually gave him to love. | generalized | Pastors can be more in love with the church they imagined leading than the church God actually gave them to love. |
| the-church | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to put myself in the center of this, because it is the pastors temptation in its purest form. I have been more in love with the church I imagined leading than the church God actually handed me to love. I have stood in front of the real congregation, the actual people, and felt a low disappointment that they were not the people in my head, the more responsive, more committed, more interesting congregation of my imagination, and that disappointment is not high standards. It is the dream turned into contempt, and it makes me, in Bonhoeffers terrible phrase, a destroyer of the very community I was ordained to serve. | generalized | And I have to put the pastor in the center of this, because it is the pastors temptation in its purest form. A pastor can be more in love with the church he imagined leading than the church God actually handed him to love. He can stand in front of the real congregation, the actual people, and feel a low disappointment that they are not the people in his head, the more responsive, more committed, more interesting congregation of his imagination, and that disappointment is not high standards. It is the dream turned into contempt, and it makes him, in Bonhoeffers terrible phrase, a destroyer of the very community he was ordained to serve. |
| the-church | `sessions[1].caseStudy` | Within four months they were gone, and on the way out the husband told Bell the group was shallow and cliquey and not real community. | generalized | Within four months they were gone, and on the way out the husband told the leader the group was shallow and cliquey and not real community. |
| the-church | `sessions[1].caseStudy` | But Bell had also watched this couple, and what he saw was a pair of people measuring every gathering against a picture in their heads and finding each one wanting, never staying in any group long enough to become the depth they were demanding from it. | generalized | But the leader had also watched this couple, and what the leader saw was a pair of people measuring every gathering against a picture in their heads and finding each one wanting, never staying in any group long enough to become the depth they were demanding from it. |
| the-church | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has, as a pastor, helped the church look more like a museum than a hospital, polishing an image instead of treating the sick. | generalized | Pastors and members alike have helped the church look more like a museum than a hospital, polishing an image instead of treating the sick. |
| the-church | `sessions[2].teaching` | And here I am implicated as deeply as anyone, because the temptation of the pastor is to run a museum. I have helped the church perform a health it did not have, curated an image of a put-together congregation, rewarded the people who hid their sickness well and quietly found the visibly broken ones inconvenient, because a hospital is messier and harder to advertise than a gallery. That is its own hypocrisy, and it is mine. | generalized | And here pastors are implicated as deeply as anyone, because the temptation of the pastor is to run a museum, to help the church perform a health it does not have, to curate an image of a put-together congregation, to reward the people who hide their sickness well and quietly find the visibly broken ones inconvenient, because a hospital is messier and harder to advertise than a gallery. That is its own hypocrisy, and no pastor is immune to it. |
| the-church | `sessions[2].caseStudy` | A man Bell calls Dean had been an elder for two decades, respected, quoted, the one people pointed to as a pillar. | generalized | Suppose a man, call him Dean, had been an elder for two decades, respected, quoted, the one people pointed to as a pillar. |
| the-church | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has built churches that were good at gathering and terrible at scattering, that kept people busy inside the building instead of sending them out. | generalized | Many churches are good at gathering and terrible at scattering, keeping people busy inside the building instead of sending them out. |
| the-church | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to confess where I have failed at this as a pastor, because the temptation of my whole profession runs the other way. I have built churches that were very good at gathering and quietly terrible at scattering. I have filled peoples weeks with church activities until they had no time, energy, or relationships left for the actual mission field, and then wondered why the neighbors never heard the gospel. I have measured success by how many came in on Sunday rather than by how faithfully they were sent out on Monday, which is to measure the base camp by how full the tents are while no one climbs. That is a bunker with good attendance, and I have built it. | generalized | And pastors have to confess where they fail at this, because the temptation of the whole profession runs the other way. Churches get built that are very good at gathering and quietly terrible at scattering. Pastors fill peoples weeks with church activities until they have no time, energy, or relationships left for the actual mission field, and then wonder why the neighbors never heard the gospel. They measure success by how many came in on Sunday rather than by how faithfully they were sent out on Monday, which is to measure the base camp by how full the tents are while no one climbs. That is a bunker with good attendance, and pastors build it. |
| the-church | `sessions[3].caseStudy` | Bell describes a church that was, by its own account, thriving. | generalized | Picture a church that was, by its own account, thriving. |
| the-church | `sessions[4].summary` | But he watches where the road so often ends, and tells the truth about it. | generalized | But he names where the road so often ends, and tells the truth about it. |
| the-church | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has loved the church only while she served him and wanted to leave the moment she cost him. | generalized | Many of us have loved the church only while she served us and wanted to leave the moment she cost us. |
| the-church | `sessions[4].teaching` | But Bell has watched where the road tends to end, and love requires telling the truth about it. | generalized | But it is worth naming where the road tends to end, and love requires telling the truth about it. |
| the-church | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I know this reflex from the inside, because I have lived it, even as the pastor. I have loved the church while she served me and wanted to be gone the moment she cost me. I have felt the pull toward the door not over abuse but over ordinary disappointment, over a board that frustrated me or a people who would not follow where I wanted to lead, and I have been tempted to call that consumer flinch a noble departure. It was not noble. It was the same thing I have watched in everyone else, the love that only stays while it is being served. | generalized | And most of us know this reflex from the inside, pastors included. We have loved the church while she served us and wanted to be gone the moment she cost us. We have felt the pull toward the door not over abuse but over ordinary disappointment, over a board that frustrated us or a people who would not follow where we wanted to lead, and we have been tempted to call that consumer flinch a noble departure. It is not noble. It is the same thing in all of us, the love that only stays while it is being served. |
| the-church | `sessions[4].caseStudy` | Two people left Bell's church in the same year, and the difference between them is the whole point of this session. The first, call her Renee, left an abusive situation, a leader who had manipulated and silenced and covered, and her leaving was an act of faith and survival, and Bell helped her get out and get help and would do it again. | generalized | Consider two people who left the same church in the same year, and the difference between them is the whole point of this session. The first, call her Renee, left an abusive situation, a leader who had manipulated and silenced and covered, and her leaving was an act of faith and survival, and helping her get out and get help was the only faithful response. |
| the-church | `leaderPrimer` | Bell pastors the kind of small church this study describes, and he has been the reason for someone's wound, the hypocrite in his own pulpit more than once, and he loves her anyway, the way you love the only family there is. | cut | Bell is a pastor, and he does not exempt himself from the church's failures, and he loves her anyway, the way you love the only family there is. |
| the-church | `faq[0].a` | It stands inside the failures with you, admits the writer has been the failure, and then asks the harder question of what the church actually is and whether you can be a Christian without one. | cut | It stands inside the failures with you, does not exempt the writer from them, and then asks the harder question of what the church actually is and whether you can be a Christian without one. |

He has built bunkers and called them sanctuaries, run museums and called them hospitals, been more in love with the church he imagined than the one God actually gave him. | cut | He does not exempt himself from the hypocrisy, the love that stays only while the church serves, the bunkers called sanctuaries, the museums called hospitals, the love for an imagined church over the one God actually gave. |

### the-creeds (16)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-creeds | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he remembers exactly how the word creed sounded from the outside, like a loyalty oath, like a wall built to keep thinking people out. He spent years assuming that real faith was the private, wordless kind and that anything written down by bishops in the fourth century was the dead part. He was wrong, and the study is honest about how he learned it. He writes from inside the change, not above it. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he knows how the word creed can sound from the outside, like a loyalty oath, like a wall built to keep thinking people out. Many assume that real faith is the private, wordless kind and that anything written down by bishops in the fourth century is the dead part. They are wrong, and the study is honest about why. He writes as one who came in from outside, not from above. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell held that instinct hard as a young skeptic and for a while after he believed. | cut | Skeptics and believers alike hold that instinct hard. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[0].discussion[3].q` | Bell admits he once believed the church would have been better off staying simple and never writing anything down. | generalized | Many believe the church would have been better off staying simple and never writing anything down. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell held that instinct as tightly as anyone. As a young atheist he thought the creeds were loyalty oaths, walls built to keep the thinking people out, and even after he came to faith he assumed for a while that the real Christianity was the private, wordless kind and the councils were the fossil. He was wrong, and the reason he was wrong is a story worth telling, because the creeds did not begin with bishops who loved precision. | cut | Many hold that instinct tightly. Skeptics often see the creeds as loyalty oaths, walls built to keep the thinking people out, and many believers assume that the real Christianity is the private, wordless kind and the councils are the fossil. They are wrong, and the reason they are wrong is a story worth telling, because the creeds did not begin with bishops who loved precision. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here is what I finally understood, years after my old instinct told me the councils were the dead part. | cut | And here is what the old instinct misses about the councils. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here is where I have to include myself, because these are not merely ancient errors, they are my defaults. I want a manageable Jesus. A God who only seemed human is convenient, because then his suffering was not quite real and I need not follow him into mine. A merely human Jesus, a great teacher, is also convenient, because then I can admire him without bowing to him. The one thing that gives me no exit is the real one, fully God so that in him I actually meet God, and fully human so that my own nature has been taken up and there is a man with scars at the right hand of the Father who was tempted exactly as I am. That Jesus I cannot manage, cannot shrink, cannot keep at a safe philosophical distance. I can only worship him, or walk away. | generalized | And here is where we have to include ourselves, because these are not merely ancient errors, they are our defaults. We want a manageable Jesus. A God who only seemed human is convenient, because then his suffering was not quite real and we need not follow him into ours. A merely human Jesus, a great teacher, is also convenient, because then we can admire him without bowing to him. The one thing that gives us no exit is the real one, fully God so that in him we actually meet God, and fully human so that our own nature has been taken up and there is a man with scars at the right hand of the Father who was tempted exactly as we are. That Jesus we cannot manage, cannot shrink, cannot keep at a safe philosophical distance. We can only worship him, or walk away. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[4].summary` | Bell carried that instinct out of his years as a skeptic and had to be unlearned from it slowly. | cut | (sentence removed) |
| the-creeds | `sessions[4].teaching` | I carried that instinct for years, first as an atheist who thought creeds were loyalty oaths and then as a young believer who assumed the genuine faith was the private, wordless kind and the recited creed was the fossilized part. It had to be unlearned in me slowly, and this last session is where I try to hand over what I learned. | cut | Many carry that instinct for years, skeptics who think creeds are loyalty oaths and believers who assume the genuine faith is the private, wordless kind and the recited creed is the fossilized part. It has to be unlearned slowly, and this last session tries to help. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[4].teaching` | And here is the mercy in it that I did not understand until my own faith went through a dark stretch. | generalized | And here is the mercy in it that many do not understand until their own faith goes through a dark stretch. |
| the-creeds | `sessions[4].teaching` | Now the deepest turn, the one that finally broke my old instinct for good. | cut | Now the deepest turn, the one that breaks the old instinct for good. |
| the-creeds | `leaderPrimer` | Bell leads this from inside it, as a former atheist who once thought the creeds were the dead part and was wrong, and who still says the words some mornings before his heart has caught up. | cut | Bell leads this from inside it, as a former atheist who stands under words he did not write. |
| the-creeds | `faq[10].q` | Why does the writer keep bringing up his own atheism and his own struggle instead of just teaching the history? | cut | Why does the writer keep bringing up his own atheism instead of just teaching the history? |
| the-creeds | `faq[10].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he remembers exactly how the creeds sounded from the outside, like a wall to keep thinking people out. He spent years assuming the real faith was the private, wordless kind and the councils were the dead part, and he was wrong, and he says so. He still says the creed some mornings before his heart has caught up. He writes from inside the change, not above it, because a study that lectured down at doubters from a height of settled certainty would only prove their suspicion that faith is for people who never really thought about it. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he knows how the creeds can sound from the outside, like a wall to keep thinking people out. He writes from inside the faith, not above it, because a study that lectured down at doubters from a height of settled certainty would only prove their suspicion that faith is for people who never really thought about it. |
| the-creeds | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer came to this from atheism and once thought the creeds were the dead part of Christianity, and he was wrong, and he still says the words some mornings before his heart has caught up, so we are not standing above this material, we are standing inside it. | cut | Third, the writer came to this from atheism, so we are not standing above this material, we are standing inside it. |
| the-creeds | `promoKit.bulletinBlurb` | Doubt is welcome, nobody is tested, and it is written from inside the change by a former atheist who once thought the creeds were the dead part and was wrong. | cut | Doubt is welcome, nobody is tested, and it is written by a former atheist who knows how the creeds can sound from the outside. |
| the-creeds | `promoKit.inviteCaption` | Written from inside the change by a former atheist who thought the creeds were the dead part and was wrong. | cut | Written by a former atheist who knows how the creeds can sound from the outside. |

### the-image-of-god (9)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-image-of-god | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, which means he spent years holding the other answer, that a human being is a clever animal and nothing more, and he knows from the inside how thin that answer turns out to be when you are standing at a hospital bed or a graveside. He was raised without a father and is raising five sons, and he writes as a man who has both doubted his own worth and quietly assigned worth to others on a sliding scale, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he knows the other answer, that a human being is a clever animal and nothing more, an answer that turns thin at a hospital bed or a graveside. He was raised without a father and is raising five sons, and he does not exempt himself from the sliding scale on which most of us assign worth, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient. |
| the-image-of-god | `sessions[0].teaching` | Now take the strong case against all of this, because a thoughtful skeptic makes it and I made it myself for years before I believed anything. | cut | Now take the strong case against all of this, because a thoughtful skeptic makes it. |
| the-image-of-god | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside it rather than above it. The doctrine does not only rescue the beggar from my contempt. It exposes me, because I have spent my life quietly assigning worth on a sliding scale, generous toward the useful and the impressive and the people who can do something for me, and stingy, dismissive, barely seeing, toward the inconvenient and the ordinary and the ones who cost me something. I have treated my own dignity as an achievement I earned and other people's as a grade I was entitled to assign. The image will not allow either. It was conferred, not earned, given to every human before a single one of them had done a thing to deserve it, which means I cannot inflate my own and I cannot dock anyone else's. | generalized | And here is where we have to stand inside it rather than above it. The doctrine does not only rescue the beggar from our contempt. It exposes us, because most of us quietly assign worth on a sliding scale, generous toward the useful and the impressive and the people who can do something for us, and stingy, dismissive, barely seeing, toward the inconvenient and the ordinary and the ones who cost us something. We treat our own dignity as an achievement we earned and other people's as a grade we are entitled to assign. The image will not allow either. It was conferred, not earned, given to every human before a single one of them had done a thing to deserve it, which means we cannot inflate our own and we cannot dock anyone else's. |
| the-image-of-god | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to say that I have run the wrong order in my own heart more than I want to admit, quietly valuing people by what they can do, sharpest toward the ones who could think with me and slowest to see the full humanity of the ones who could not. The argument about what the image is looked like a parlor game. It turns out to be the argument about whether the weakest person in the room is as fully human as I am. | generalized | And most of us run the wrong order in our own hearts more than we want to admit, quietly valuing people by what they can do, sharpest toward the ones who can think with us and slowest to see the full humanity of the ones who cannot. The argument about what the image is looked like a parlor game. It turns out to be the argument about whether the weakest person in the room is as fully human as we are. |
| the-image-of-god | `sessions[2].teaching` | If the image were destroyed, then some people really could be written off, past saving, fit only to be discarded, and every one of us keeps a private list of exactly such people, and I have kept mine. | cut | If the image were destroyed, then some people really could be written off, past saving, fit only to be discarded, and every one of us keeps a private list of exactly such people. |
| the-image-of-god | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to say this stands over me too, because I find some of these faces genuinely easy and others genuinely hard, and the hard ones are not random, they are the ones my own instincts and my own tribe have trained me to look past, and the doctrine keeps dragging my eyes back to exactly them. | generalized | And this stands over all of us, because most of us find some of these faces genuinely easy and others genuinely hard, and the hard ones are not random, they are the ones our own instincts and our own tribe have trained us to look past, and the doctrine keeps dragging our eyes back to exactly them. |
| the-image-of-god | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, so he knows the other answer from the inside, and he confesses that he still assigns worth on a sliding scale, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism, so he knows the other answer from the inside, and he does not exempt himself from the sliding scale, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient. |
| the-image-of-god | `faq[9].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, so he held the other answer for years and knows it from the inside, and he confesses that he still assigns human worth on a sliding scale, generous toward the useful and impressive and stingy toward the inconvenient and ordinary. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, so he knows the other answer from the inside, and he does not exempt himself from the sliding scale on which human worth gets assigned, generous toward the useful and impressive and stingy toward the inconvenient and ordinary. |
| the-image-of-god | `facilitatorScript.open` | He came to faith from atheism, so he held the other answer, and he admits he still grades human worth on a sliding scale, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient, and so will we. | generalized | He came to faith from atheism, so he held the other answer, and he does not exempt himself from grading human worth on a sliding scale, generous to the useful and stingy toward the inconvenient, and neither will we. |

### the-lords-prayer (15)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-lords-prayer | `summary` | He is the man who prayed with his mouth while his mind wandered to his calendar, who said Our Father for years before he could feel the word Father meant anything, who asked God to forgive him in the same breath he refused to forgive someone else. | generalized | Many of us have prayed with our mouths while our minds wandered to the calendar, said Our Father for years before we could feel the word meant anything, asked God to forgive us in the same breath we refused to forgive someone else. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell says he could recite the Lord's Prayer before he believed a word of it, and that the word that stopped him, when belief finally came, was the first one. Our Father. He was raised without a father, and he had spent years assuming the word carried nothing, or worse, that it carried an ache he had learned to route around. | cut | Many people can recite the Lord's Prayer before they believe a word of it, and for some the word that stops them, when belief finally comes, is the first one. Our Father. Bell was raised without a father, and he knows that for many the word carries nothing, or worse, an ache they have learned to route around. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he could recite this prayer long before he believed it, and that the word Father was the one that finally stopped him. | generalized | Many people can recite this prayer long before they believe it, and for some the word Father is the one that finally stops them. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he prayed the word Father for years before he could feel it meant anything. | generalized | Many of us pray the word Father for years before we can feel it means anything. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell says he could recite the Lord's Prayer before he believed a single word of it. He had it by heart the way you keep a childhood address, a thing lodged in memory that means nothing until the day it suddenly means everything. And when belief finally came to him, out of a long atheism he had thought was permanent, the word that stopped him was the very first one. Our Father. He was raised without a father. He had spent his life assuming the word carried nothing for him, or worse, that it carried a specific ache he had learned to walk around, the way you learn the layout of a dark room so you never have to turn on the light. | cut | Many people can recite the Lord's Prayer before they believe a single word of it. They have it by heart the way you keep a childhood address, a thing lodged in memory that means nothing until the day it suddenly means everything. And for some, when belief finally comes, the word that stops them is the very first one. Our Father. Bell was raised without a father, and for anyone raised that way the word can carry nothing at all, or worse, a specific ache they have learned to walk around, the way you learn the layout of a dark room so you never have to turn on the light. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[0].teaching` | And here is where I have to stand inside it, because the writer is never outside this prayer. I prayed the word Father for years before I could feel that it meant anything, saying it out into what felt like an empty room, my mind on my calendar and my heart three steps behind my mouth. I have treated God as a comfort I could summon and forgotten he is a fire. I have wanted the Abba without the hallowed, the nearness without the awe, a Father shaped conveniently like my own preferences. The gospel will not let me keep him. | generalized | And here is where we have to stand inside it, because the writer is never outside this prayer. Many of us pray the word Father for years before we can feel that it means anything, saying it out into what feels like an empty room, our minds on the calendar and our hearts three steps behind our mouths. We treat God as a comfort we can summon and forget he is a fire. We want the Abba without the hallowed, the nearness without the awe, a Father shaped conveniently like our own preferences. The gospel will not let us keep him. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[1].teaching` | It is the small, comfortable, well-defended kingdom I have built with myself on the throne, the domain where my preferences are law and my comfort is the highest good and every person and circumstance is quietly evaluated by whether it serves me. | generalized | It is the small, comfortable, well-defended kingdom we have built with ourselves on the throne, the domain where our preferences are law and our comfort is the highest good and every person and circumstance is quietly evaluated by whether it serves us. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I am the man who has prayed my will be done for most of my life while my mouth said his. I have built the small kingdom. I have arranged a life around my own comfort and called the arrangement wisdom. I have prayed your will be done at the exact moments I had already made sure my will was going to be done, using the prayer as a decoration on a decision. The gospel keeps prying my fingers off the throne, one petition at a time. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us have prayed my will be done for most of our lives while our mouths said his. We have built the small kingdom. We have arranged a life around our own comfort and called the arrangement wisdom. We have prayed your will be done at the exact moments we had already made sure our will was going to be done, using the prayer as a decoration on a decision. The gospel keeps prying our fingers off the throne, one petition at a time. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[2].teaching` | The competent among us hate this. I hate this. Dependence feels like weakness, and we have built entire lives designed to need no one, least of all a God we cannot see. | cut | The competent among us hate this. Dependence feels like weakness, and we have built entire lives designed to need no one, least of all a God we cannot see. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I am the man who prays give us this day our daily bread and then lies awake calculating tomorrow. I have chased the number. I have believed, without ever saying it out loud, that a little more would finally be the amount that made me safe, and I have watched the number move every time I got close, because the fear was never actually about money and no sum was ever going to quiet it. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us pray give us this day our daily bread and then lie awake calculating tomorrow. We chase the number. We believe, without ever saying it out loud, that a little more would finally be the amount that made us safe, and we watch the number move every time we get close, because the fear was never actually about money and no sum was ever going to quiet it. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I am that servant more often than I can stand to admit. I have prayed forgive me in one breath and rehearsed someone's offense against me in the next. I have asked God for an ocean and handed my neighbor a closed fist. I have kept a debt filed and ready, produced it in arguments, nursed it in private, told myself I was owed, and all the while I was the man Jesus described, walking out of the throne room where my own impossible debt had just been wiped clean, straight into another man's face, demanding he pay me what he owed. So here is the reversal, and it is the only thing that has ever loosened my grip. The unforgiveness I am nursing is not evidence that I am owed something. It is evidence that I have forgotten what I was forgiven. The cure is not to grit my teeth and try harder to release the debt, because willpower has never once opened that fist. The cure is to go back and stand again in the size of my own forgiven debt, the ten thousand talents, the ocean, the whole unpayable weight of it absorbed at a cross I did not carry, until the disproportion becomes unbearable, until it is simply no longer possible to keep choking a man over pocket change while standing knee-deep in a mercy that large. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us are that servant more often than we can stand to admit. We pray forgive me in one breath and rehearse someone's offense against us in the next. We ask God for an ocean and hand a neighbor a closed fist. We keep a debt filed and ready, produce it in arguments, nurse it in private, tell ourselves we are owed, and all the while we are the man Jesus described, walking out of the throne room where our own impossible debt had just been wiped clean, straight into another man's face, demanding he pay what he owed. So here is the reversal, and it is the only thing that ever loosens the grip. The unforgiveness we are nursing is not evidence that we are owed something. It is evidence that we have forgotten what we were forgiven. The cure is not to grit our teeth and try harder to release the debt, because willpower has never once opened that fist. The cure is to go back and stand again in the size of our own forgiven debt, the ten thousand talents, the ocean, the whole unpayable weight of it absorbed at a cross we did not carry, until the disproportion becomes unbearable, until it is simply no longer possible to keep choking a man over pocket change while standing knee-deep in a mercy that large. |
| the-lords-prayer | `sessions[4].teaching` | And here I have to stand inside it, because I am a man who has tried to keep all three. I have built small kingdoms and called them my life. I have trusted my own power and been ashamed to admit its limits. I have taken glory that was never mine, in a hundred small thefts, wanting the credit, wanting to be seen, wanting the name that is hallowed to share its light with mine. The doxology is where I have to set all of it down. | generalized | And here we have to stand inside it, because most of us have tried to keep all three. We build small kingdoms and call them our lives. We trust our own power and are ashamed to admit its limits. We take glory that was never ours, in a hundred small thefts, wanting the credit, wanting to be seen, wanting the name that is hallowed to share its light with ours. The doxology is where we have to set all of it down. |
| the-lords-prayer | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and every one of those facts sits inside this prayer, in the first word he could not feel, in the will he did not want to surrender, in the debts he asked forgiven while withholding forgiveness, in the glory he kept taking. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and every one of those facts sits inside this prayer, beginning with its first word. |
| the-lords-prayer | `faq[7].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism, was raised without a father, and is raising five sons, and he does not stand outside the struggles this prayer exposes. ### the-parables (12)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-parables | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he remembers what it was to hear these stories as quaint and to be ambushed by them anyway. He reads them here as a pastor who has been every wrong character in them, the older brother nursing his ledger, the priest crossing the road, the servant forgiven a fortune and throttling a neighbor over pocket change. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he reads these stories as a pastor who does not exempt himself from any of their wrong characters, the older brother nursing his ledger, the priest crossing the road, the servant forgiven a fortune and throttling a neighbor over pocket change. |
| the-parables | `sessions[0].teaching` | I want to honor that, because I remember making some version of it myself before I believed anything, and the anger in it is closer to reverence than the person making it usually knows. | cut | I want to honor that, because the anger in it is closer to reverence than the person making it usually knows. |
| the-parables | `sessions[0].teaching` | I have been every kind of ground. I have been the beaten path where the word could not even break the surface, and I have called it being realistic. | the-parables | `faq[8].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism and remembers being caught by them when he thought he was above them, and the study invites you to read one slowly, as if for the first time, and to notice whether it merely teaches a moral you already hold or whether it finds you standing somewhere you did not mean to stand. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and the study invites you to read one slowly, as if for the first time, and to notice whether it merely teaches a moral you already hold or whether it finds you standing somewhere you did not mean to stand. |

### the-psalms (19)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-psalms | `summary` | The writer came to faith out of atheism, and he remembers what it was to have no one to say the words to. He was raised without a father, and the fatherless ache runs under every psalm that cries out to God as a father. He has five sons, and he has prayed the desperate psalms over each of them in the dark. He does not point from outside the Psalter. He learned to pray inside it, badly, and is still learning. | cut | The writer came to faith out of atheism. He was raised without a father, and he has five sons. He does not point from outside the Psalter. He writes as one still learning to pray it. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell says the first time he tried to pray, newly out of atheism and sure he was doing it wrong, no one told him there was a book already written for exactly that. There was. | generalized | Many new believers try to pray sure they are doing it wrong, and no one tells them there is a book already written for exactly that. There is. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[0].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he tried to pray without knowing a prayer book already existed for exactly that. | generalized | Many people try to pray without knowing a prayer book already exists for exactly that. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[0].teaching` | The first time Bell tried to pray, he was newly out of atheism and certain he was doing it wrong, kneeling by a bed with no idea what a person was supposed to say to a God he had spent years denying. What no one told him, in that fumbling season, was that there was already a book written for exactly this, that he did not have to invent from scratch what to say to God because God had provided the words. | cut | Many people's first prayers are fumbling ones, prayed with the certainty of doing it wrong and no idea what a person is supposed to say to God. What no one tells them, in that fumbling season, is that there is already a book written for exactly this, that they do not have to invent from scratch what to say to God because God has provided the words. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[0].teaching` | I know that performance from the inside, because I did it for years, thanking God in the words I thought he wanted while the true words sat locked behind my teeth. | generalized | Many of us know that performance from the inside, thanking God in the words we think he wants while the true words sit locked behind our teeth. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[0].teaching` | That is the gift Bell wishes someone had handed him by that bed. | cut | That is the gift every fumbling beginner deserves to be handed. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell says the church taught him a hundred ways to praise and not one way to grieve in God's direction, so when the grief came he assumed it meant his faith had failed. It did not. | generalized | Many believers are taught a hundred ways to praise and not one way to grieve in God's direction, so when the grief comes they assume it means their faith has failed. It has not. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[1].discussion[0].q` | Bell says the church taught him to praise but never to grieve in God's direction, so when grief came he assumed his faith had failed. | generalized | Many churches teach people to praise but never to grieve in God's direction, so when grief comes they assume their faith has failed. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[1].teaching` | The church taught Bell a hundred ways to praise God and never once taught him how to grieve in God's direction, so when the grief finally came, and it comes for everyone, he did the natural thing and assumed it meant his faith had collapsed. It had not. What had collapsed was a false picture of faith, the picture in which a real believer only ever thanks and trusts and never questions, and that picture does not come from the Bible. | generalized | Many churches teach a hundred ways to praise God and never once teach how to grieve in God's direction, so when the grief finally comes, and it comes for everyone, people do the natural thing and assume it means their faith has collapsed. It has not. What has collapsed is a false picture of faith, the picture in which a real believer only ever thanks and trusts and never questions, and that picture does not come from the Bible. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[1].teaching` | I have needed that psalm. I have prayed in the dark over my sons and felt the ceiling was made of iron, and the cheerful believers around me had run out of things to say, and Psalm 88 was the only prayer that did not lie. | generalized | Many of us have needed that psalm, praying in the dark over the people we love and feeling the ceiling made of iron, when the cheerful believers around us had run out of things to say and Psalm 88 was the only prayer that did not lie. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[2].teaching` | I know this from a strange angle, as a man who came to God from atheism carrying more rage than I could name, some of it at a father who was not there. I did not know what to do with it, so for years I buried it and called the burying maturity, and it did not stay buried, it seeped. What the imprecatory psalms taught me is that God would rather I bring him the whole ugly load than pretend I do not carry it, that the rage handed to him is rage I am no longer holding myself. | cut | Many people come to God carrying more rage than they can name, some of it at people who were not there when they should have been. Not knowing what to do with it, they bury it for years and call the burying maturity, and it does not stay buried, it seeps. What the imprecatory psalms teach is that God would rather we bring him the whole ugly load than pretend we do not carry it, that the rage handed to him is rage we are no longer holding ourselves. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[3].teaching` | There is a real challenge here, and I feel it, and the sentimental reading of Psalm 23 fully deserves the charge of naivety. | cut | There is a real challenge here, and the sentimental reading of Psalm 23 fully deserves the charge of naivety. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[3].teaching` | I have to do this by hand. Left to itself my soul forgets every benefit by breakfast and remembers every grievance by lunch, and the only way I have ever found to thank God honestly is to do what the psalmist does, to talk to my own soul and make it count the mercies one by one. | generalized | Most of us have to do this by hand. Left to itself the soul forgets every benefit by breakfast and remembers every grievance by lunch, and the only honest way to thank God is to do what the psalmist does, to talk to your own soul and make it count the mercies one by one. |
| the-psalms | `sessions[4].teaching` | I came to God from atheism with no road behind me and no father to have shown me the way up, and what these psalms have given me over the years is not a shortcut to the summit but a road, an honest road, wide enough for the grief and the rage and the doubt, that climbs, slowly, toward a praise I could not have faked at the start and cannot stop singing now. | cut | For anyone who comes to God from atheism with no road behind them, or without a father to have shown them the way up, what these psalms give is not a shortcut to the summit but a road, an honest road, wide enough for the grief and the rage and the doubt, that climbs, slowly, toward a praise no one could have faked at the start. |
| the-psalms | `leaderPrimer` | He came to faith from atheism and remembers having no one to pray to, he was raised without a father and knows the fatherless ache that runs under the psalms, and he has prayed the desperate psalms over his five sons in the dark. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, he was raised without a father, and he has five sons. |
| the-psalms | `faq[10].a` | The writer came to faith from atheism and remembers the wordlessness of having no one to pray to, and what he found in the Psalms was not a technique for cheerfulness but a book honest enough to hold the worst of him. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and what the Psalms offer is not a technique for cheerfulness but a book honest enough to hold the worst of anyone. |
| the-psalms | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer prays these prayers from inside, as someone who came to God from atheism with no idea how to pray and is still learning, so we are all beginners here, leader included. | cut | Third, the writer prays these prayers from inside, as someone who came to God from atheism and is still learning, so we are all beginners here, leader included. |
| the-psalms | `promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | The writer leads it as a man who came to faith from atheism and had to learn to pray with no idea how. | cut | The writer leads it as a man who came to faith from atheism and is still learning to pray. |
| the-psalms | `promoKit.quoteCards[1]` | The church taught me a hundred ways to praise and not one way to grieve in God's direction. | generalized | Many of us were taught a hundred ways to praise and not one way to grieve in God's direction. |

### the-story-of-the-bible (13)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-story-of-the-bible | `summary` | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he remembers reading Scripture as a young man the way most skeptics read it, as disconnected fragments a person quotes to win an argument, a verse for weddings and a verse for funerals and a verse for whatever you already wanted to do. He was not wrong that the fragments, cut loose, can be made to say almost anything. He was wrong that fragments were all there was. What he could not see, and what this study is built to help a person see, is that the sixty-six books are not a filing cabinet of religious sayings but a single, unfolding story with a beginning that explains the ache and an ending that answers it, and a center, a cross, that holds the whole arc together. | cut | The writer came to faith from atheism, and he knows how most skeptics read Scripture, as disconnected fragments a person quotes to win an argument, a verse for weddings and a verse for funerals and a verse for whatever you already wanted to do. The skeptic is not wrong that the fragments, cut loose, can be made to say almost anything. He is wrong that fragments are all there is. What he cannot see, and what this study is built to help a person see, is that the sixty-six books are not a filing cabinet of religious sayings but a single, unfolding story with a beginning that explains the ache and an ending that answers it, and a center, a cross, that holds the whole arc together. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell says if the world is a gift, then gratitude is the only honest first response, and he confesses he mostly lives as if it were owed to him. | generalized | Bell says if the world is a gift, then gratitude is the only honest first response, and that most of us, the writer included, live as if it were owed to us. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[0].teaching` | I once held it myself, and I did not hold it out of rebellion, I held it because it seemed like the grown-up thing to believe. But here is what I could not make it explain, and what it still cannot explain, because it has to keep assuming the very thing it denies. | cut | I once held it myself, and most people who hold it do so not out of rebellion but because it seems like the grown-up thing to believe. But here is what it cannot explain, because it has to keep assuming the very thing it denies. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[0].teaching` | It tells me the sunset is photons scattering in an atmosphere, and it is right, and it has not touched the thing that made me stop and call it beautiful. | generalized | It tells me the sunset is photons scattering in an atmosphere, and it is right, and it has not touched the thing that makes me stop and call it beautiful. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I will tell you plainly where I fail this session, because the law names the writer first. I do not live as though the world were a gift. I live as though it were owed to me, and I only notice the giving when something is taken away. The whole of the modern self is trained toward entitlement, and I am a fully trained modern self. | generalized | And here is where this session names all of us, the writer first. We do not live as though the world were a gift. We live as though it were owed to us, and we only notice the giving when something is taken away. The whole of the modern self is trained toward entitlement, and we are fully trained modern selves. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[1].teaching` | It is a thing that happens in me before breakfast. I make the same reach for the fruit, the same grasp at being the one who decides what is good for me. I do the same hiding when I have done it, arranging the fig leaves, curating the version of myself that does not have to be forgiven. And I do the same blaming, the instinct older than history to make my wrong somebody else's fault, and if no one is handy, to blame the God who put me here. | generalized | It is a thing that happens in every one of us before breakfast, the writer first. We make the same reach for the fruit, the same grasp at being the one who decides what is good for us. We do the same hiding when we have done it, arranging the fig leaves, curating the version of ourselves that does not have to be forgiven. And we do the same blaming, the instinct older than history to make our wrong somebody else's fault, and if no one is handy, to blame the God who put us here. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[2].teaching` | And this is where I have to stop narrating Israel and admit that I am Israel, because the prophets are not preaching to people far away and long ago, they are preaching to me. I hear the call to come back, and I do not come. I know the drift, the slow turning of my heart toward other gods, the security I trust more than God, the comfort I serve, the self I keep putting on the throne, and I hear the word calling me home and I calculate the cost and I stay where I am, exactly as Israel did, generation after generation, with the prophet standing right there. The Old Testament is a mirror, and the face in it is mine. | generalized | And this is where we have to stop narrating Israel and admit that we are Israel, because the prophets are not preaching to people far away and long ago, they are preaching to us. We hear the call to come back, and we do not come. We know the drift, the slow turning of the heart toward other gods, the security we trust more than God, the comfort we serve, the self we keep putting on the throne, and we hear the word calling us home and we calculate the cost and we stay where we are, exactly as Israel did, generation after generation, with the prophet standing right there. The Old Testament is a mirror, and the face in it is ours. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here is where I stop teaching and put it to you the way it was once put to me, when I was the skeptic in the room and not the pastor at the front. | cut | And here is where I stop teaching and put it to you as plainly as it can be put to any skeptic in the room. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I have to tell you what this does to me, having come to this book from atheism, because I did not expect the Bible to be this coherent. I expected a pile of stories. I found an arc that begins in a garden and ends in a garden, that opens a wound in chapter three and closes the same wound, by name, in the second-to-last chapter, thousands of pages later, a single sustained answer to the single deepest ache of every human heart. | cut | And think what this does to a reader who comes to this book from outside it, a skeptic expecting a pile of stories. What waits for that reader is an arc that begins in a garden and ends in a garden, that opens a wound in chapter three and closes the same wound, by name, in the second-to-last chapter, thousands of pages later, a single sustained answer to the single deepest ache of every human heart. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `leaderPrimer` | Bell leads this from inside it, as a man who came to this book from atheism and did not expect to find a story that held together, and who still reads it more as a character being addressed than as an expert dispensing. | cut | Bell leads this from inside it, as a man who came to this book from atheism and who reads it more as a character being addressed than as an expert dispensing. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `faq[10].a` | The writer does not stand outside the doubts he describes, he held many of them, and he read this book as a skeptic long before he read it as a pastor. | cut | The writer does not stand outside the doubts he describes, because he came to faith from atheism. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `faq[10].a` | When the fourth session presses the good-teacher objection, he is pressing a compromise he once found reasonable. | generalized | When the fourth session presses the good-teacher objection, he is pressing a compromise many thoughtful people find reasonable. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `facilitatorScript.open` | Third, the writer of this study came to it from atheism and read this book as a skeptic long before he read it as a pastor, so we are not standing above these questions. | cut | Third, the writer of this study came to it from atheism, so we are not standing above these questions. |

### the-undivided-life (29)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the-undivided-life | `summary` | He came to faith from atheism and arrived with the convert's certainty that everything had changed, then discovered over slow years he had let almost nothing change in the places no one could see. He has lived in the boxes. He runs hot, confuses output with worth, has refused rest and called it devotion, has served the rival god and built a fortress called security. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he has lived in the boxes. Most of us have, confusing output with worth, refusing rest and calling it devotion, serving the rival god and building a fortress called security. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he came to faith certain everything had changed and then let almost nothing change in the places no one could see. | generalized | Many believers come to faith certain everything has changed and then let almost nothing change in the places no one can see. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[0].teaching` | Bell says he has been that man. He came to faith from atheism, arrived with the convert's certainty that everything had changed, and then discovered over the slow years that he had let almost nothing change in the places no one could see. His theology was total. His life was sorted into boxes. And the boxes did not know about each other. | generalized | Many of us have been that man. A convert can arrive with the certainty that everything has changed and then discover over the slow years that almost nothing has changed in the places no one can see. The theology is total. The life is sorted into boxes. And the boxes do not know about each other. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he still reaches for the scale, still confuses output with worth, still tries to balance what was meant to be surrendered. | generalized | Most of us still reach for the scale, still confuse output with worth, still try to balance what was meant to be surrendered. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[1].discussion[4].behind` | Bell does not claim to have arrived, only to have tasted the altar a few times and found it was not where things die. | cut | Bell does not claim to have arrived, and no one in the room needs to, only to lay one piece on the altar and find it is not where things die. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[1].objections[1].response` | Hear the exhaustion, because Bell shares it and names it plainly. He runs hot. He confuses output with worth and still catches himself reaching for the scale. | generalized | Hear the exhaustion, because it is real and Bell names it plainly. Most of us run hot, confuse output with worth, and still catch ourselves reaching for the scale. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[1].teaching` | Bell says he has felt both. He has spent seasons saving his life, managing it, balancing it, gripping it, and the saving was exhausting and the life leaked away anyway. And he has had hours, too few, where he actually let go of a piece of it onto the altar, a fear, an ambition, a need to be seen, and the strange mercy of the living sacrifice is that he rose from the altar lighter and not poorer. The thing he gave up he had only ever been carrying, never owning. The altar did not take it from him. It revealed he had never possessed it. | generalized | Most of us have felt both. We spend seasons saving our lives, managing them, balancing them, gripping them, and the saving is exhausting and the life leaks away anyway. And there are hours, too few, when we actually let go of a piece of it onto the altar, a fear, an ambition, a need to be seen, and the strange mercy of the living sacrifice is that we rise from the altar lighter and not poorer. The thing given up was only ever being carried, never owned. The altar does not take it from us. It reveals we never possessed it. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[1].teaching` | He is a man who runs hot, who has confused output with worth since before he could name the wound, and he still catches himself reaching for the scale. But every time he has actually laid a piece of his life on the altar instead of trying to balance it, he has found the altar is not where things die. | generalized | Most of us run hot, confuse output with worth, and still catch ourselves reaching for the scale. But every time a piece of a life is actually laid on the altar instead of balanced, the altar turns out not to be where things die. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[2].summary` | A woman who had been married eleven years sat in Bell's office and said she wanted out, and the reason she gave was that she was no longer happy. She was not lying about her own interior weather. She had simply absorbed an account of marriage in which her happiness was the product the marriage existed to deliver, and when it stopped delivering she concluded the machine was broken. The first question is not how to fix a marriage but what marriage even is, because she and the man she married had never agreed. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, a woman eleven years into a marriage who says she wants out, and the reason she gives is that she is no longer happy. She is not lying about her own interior weather. She has simply absorbed an account of marriage in which her happiness is the product the marriage exists to deliver, and when it stops delivering she concludes the machine is broken. The first question is not how to fix a marriage but what marriage even is, because she and the man she married never agreed. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[2].discussion[0].q` | Bell says the woman in his office and her husband had never agreed on what marriage was, one assuming a contract, the other a covenant. | generalized | Bell says couples like the one in this session often never agreed on what marriage was, one assuming a contract, the other a covenant. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[2].teaching` | A woman Bell had married eleven years earlier sat in his office and told him she wanted out, and the reason she gave was that she was no longer happy. She was not lying. She was not even wrong about her own interior weather. She had simply absorbed, the way all of us absorb the air, an account of marriage in which her happiness was the product the marriage existed to deliver, and when it stopped delivering she concluded the machine was broken. Bell says he did not argue with her feelings. He argued with her categories. Because the trouble in that office was not that her marriage had failed. The trouble was that she and the man she married had never agreed on what marriage was. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, a woman eleven years into her marriage who says she wants out, and the reason she gives is that she is no longer happy. She is not lying. She is not even wrong about her own interior weather. She has simply absorbed, the way all of us absorb the air, an account of marriage in which her happiness is the product the marriage exists to deliver, and when it stops delivering she concludes the machine is broken. The wise answer does not argue with her feelings. It argues with her categories. Because the trouble is not that her marriage has failed. The trouble is that she and the man she married never agreed on what marriage was. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[2].teaching` | Bell has been married long enough to have stood on both sides of that vow, kept it on momentum and on gritted teeth and, by grace, been carried by it when he had nothing left to bring. A husband of one wife and a father of five sons learns something the contract can never teach. The promise is not the cage. The promise is the freedom. Because his wife is not lying awake auditioning for her place in his life, and he is not auditioning for his. | cut | Anyone married long enough has stood on both sides of that vow, kept it on momentum and on gritted teeth and, by grace, been carried by it when there was nothing left to bring. A long marriage teaches something the contract never can. The promise is not the cage. The promise is the freedom. Because in a covenant the wife is not lying awake auditioning for her place in her husband's life, and he is not auditioning for his. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[2].teaching` | Bell admits he has been the husband keeping a ledger he had no right to keep, until he remembered the canceled note in his own name. | generalized | Most spouses have kept a ledger they had no right to keep, until they remembered the canceled note in their own name. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[3].discussion[4].behind` | Bell confesses the anxiety that rises in him at an unplanted field, unbelief wearing the costume of responsibility. | generalized | Bell names the anxiety that rises in most of us at an unplanted field, unbelief wearing the costume of responsibility. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[3].teaching` | There is a sentence in the first chapter of the Bible that should have stopped Bell cold and did not, because he came to faith from atheism and read those chapters looking for a fight about origins. | cut | There is a sentence in the first chapter of the Bible that should stop every reader cold and rarely does, because so many read those chapters looking for a fight about origins. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[3].teaching` | Bell watched a pastor he respected run himself into the ground and call it faithfulness, take every meeting, answer every midnight call, skip every vacation, and when his body failed he was genuinely surprised, because he had spent years treating exhaustion as the evidence of his love for God. He was not chasing a private jet. He was chasing the appearance of indispensability, a god the church almost never names because it wears the robes of virtue. Bell watched him with dread because he saw himself. He runs hot. A man raised without a father learns early that he must produce his own worth, that no one will hand him a name, and that wound does not heal because you got saved. | generalized | Picture a familiar figure, a pastor who runs himself into the ground and calls it faithfulness, takes every meeting, answers every midnight call, skips every vacation, and when his body fails he is genuinely surprised, because he has spent years treating exhaustion as the evidence of his love for God. He is not chasing a private jet. He is chasing the appearance of indispensability, a god the church almost never names because it wears the robes of virtue. Most of us can see ourselves in him. Many of us learned early that we must produce our own worth, that no one will hand us a name, and that wound does not heal because we got saved. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[3].teaching` | Bell says he finds this almost unbearable, which tells him how deep the disease runs. The thought of leaving a field unplanted fills him with a low anxiety that is nothing but unbelief wearing the costume of responsibility. The land knew how to rest before he did. | generalized | Most of us find this almost unbearable, which tells us how deep the disease runs. The thought of leaving a field unplanted fills the driven heart with a low anxiety that is nothing but unbelief wearing the costume of responsibility. The land knew how to rest before we did. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].summary` | A man told Bell once, with real relief, that he had finally gotten his finances under control. He had a budget, an emergency fund, a retirement number that let him sleep. And underneath Bell's genuine gladness was an unease, because everything the man described was wise and yet the whole picture had quietly rearranged itself around a center that was not God. He had not bought a foolish thing. He had built a fortress, and the fortress had a name, and the name was security. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, a man who says with real relief that he has finally gotten his finances under control. He has a budget, an emergency fund, a retirement number that lets him sleep. Everything he describes is wise, and yet the whole picture has quietly rearranged itself around a center that is not God. He has not bought a foolish thing. He has built a fortress, and the fortress has a name, and the name is security. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].discussion[0].q` | Bell describes a man who got his finances under control and built a fortress called security, and felt only unease watching it. | generalized | Bell describes a familiar figure, a man who gets his finances under control and builds a fortress called security. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell ends by confessing he has not arrived, that the productivity ruler is still the first one his hand reaches for in the dark, and that he has only tasted shalom, not reached it. | generalized | Bell ends by admitting that no one has arrived, that for most of us the productivity ruler is still the first one the hand reaches for in the dark, and that shalom is tasted here, not reached. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].objections[1].response` | Bell names this danger directly, because the achiever in him reaches for it immediately. | generalized | Bell names this danger directly, because the achiever in all of us reaches for it immediately. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | A man told Bell once, with real relief in his voice, that he had finally gotten his finances under control. He had a budget. He had an emergency fund. He had a retirement plan with a number at the end of it that let him sleep. And as Bell listened he felt an unease underneath his genuine gladness, because everything the man described was wise and he would counsel anyone to do the same, and yet the whole picture had quietly rearranged itself around a center that was not God. He had not bought a single foolish thing. He had simply built a fortress, and the fortress had a name, and the name was security. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, a man who says with real relief in his voice that he has finally gotten his finances under control. He has a budget. He has an emergency fund. He has a retirement plan with a number at the end of it that lets him sleep. Everything he describes is wise, the kind of thing Bell would counsel anyone to do, and yet the whole picture has quietly rearranged itself around a center that is not God. He has not bought a single foolish thing. He has simply built a fortress, and the fortress has a name, and the name is security. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | Bell knows that fortress. He was raised without a father, which among other things means he was raised with a low-grade financial fear that never fully leaves a person. When you grow up watching a household do math at the kitchen table with not enough on the page, money stops being a tool and becomes a god, the thing that decides whether you are safe in the world. So he writes as a man still arguing with his own heart, not as one who has won. | cut | Most of us know that fortress. For anyone who grew up watching a household do math at the kitchen table with not enough on the page, money can stop being a tool and become a god, the thing that decides whether you are safe in the world. So Bell writes as a man still arguing with his own heart, not as one who has won. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | Bell has felt the anesthetic, seasons the account had enough that he stopped praying with any urgency, not because his faith had grown but because his fear had been temporarily medicated by a number. And he has felt it wear off in the night, when the number suddenly looked small against a future he could not control, and understood he had quietly traded the living God for a fortress with walls but no roof, and the rain was coming in. | generalized | Many of us have felt the anesthetic, seasons when the account had enough that we stopped praying with any urgency, not because faith had grown but because fear had been temporarily medicated by a number. And many have felt it wear off in the night, when the number suddenly looked small against a future no one could control, and understood they had quietly traded the living God for a fortress with walls but no roof, and the rain was coming in. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | He is a man who runs hot, who confuses output with worth, who was raised without a father and learned to produce a name for himself before he could spell, and the productivity ruler is still the first one his hand reaches for in the dark. He has lived in the boxes. He has served the rival god. He has refused to rest and called it devotion. | cut | He was raised without a father, and he has lived in the boxes. Most of us have, serving the rival god, refusing to rest and calling it devotion, the productivity ruler still the first one the hand reaches for in the dark. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | But here is what he has seen. The few times he actually offered a piece of his life whole instead of balancing it, the few times he let the wall come down between the rooms, the few sabbaths he kept and the few gifts he gave before he was ready, he tasted shalom. Not arrived at it. Tasted it. | generalized | But here is what can be seen from the road. The few times a piece of a life is actually offered whole instead of balanced, the few times a wall comes down between the rooms, the few sabbaths kept and the few gifts given before anyone felt ready, shalom is tasted. Not arrived at. Tasted. |
| the-undivided-life | `sessions[4].teaching` | And every taste told him the same thing, that this is what he was made for, that the boxes were always a prison he mistook for adulthood, that the integrated life is not a heavier burden but the only rest there is. | generalized | And every taste tells the same thing, that this is what we were made for, that the boxes were always a prison we mistook for adulthood, that the integrated life is not a heavier burden but the only rest there is. |
| the-undivided-life | `leaderPrimer` | Bell came to faith from atheism certain that everything had changed, and then spent slow years discovering how many rooms he had kept dark, the output he mistook for worth, the rest he refused and called devotion, the fortress he built and named security. A pastor, and divided down the middle. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from the dividing, the output mistaken for worth, the rest refused and called devotion, the fortress built and named security. A pastor is no less divided than anyone at the table. |
| the-undivided-life | `faq[9].a` | He came to faith from atheism and let almost nothing change in the places no one could see. He runs hot, confuses output with worth, has refused rest and called it devotion, built a fortress called security out of an old financial fear, kept a ledger in his marriage he had no right to keep. | cut | He came to faith from atheism, and he does not exempt himself from any of the dividing this study names, the output mistaken for worth, the rest refused and called devotion, the fortress called security, the ledger kept where it had no right to be kept. |

### witness (27)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
