| witness | `summary` | Bell knows the dread from the inside, and he knows the other side of it too. He came to faith from atheism. He was, for years, the man being witnessed to, and most of the people who tried got it wrong, treated him as a project to be closed rather than a person to be known. | cut | Bell does not stand outside the dread. He came to faith from atheism, and he takes seriously how evangelism feels from the other side of the table, where many skeptics have been treated as a project to be closed rather than a person to be known. |
| witness | `summary` | He has frozen at the table. He has said nothing when he should have spoken and said too much when he should have listened. | generalized | Most of us have frozen at the table, said nothing when we should have spoken and said too much when we should have listened. |
| witness | `sessions[0].summary` | Bell takes the fear at full strength, because he carries it himself. | generalized | Bell takes the fear at full strength, because it is real and he does not exempt himself from it. |
| witness | `sessions[0].discussion[3].q` | Bell admits that much of his own silence has been plain fear dressed up as principle, and that when he has spoken he has sometimes been the salesman or the arguer himself. | generalized | Much of our silence is plain fear dressed up as principle, and when we have spoken we have sometimes been the salesman or the arguer ourselves. |
| witness | `sessions[0].objections[1].response` | Bell carries this fear and came to Christ from atheism, so he knows both the dread of speaking and the experience of being spoken to badly. | cut | Bell came to Christ from atheism and does not exempt himself from this fear, and he takes seriously both the dread of speaking and the experience of being spoken to badly. |
| witness | `sessions[0].teaching` | I came to faith from atheism, which means for most of my life I was on the other end of this, I was the project, the target, the soul somebody was trying to close, and I will tell you about that in a moment because it matters. But I also know the dread from inside the church, because I have it too. I have frozen at tables. I have let conversations sail right past the moment I should have spoken. I have rehearsed something to say to a neighbor and then said nothing for years. | cut | I came to faith from atheism, so I take seriously what this looks like from the other end, where a person can be made the project, the target, the soul somebody is trying to close, and we will come back to that in a moment because it matters. But the dread lives inside the church too, and I do not exempt myself from it. Most of us have frozen at tables, let conversations sail right past the moment we should have spoken, rehearsed something to say to a neighbor and then said nothing for years. |
| witness | `sessions[0].teaching` | I know this one from the inside, because I was sold to. People who I later realized had befriended me as a strategy, whose interest evaporated the moment it was clear I was not going to pray their prayer on their timeline. I was a target, and I knew it, and it did not make me want their Jesus. It made me trust them less. | generalized | Many skeptics know this one from the inside. They have been befriended as a strategy by people whose interest evaporated the moment it was clear they were not going to pray the prayer on schedule. They were targets, and they knew it, and it did not make them want that Jesus. It made them trust Christians less. |
| witness | `sessions[0].teaching` | There is a place for good writing, I write for a living, but the tract-pusher uses the paper as a substitute for the relationship, a way to discharge the duty without the cost of actually being known by the person, and it can become a way of obeying the letter of evangelism while avoiding its entire heart, which was never a transaction of information but the offering of a life. | generalized | There is a place for good writing, and I write books, but the tract-pusher uses the paper as a substitute for the relationship, a way to discharge the duty without the cost of actually being known by the person, and it can become a way of obeying the letter of evangelism while avoiding its entire heart, which was never a transaction of information but the offering of a life. |
| witness | `sessions[0].teaching` | When I have actually spoken about Jesus, and it has been rarer than it should be, I have been the salesman more than once, steering toward a close. I have hidden behind the equivalent of the tract, the shared article, the invitation to a service, anything to discharge the duty without the exposure of being known. And I have been the argument-winner, oh, I have been that, more interested in the rightness of my point than in the person I was supposedly loving. So the indictment lands on me before it lands on anyone in this room. Much of my own silence has been plain fear wearing the costume of principle, and much of my own speaking has been the very thing I now criticize. | generalized | When we have actually spoken about Jesus, and for most of us it has been rarer than it should be, we have been the salesman more than once, steering toward a close. We have hidden behind the equivalent of the tract, the shared article, the invitation to a service, anything to discharge the duty without the exposure of being known. And we have been the argument-winner, more interested in the rightness of our point than in the person we were supposedly loving. So the indictment lands on the writer before it lands on anyone in this room. Much of our silence has been plain fear wearing the costume of principle, and much of our speaking has been the very thing we now criticize. |
| witness | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has tried to be the closer, to manufacture a verdict that was never his to produce, and to overclaim a certainty he did not have. | generalized | Most of us have tried to be the closer, to manufacture a verdict that was never ours to produce, and to overclaim a certainty we did not have. |
| witness | `sessions[1].objections[0].response` | You should not trust that, and Bell will be the first to say so, because he was you, the skeptic who could feel the agenda under the warmth and watched the interest evaporate the moment the close failed. | cut | You should not trust that, and Bell will be the first to say so, because he came to faith from atheism and takes the skeptic's side of the table seriously, the one who can feel the agenda under the warmth and watches the interest evaporate the moment the close fails. |
| witness | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to put myself inside the indictment here, because I have not been the man born blind. I have been the man who tried to be the closer, who reached for verdicts that were never mine, who felt the conversation slipping and squeezed harder, who claimed more certainty than I actually had because I thought confidence was the same as faithfulness. I have overclaimed. I have manufactured. I have treated the restraint of the honest witness as if it were a weakness to be overcome rather than the very integrity that makes a witness believable. | generalized | And we have to put ourselves inside the indictment here, because most of us have not been the man born blind. We have been the ones who tried to be the closer, who reached for verdicts that were never ours, who felt the conversation slipping and squeezed harder, who claimed more certainty than we actually had because we thought confidence was the same as faithfulness. We have overclaimed. We have manufactured. We have treated the restraint of the honest witness as if it were a weakness to be overcome rather than the very integrity that makes a witness believable. |
| witness | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits his own life and his own household have often not been the kind that makes anyone wonder anything. | generalized | Most of our lives and households are not the kind that makes anyone wonder anything. |
| witness | `sessions[2].teaching` | My own life has too often not been the kind that makes anyone wonder anything. I have handled losses no differently than my unbelieving neighbors handled theirs. My household has not always been a place marked by a love so strange that a guest left puzzled by it. I have hoarded my home and my table out of busyness and self-protection, kept my hospitality for the people who were already family, and wondered, with a kind of dishonesty, why no one ever asks me about my hope. They do not ask because there is nothing to ask about. My life has raised no question, and you cannot answer a question no one has reason to pose. | generalized | Most of our lives have too often not been the kind that makes anyone wonder anything. We handle losses no differently than our unbelieving neighbors handle theirs. Our households are not always places marked by a love so strange that a guest leaves puzzled by it. We hoard our homes and our tables out of busyness and self-protection, keep our hospitality for the people who are already family, and wonder, with a kind of dishonesty, why no one ever asks us about our hope. They do not ask because there is nothing to ask about. Our lives have raised no question, and you cannot answer a question no one has reason to pose. |
| witness | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has told the cleaned-up version of his own story, the dramatic atheist-to-believer arc, while hiding the doubt that is still in it and the cost he is still counting. | cut | Anyone with a dramatic story, the atheist-to-believer arc among them, is tempted to tell the cleaned-up version while hiding the doubt that is still in it and the cost still being counted. |
| witness | `sessions[3].objections[1].response` | Bell came to faith from atheism and will tell you plainly that the doubt did not vanish at conversion, that he has prayed into silence and wrestled with the hardest questions and believes still, not despite the honesty but through it. | cut | Bell came to faith from atheism, and he will tell you plainly that for most believers doubt does not vanish at conversion, and that honest faith believes still, not despite the honesty but through it. |
| witness | `sessions[3].teaching` | And here I have to step into the indictment, because I am as guilty of the formula as anyone, maybe more, because my story fits the dramatic template so neatly that I am tempted to perform it. Atheist to believer, the unbeliever who came to faith, it has a satisfying arc, and I have told the cleaned-up version of it more than once, the impressive before and the resolved after, and hidden the truer thing underneath. Because the truer thing is that the doubt did not vanish when I believed. I have prayed into silence since. I have wrestled with the hardest questions and not always come out with a tidy answer. And the cost did not end at conversion, it began there, and I am still counting it. When I tell the polished version, I get the admiration and I lose the credibility, and worse, I lie a little, by editing out the parts that would let a struggling person see themselves in me. | cut | And here the writer has to step into the indictment, because a story like his, atheist to believer, fits the dramatic template so neatly that it invites performance. It has a satisfying arc, the impressive before and the resolved after, and the temptation with any such story is to tell the cleaned-up version and hide the truer thing underneath. Because the truer thing, for most believers, is that the doubt does not vanish at belief. There are prayers into silence. There are hard questions that do not come out with a tidy answer. And the cost does not end at conversion, it begins there, and it is still being counted. The polished version wins admiration and loses credibility, and worse, it lies a little, by editing out the parts that would let a struggling person see themselves in the witness. |
| witness | `sessions[4].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits he has both pressured people out of his own anxiety and stayed silent out of his own cowardice, and called both of them faithfulness. | generalized | Most of us have both pressured people out of our own anxiety and stayed silent out of our own cowardice, and called both of them faithfulness. |
| witness | `sessions[4].objections[0].response` | Bell grants this completely, because the danger is real and he has lived it, used patience as a cover for cowardice and called the silence faithfulness when it was just fear. | generalized | Bell grants this completely, because the danger is real and most of us have lived it, used patience as a cover for cowardice and called the silence faithfulness when it was just fear. |
| witness | `sessions[4].teaching` | And I grant the danger fully, because I have lived inside it. I have used patience as a cover for fear. I have told myself I was being a patient friend when I was simply being a coward, postponing forever the honest word because the honest word was costly, and calling the avoidance faithfulness. | generalized | And I grant the danger fully, because most of us have lived inside it. We have used patience as a cover for fear. We have told ourselves we were being patient friends when we were simply being cowards, postponing forever the honest word because the honest word was costly, and calling the avoidance faithfulness. |
| witness | `sessions[4].teaching` | And here, at the end, I have to stand inside the indictment one final time, because I have failed in both directions and called both failures faithfulness. I have pressured people, pushed for the close, manufactured urgency, gripped at an outcome that was never mine, out of my own anxiety, and told myself it was zeal. And I have stayed silent, year after year, with people I loved, never finding the honest word, out of plain cowardice, and told myself it was patience. The pressure was anxiety pretending to be faith. The silence was fear pretending to be patience. And the truth I have had to learn slowly is that the way out of both is the same thing, the long table, the patient love that does speak in time and then lets God do the growing. | generalized | And here, at the end, we have to stand inside the indictment one final time, because most of us have failed in both directions and called both failures faithfulness. We have pressured people, pushed for the close, manufactured urgency, gripped at an outcome that was never ours, out of our own anxiety, and told ourselves it was zeal. And we have stayed silent, year after year, with people we loved, never finding the honest word, out of plain cowardice, and told ourselves it was patience. The pressure was anxiety pretending to be faith. The silence was fear pretending to be patience. And the truth most of us learn slowly is that the way out of both is the same thing, the long table, the patient love that does speak in time and then lets God do the growing. |
| witness | `leaderPrimer` | Bell spent years on the other side of the table, the atheist being witnessed to, treated as a project by people keeping score, and he has also been the Christian who froze and said nothing at the moment that asked for one sentence. ### womanhood (18)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| womanhood | `summary` | It is written by a man, a pastor, a husband, the father of five sons, who has to confess at the outset that men built much of the church for men, that the sermons he grew up on quoted Paul's hard sentences to women and skipped the women Paul honored by name, that he himself has taught rooms as if half of them were furniture. | cut | It is written by a man, a pastor, the father of five sons, who has to confess at the outset that men built much of the church for men, that its sermons have often quoted Paul's hard sentences to women and skipped the women Paul honored by name, that men in pulpits have taught rooms as if half of them were furniture. |
| womanhood | `sessions[0].summary` | A woman told Bell, after a lifetime in church, that she had learned to think of herself as a supporting character in someone else's story, useful to a husband, a family, a ministry, and worth exactly what she produced for them. She was not bitter. She was reporting what she had been taught, and much of it she had been taught in a sanctuary. | generalized | Many women, after a lifetime in church, have learned to think of themselves as supporting characters in someone else's story, useful to a husband, a family, a ministry, and worth exactly what they produce for them. They are not bitter. They are reporting what they were taught, and much of it they were taught in a sanctuary. |
| womanhood | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that men, himself included, have built much of the church as if it were for men, and taught rooms as though half of them were furniture. | generalized | Bell says that men have built much of the church as if it were for men, and taught rooms as though half of them were furniture. |
| womanhood | `sessions[0].teaching` | A woman told Bell, near the end of a lifetime spent inside the church, that she had come to think of herself as a supporting character in somebody else's story. Useful to a husband. Useful to her children. Useful to a ministry that needed hands. And worth, she said, about what she produced for them in a given week. She was not angry when she said it. That was the part that stopped him. She was calmly reporting a lesson she had learned, and she had learned a good deal of it in a sanctuary, from people who loved her and would have been horrified to hear it named. | generalized | Start with a familiar figure, a woman near the end of a lifetime spent inside the church who has come to think of herself as a supporting character in somebody else's story. Useful to a husband. Useful to her children. Useful to a ministry that needed hands. And worth, she would say, about what she produced for them in a given week. She is not angry when she says it. That is the part that should stop us. She is calmly reporting a lesson she has learned, and she learned a good deal of it in a sanctuary, from people who loved her and would have been horrified to hear it named. |
| womanhood | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this before I say another word to anyone, because men built much of the church I serve in, and we built it for men. I grew up on sermons that could quote Paul's two hard sentences to women from memory and had never once mentioned Deborah or Huldah or Junia or Phoebe. I have stood in front of rooms and taught as though half the people in them were furniture, present but not addressed, there to support the real work rather than to do it. | cut | And I have to stand inside this before I say another word to anyone, because men built much of the church, and we built it for men. Many of us grew up on sermons that could quote Paul's two hard sentences to women from memory and had never once mentioned Deborah or Huldah or Junia or Phoebe. Men in pulpits, and I do not exempt myself, have stood in front of rooms and taught as though half the people in them were furniture, present but not addressed, there to support the real work rather than to do it. |
| womanhood | `sessions[1].summary` | Bell says he could recite Paul's two hard sentences to women before he was ten, and did not learn until he was a grown pastor that Paul had also greeted a woman named Junia and called her, in the same letter, notable among the apostles. The gap was not in the Bible. It was in what he had been handed. | cut | Many Christians could recite Paul's two hard sentences to women as children and never learned that Paul had also greeted a woman named Junia and called her, in the same letter, notable among the apostles. The gap was not in the Bible. It was in what they had been handed. |
| womanhood | `sessions[1].discussion[0].q` | Bell says he could quote Paul's hard sentences to women years before he ever heard the name Junia. | generalized | Many of us could quote Paul's hard sentences to women years before we ever heard the name Junia. |
| womanhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | Bell says he could recite Paul's two hard sentences about women, the ones from First Timothy and First Corinthians, from memory before he was ten years old. He did not learn until he was a grown man, ordained, standing in pulpits, that the same apostle who wrote those sentences also ended his letter to the Romans by greeting a woman named Junia and calling her, in the same breath, notable among the apostles. | cut | Many Christians could recite Paul's two hard sentences about women, the ones from First Timothy and First Corinthians, from memory as children. Many do not learn until they are grown, some not until they are ordained and standing in pulpits, that the same apostle who wrote those sentences also ended his letter to the Romans by greeting a woman named Junia and calling her, in the same breath, notable among the apostles. |
| womanhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | The gap was in what he had been handed, in the reading list he had been given, in the verses that got underlined for him and the ones that got quietly turned past. | generalized | The gap was in what they had been handed, in the reading list they had been given, in the verses that got underlined for them and the ones that got quietly turned past. |
| womanhood | `sessions[1].teaching` | I know, because I was that boy, and then I was the man in the pulpit doing it again, and it took me far too long to read the pages I had been taught to skip. | generalized | Many of us were that child, and some of us grew up to stand in the pulpit and do it again, slow to read the pages we had been taught to skip. |

### work-and-vocation (15)

| Guide | Field path | Original (verbatim) | Action | New text |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| work-and-vocation | `summary` | He is a pastor, and the pastorate is a job, a job he has loved into an idol, a job he has hidden behind, a job whose busyness he has called faithfulness when it was really fear. He has made the work of God a way of avoiding God. | generalized | He is a pastor, and the pastorate is a job, a job that can be loved into an idol, hidden behind, its busyness called faithfulness when it is really fear, the work of God made a way of avoiding God. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[0].summary` | A man told Bell, after a long week, that he could not wait for heaven so he would finally never have to work again. It was said as a joke, and it was not a joke. | generalized | Many people, after a long week, joke that they cannot wait for heaven so they will finally never have to work again. It is said as a joke, and it is not a joke. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[0].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses he has treated his own work as the thing standing between him and the life he really wanted. | generalized | Many of us treat our work as the thing standing between us and the life we really want. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[0].teaching` | Start where the trouble starts, with a man who said something he did not know he believed. After a long week he told me, half laughing, that he could not wait for heaven so that he would finally never have to work again. We both laughed. And then I could not stop thinking about it, because underneath the joke sat a conviction that almost every Christian I know carries without ever once examining it. | generalized | Start where the trouble starts, with a familiar line, the one people say after a long week, half laughing, that they cannot wait for heaven so that they will finally never have to work again. Everyone laughs. But underneath the joke sits a conviction that almost every Christian carries without ever once examining it. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[0].teaching` | And I have to stand inside this, because I have done it for years. I have treated my own work, even the work of ministry, as the thing standing between me and the life I actually wanted, living for the day off and the study leave and the eventual rest, as though God were waiting for me somewhere on the far side of my labor instead of present in the middle of it. The man who joked about never working again was only saying out loud what I had been believing in silence. | generalized | And we have to stand inside this, because most of us have done it for years. We treat our own work, even the work of ministry, as the thing standing between us and the life we actually wanted, living for the day off and the vacation and the eventual rest, as though God were waiting for us somewhere on the far side of our labor instead of present in the middle of it. The joke about never working again only says out loud what many of us believe in silence. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[0].caseStudy` | A woman in the congregation, raising three children at home, told Bell she had stopped saying what she did when people asked, because the answer just a mom always landed like an apology. She had sat through years of sermons celebrating people who left their careers for the mission field and felt, every time, that her own days of laundry and meals and the slow shaping of small souls were the part of life God was waiting out. | generalized | Picture a woman raising three children at home who has stopped saying what she does when people ask, because the answer just a mom always lands like an apology. She has sat through years of sermons celebrating people who left their careers for the mission field and felt, every time, that her own days of laundry and meals and the slow shaping of small souls were the part of life God was waiting out. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[1].discussion[4].q` | Bell admits there are mornings he has resented even the work he was called to, ministry itself, and has had to drag himself to it like a man pulling thorns. | generalized | Most of us have mornings when we resent even the work we were called to, and drag ourselves to it like someone pulling thorns. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[1].teaching` | And I have to confess that I live in the thorns as much as anyone, and that I have resented even the work I was most sure God called me to. There are mornings I have dragged myself to the work of ministry like a man pulling weeds, mornings I have stood to preach with no fire in me, weeks where the calling felt like sweat and nothing else. I have hidden that, because pastors are supposed to be above it, which is its own kind of lie. So let me say it plainly, so you can say it too. | generalized | And no one lives outside the thorns, pastors included, and most of us have resented even the work we were most sure God called us to. There are mornings when even called work feels like pulling weeds, when a preacher stands to preach with no fire, weeks when the calling feels like sweat and nothing else. Pastors tend to hide that, because they are supposed to be above it, which is its own kind of lie. So let it be said plainly, so you can say it too. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[2].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that as a pastor he has secretly believed his work counted more to God, that the collar made his labor holier than the labor of the people in his church. | generalized | Pastors are tempted to believe secretly that their work counts more to God, that the collar makes their labor holier than the labor of the people in their churches. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[2].teaching` | And I will confess to you that I have not always believed that in my bones. I have stood up here and quietly felt that what I do, the preaching and the praying and the counseling, counts to God in a way that the labor of the woman who cleans this building after we leave does not. | generalized | And pastors are tempted not to believe that in their bones. It is easy to stand up front and quietly feel that the preaching and the praying and the counseling count to God in a way that the labor of the one who cleans the building after everyone leaves does not. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[3].discussion[4].q` | Bell confesses that he has worked himself to exhaustion for the church and called it faithfulness, when it was really that he could not bear to find out whether the work needed him. | generalized | Pastors and church workers can work themselves to exhaustion for the church and call it faithfulness, when really they cannot bear to find out whether the work needs them. |
| work-and-vocation | `sessions[3].teaching` | And I have to tell you that I am the chief of sinners here, and that I have hidden my idolatry under the holiest disguise available, which is the work of God itself. I have worked myself to exhaustion for the church and called it faithfulness. I have skipped the rest, answered the email at midnight, carried the whole congregation in my chest like a stone I could not set down, and told myself it was devotion. It was not, or not only. Underneath, the truth was uglier and simpler. I could not bear to find out whether the work needed me. I needed to be needed. I needed the church to depend on me, because if it ran fine without me, then I would have to face the question of who I am when I am not producing for God, and that question terrified me. | generalized | And pastors are the chief of sinners here, because they can hide their idolatry under the holiest disguise available, which is the work of God itself. It is easy to work yourself to exhaustion for the church and call it faithfulness, to skip the rest, answer the email at midnight, carry the whole congregation in your chest like a stone you cannot set down, and tell yourself it is devotion. It is not, or not only. Underneath, the truth is uglier and simpler. The worker cannot bear to find out whether the work needs him. He needs to be needed. He needs the church to depend on him, because if it runs fine without him, then he has to face the question of who he is when he is not producing for God, and that question terrifies him. |
| work-and-vocation | `leaderPrimer` | Bell knows the idol from inside the pastorate, a job he has loved into a god, hidden behind, worked at in order to avoid the God it was supposedly for. | generalized | Bell writes about the idol from inside the pastorate, a job easily loved into a god, hidden behind, worked at in order to avoid the God it is supposedly for. |
| work-and-vocation | `faq[3].a` | Bell confesses that he has worked himself to exhaustion for the church and called it faithfulness, that he has hidden inside his busyness, that he has secretly believed his collar made his labor count more to God than the labor of the people he serves. | generalized | Bell names how a pastor can work himself to exhaustion for the church and call it faithfulness, hide inside his busyness, and secretly believe his collar makes his labor count more to God than the labor of the people he serves. |
| work-and-vocation | `facilitatorScript.open` | He is a pastor who has made an idol of his own work and hidden behind his busyness, and I have my own version of that. | generalized | He is a pastor who does not exempt himself from making an idol of work and hiding behind busyness, and I have my own version of that. |

The added biography (praying by a bed newly out of atheism, praying the desperate psalms over his sons in the dark, rage at an absent father buried for years, having no one to pray to) was cut or generalized. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `sessions[0].teaching / faq[10].a / sessions[1].summary / sessions[1].discussion[4].q / sessions[0].discussion[4].behind` | I once held it myself [the materialist account] / he is granting a view he once held / It happens in the writer every day, the same reach for the fruit ... / the native posture of the modern heart, the writer's included, is entitlement | Holding the materialist view restates the allowed fact of his atheism (kept for consistency with skeptic and spiritual-warfare); the other lines are short theological self-implication with no event. Cut: reading Scripture as a young skeptic, the question once put to him as the skeptic in the room, not expecting the Bible to cohere, reading it as a skeptic long before reading it as a pastor, finding the good-teacher compromise reasonable, and why he held materialism. |
| the-story-of-the-bible | `promoKit.bulletinBlurb / promoKit.inviteCaption / promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement / faq[3].a` | a man who came to this book from atheism / reads this book as one still being surprised by it / who came to faith from atheism himself | Allowed fact, plus a stance line. |
| the-undivided-life | `summary / sessions[0].teaching / sessions[3].teaching / sessions[4].teaching / faq[0].a / sessions[4].discussion[4].behind` | he has lived in the boxes / Bell says he has flinched. So have you. / Bell says it about himself before he says it about you ... The world rests on God's shoulders, not mine ... I believe it rests on me. / So Bell writes as a man still arguing with his own heart / He has not arrived at this ... a map drawn by a man still walking it / a man who admits he is still divided himself | Short universal self-implication and stance lines; they narrate no event. Cut or generalized: the convert's certainty followed by slow years of change, runs hot, output confused with worth since before he could name the wound, financial fear from being raised without a father, the kitchen-table childhood, the anesthetic of the account number, a woman Bell had married eleven years earlier in his office, his own marriage and the ledger in it, a pastor he watched burn out, a man who told Bell about his finances, tasting shalom. |
| the-undivided-life | `facilitatorScript.open / promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | I have lived in the boxes. I have reached for the wrong rulers in the dark. / It implicates the preacher first, because I have lived in the boxes too. | Scripted lines spoken by the local facilitator or announcer, not by Bell. |
| the-undivided-life | `summary / promoKit.bulletinBlurb` | drawn from James Bell's book The Undivided Life | Verified: The Undivided Life is listed in client/public/catalogue/index.json and has a cover at client/public/books/the-undivided-life.svg. |
| witness | `summary / sessions[1].summary / sessions[1].teaching / sessions[0].teaching / sessions[2].teaching / sessions[3].teaching` | He is one of them. / he takes it from the inside, because he was the skeptic / I take it from the inside, because I was the skeptic / This one tempts the smart and the well-read most, and it has tempted me. / I cannot let us do that, because I cannot do it myself. / this session indicts me as much as any, maybe more / So let me take the honest person's resistance at full strength, because I share it. | Restatements of the allowed fact (he was an atheist) and short stance lines; no event narrated. Cut or generalized: being witnessed to for years, people who befriended him as a strategy, freezing at tables, a neighbor he rehearsed words for, his household and table, telling the cleaned-up version of his conversion, praying into silence since conversion, the cost he is still counting, patience used as cover, pressuring people out of anxiety. 'I write for a living' became 'I write books' (he is an author and a pastor). |
| witness | `facilitatorScript.open / promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | I have frozen at tables. I have stayed silent for years out of plain fear ... / It implicates the preacher first, because I have frozen at tables ... | Scripted lines spoken by the local facilitator or announcer, not by Bell. |
| womanhood | `sessions[3].teaching / sessions[3].summary / faq[1].a / faq[3].a / faq[11].a` | I land as a complementarian, but a chastened one ... / the author lands as what he calls a chastened complementarian | A doctrinal position, not an anecdote, and CLAUDE.md requires naming where the author lands on a contested question. I cannot verify that this is James's actual position; flagged for him to confirm. Narrated support for it ('I have watched this position abused', 'most of the ones I know') was generalized. |
| womanhood | `summary / promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement / faq[8].a / sessions[3].teaching` | He writes from inside that failure, not above it. / The writer, a man, leads it from inside his own blindness, not above it. / the thing that costs me something to write | Stance lines. Cut or generalized: 'a husband' (marital status), the sermons he grew up on, reciting Paul's verses before he was ten, learning of Junia only when grown and ordained, teaching rooms as if half were furniture, a woman who told Bell she was a supporting character, watching a woman's face close at Proverbs 31, seeing the reframe wound mothers. |
| work-and-vocation | `summary / sessions[2].teaching / sessions[3].teaching / sessions[2].discussion[4].behind` | Bell does not stand outside this. / I am the professional Christian here, the man with the collar, the one this lie flatters most. / The moment I think my work matters more to God because it happens in a church, I have rejoined the very lie ... / The Sabbath command stands over me exactly as it stands over the banker who lives at his desk. | Stance lines, a true fact (he is a pastor), and conditional or theological self-application; no event narrated. Cut or generalized: a man who told Bell he could not wait for heaven, a woman in the congregation who told Bell she was just a mom, living for study leave, dragging himself to ministry, preaching with no fire, feeling his work counted more than the woman who cleans the building, working himself to exhaustion and needing to be needed. |
| work-and-vocation | `promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement / facilitatorScript.open` | It implicates the preacher first, because I have made an idol of my own work more than once. / I have my own version of that. | Scripted lines spoken by the local announcer or facilitator, not by Bell. |
| doubt | `summary / faq[1].a / leaderPrimer` | he remembers the inside of the question, not just the answer to it / he lived in it and remembers it as a real and reasonable place / he knows the objections from the inside, not as a cartoon but as a reasonable country | Restatements of the allowed fact (he was an atheist) as a vantage point; no event, date, or account of how or why he converted. James may prefer 'knows' to 'remembers'. |
| believe | `summary / sessions[0].teaching / sessions[0].discussion[4].q / faq[9].a / facilitatorScript.open` | he has sat on the other side of the table and meant his objections / I have sat on the other side of this table and meant my objections / he once meant the objections he now answers | Metaphor for the allowed fact (he was an atheist); no scene or conversation narrated. |
| (all guides) | `facilitatorScript.open / promoKit.pulpitAnnouncement` | e.g. grief: I have stood at gravesides and said the right words and driven home undone. / lament: I have stood at gravesides with nothing true to say. / prayer: I have prayed into a ceiling. / witness: I have frozen at tables. | About 20 guides script first-person confessions for the local facilitator and the pulpit announcer. They are not claims about Bell and were left as written, but several echo Bell stories that were cut elsewhere, and if James reads a pulpit announcement himself they become his. Worth a read. |

## Changed sentences that also appear elsewhere in the repo

82 of the original sentences changed above also appear word for word outside the study guides, mostly in the books and essays under `content/archive/free-library/`, the article data in `api/_data/` and `client/src/data/`, and `client/src/data/social-quotes.ts`. Those files were outside this pass and still carry the same stories. If a story is James's own and true, these are the places it already lives; if it is not, these are the places it still needs to come out. The sentence start is enough to find it.
